• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-references to annexes, e.g. project description, methodology, assumptions, etc.
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes are consistent with the details provided in the funding proposal (updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents).
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Proposals exceeding this prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
• The maximum font size is Arial, size 11.
• Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the proposal being subject to review of any information that has not been disclosed pursuant to the IDP. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

The Royal Government of Cambodia, acting through the Ministry of Economy and Finance, the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology, and the National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development Secretariat will be the Executing Entity.

In 1996, the ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF) was established by law. Its organization and functioning is defined by a sub-decree of the Royal Government of Cambodia. The minister responsible for the MEF is selected by the prime minister from among members of the National Assembly. The minister is appointed along with the Council of Ministers by the king, who also appoints the deputy ministers and secretaries-general. The MEF is responsible for formulating policies that contribute to the government's economic and financial policy. Included in the new responsibilities are the development and implementation of a uniform financial system, preparation and implementation of the national budget, debt management and redemption of all national revenues, inspection of public finance, and monitoring of the implementation of national policies.

## A.20 Executing Entity information

The Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM) will be the main executing entity for CASASAR. Established in 1998, MOWRAM is a key government body in Cambodia, led by a minister. Its responsibilities include developing and implementing policies on water resources management and hydrology data; identifying innovative techniques for water use and treatment; and providing technical assistance to provinces for the development of water resources and meteorology. The Ministry comprises ten departments and one secretariat: the Provincial Departments of Water Resources and Meteorology (PDWRMs) in each of Cambodia's 24 provinces for ground implementation.

The National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development Secretariat (NCSSDS) was established to implement decentralization reform policies, local governance, and capacity development. It will also be an Executing Entity for the project. The NCSSDS is responsible for providing technical assistance in financing to local governments and will play a crucial role in the implementation of Component 2 and Sub-component C. in close collaboration with provincial departments and service providers.

## A.21 Executive summary

### Climate change problem
Cambodia's agriculture sector is crucial for the economy but also highly susceptible to the effects of climate change. The country's vulnerability index is ranked 148 out of 167 countries in the 2022 ND-GAIN Index, was the 12th most disaster-prone country among 172 countries between 1999-2016, and remains one of the five least developed countries (LDCs) in Southeast Asia. The Royal Government of Cambodia faces high risk of disaster as a result of extreme natural events, with an exposure index of 27% and a vulnerability index of 35%. These risks are exacerbated by climate change, leading to increased temperatures, increasing exposure to floods, storms, and droughts. The Tonle Sap Lake Basin, often referred to as the rice basket of Cambodia, undergoes cycles of severe droughts and floods. This has resulted in lower

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

## The impact of climate change on Cambodia's agricultural productivity and reduced cultivation for smallholder farmers. Similar to other monsoon-dependent countries, climate change is altering Cambodia's monsoon calendar, increasing total drought days and shifting the start of the rainy season. While annual rainfall is expected to increase, the number of dry spells will also rise, leading to more frequent water stress in agriculture and flooding risks. Furthermore, identified hazards are as high across the whole country for floods, droughts, and heatwaves. The frequency of extreme weather events such as heavy precipitation days in the rainy season, and an increase in the number of consecutive dry days. The mean temperature is projected to increase by 1.3°C to 2.09°C under RCP4.5 (moderate) and RCP8.5 (high) scenarios.

### The impact on Cambodia's economy

The agricultural sector accounts for approximately 27% of the country’s GDP, with agriculture contributing about 60% of the total employment in the country as of the GPD in 2022. The rural population represents 75.8% of the national population in Cambodia. Since 2020, the negative impacts of climate change and the pandemic have led to a 10% loss in Gross Domestic Product (GDP). In the agricultural sector, the main challenges are limited access to credit for smallholder farmers, high labour costs and the limited scope for cropland expansion in the country pose challenges for the agricultural sector. The lack of knowledge on climate change adaptation practices and technologies leads to food shortages for vulnerable communities. Seven of Cambodia's 25 provinces (including Phnom Penh) are classified as highly vulnerable to climate change, with a high proportion of smallholder farmers who rely heavily on rain-fed agriculture and non-poor households are vulnerable to falling back into poverty in the event of a natural disaster or other shocks.

### The lack of irrigation water is a crucial barrier for farmers

The projected increase in temperature-induced reduced water shortages, which prevent the cultivation of more than a single crop each year. Studies suggest that shifting to irrigated cultivation could result in increased yields and higher profits per hectare. However, smallholder farmers are often unaware or unable to reduce the greenhouse gas emissions from rice cultivation. The lack of timely information on droughts and floods can lead to poor decision-making by farmers. Smallholder farmers also tend to be less aware of climate change adaptation practices such as improved planting materials, efficient input management, or the benefits of applying these practices.

### Cambodia's progress towards achieving its national development goals

Cambodia is unlikely to achieve 5% of its annual agricultural growth target by 2030 without more investment in irrigation infrastructure and climate-resilient agriculture. The lack of access to credit for smallholder farmers, limited access to finance, and insufficient institutional capacities have left the rural agricultural population vulnerable to negative impacts of climate change, resulting economic slowdowns and environmental degradation.

## Proposed Interventions

### The Cambodia Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CAISAR) project

The Cambodia Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CAISAR) project originated from the RUG’s National Water Resources Management and Sustainable Irrigation Road Map and was developed in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF), and Meteorology (MOWRAM) with its strategic plan to modernize the irrigation sector. The overall goal of the CAISAR project is to improve the resilience of Cambodia’s agricultural sector by 2030.

The specific objective of the project is to modernize the irrigation sector by installing resilient irrigation systems and enable farmers to adapt to climate risks and mitigate crop emissions through use of energy-efficient technologies, improved water management practices, and better access to credit. The CAISAR project will be implemented at three levels: national level, provincial level, district level, irrigation scheme level and at the national level by creating a strong institutional base and an enabling environment.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

#### v. Objectives of system modernization and improvement of farmer resilience will be achieved by implementing three components which intend to address climate change vulnerabilities, reduce GHG emissions, increase agriculture productivity, and develop institutional capacities: Component 1: Farm-level climate-resilient agricultural systems development through the adoption of climate-smart practices on select value chains, including rice, vegetable, poultry and aquaculture, providing opportunities for greater income generation. This will be achieved by promoting the use of improved seeds, increasing access to agro-meteorological information and services; Component 2: Upgrading and climate-proofing water infrastructure which includes the construction of small-scale irrigation systems, rehabilitation and drainage improvements, and the establishment and training of Farmers Water User Communities (FWUC) and Component 3: Institutional strengthening of government institutions, principally MOIWARM. This will be achieved by providing technical assistance to MOIWARM in areas such as climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction, as well as the FWUCs. While the focus for MOIWARM will be on upgrading technical capacity in various aspects of climate-smart irrigation design and management, river basin management and water accounting.

#### Climate results/benefits:

vi. The Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CAISAR) project will be focusing on climate change adaptation with cross-cutting benefits through the reduction of GHG emissions from agriculture and increased agricultural productivity. The project will focus primarily on rice, the latter is underpinned by extension and advisory to farmers on intermittent flooding (e.g., AWD), early warning systems for floods and droughts, and improved water management practices. It is expected that the CAISAR project will benefit 562,757 rural people directly by making smallholders more resilient to climate change through increased agricultural productivity and reduced GHG emissions. The benefits of the project are expected to be generated from investments in enhanced smallerholder adaptation, market integration and improved water management practices. These include: (i) reduction in GHG emissions due to changes in the cropping pattern; additional income generation in rice production, second crop production through use of solar pumps; (ii) increase in income due to improved yields and production in crops losses; (iii) improved health and well-being due to increased incomes of food consumption; (iv) increased commercialization of agricultural products leading to better livelihoods and (v) the active participation of the private sector in the water and agriculture economy. The project is expected to generate significant irrigation benefits as a result of reducing emissions from agriculture by 1,006,507.43 tCO2-e over 20 years.

# B. Project Programme Information

## Country and Agricultural Context

### Climate context

Climate change is highly sensitive to climate change and disaster risks. It was ranked 149 out of 187 countries in the 2022 ND-GAIN Index, was the 12th most disaster-prone country among 172 countries for 1999–2018 and the 3rd most vulnerable country to climate change. The total economic losses from natural disasters in the country were estimated at 0.7% of GDP in 2011. The World Risk Index of 15.6% is above the global average of 14%. The country has a high vulnerability index (VI) of 83%, and a risk of 27% with a very high exposure of 99%. Approximately 80% of the country is within the Mekong River and Tone Sap basins, increasing exposure to floods, storms, and droughts. Identified hazards are assessed as high vulnerability in terms of population and economic assets. The country has a high proportion (45%) of the proportion of the population affected. Cambodia is one of the world's most flood-exposed countries in the world with 10% of its land area being flooded annually, and it is also highly vulnerable to droughts. As an extreme river flow occurs: A WB study estimates the increase of population exposed to floods by 2500 under the RCP8.5 scenario. The country has experienced a significant increase in flood events since 1970 as Cambodia experiences over USD 250 million in average annual losses due to damage caused by floods (out of which USD 34 million is due to crop loss). This is expected to become more severe floods and droughts, which is expected to affect Cambodia's GDP by nearly 10% by 2070.

### Agriculture

Agriculture is the primary source of livelihood for the communities living in most provinces of Cambodia. It contributes about 35% of the country’s GDP and employs over 80% of the population. By mid-century, climate change will result in more frequent heavy precipitation days, from 2–4 days to 6-9 days in a year. The average annual rainfall is projected to increase by 17%. The number of dry days per year could decrease from 35 to 4-4 days. The average monthly temperature is projected to increase by 1.83°C by 2050 under RCP8.5 scenario. This will lead to a reduction in the growing season, which is currently about 6 months and will reduce to 4–4 days. The average monthly temperature is projected to increase by 1.83°C by 2050 under RCP8.5 scenario. This will lead to a reduction in the growing season, which is currently about 6 months and will reduce to 4-4 days. The average monthly temperature is projected to increase by 1.83°C by 2050 under RCP8.5 scenario. This will lead to a reduction in the growing season, which is currently about 6 months and will reduce to 4–4 days.

### Farming

Farming remains mostly subsistence-based and rain-fed, with low productivity. Only 7 – 8% of the total potential agricultural land area is under full irrigation in Cambodia. Further, only 15% of the cultivated rice area is under partial or full irrigation. The country’s rice production system is highly vulnerable to climate change-induced water shortages, and to cultivate more than a single crop per year - limiting their resilience to shocks and stresses. Hence, farmers are engaged in some autonomous adaptation actions but also

these effects on the cost of yields and economic returns. For example, because wet season rice depends on irrigation water, farmers cultivate mature medium (120–150 days) or late maturity (more than 150 days) rice. Medium-early rice varieties are more vulnerable to drought stress and can be affected by potential damage from shortages in late November, since they are harvested in early November. Late-maturing varieties, which have a better resistance to flood, are chosen by farmers in areas that normally experience flooding during the wet season. These varieties are also more suitable for household consumption (with the exception that yield will be lower than dry season rice).

4. **Supporting agricultural transformation and adaptation:** Farmers in Cambodia are largely unaware of climate resilient practices (e.g., alternate wetting and drying, direct seed seeding and land leveling), stress-tolerant seed varieties and improved planting materials, efficient input management, and other technologies that can help them adapt to the changing climate. A recent survey conducted by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries in 2015–2016 revealed that only 26% of farmers have received agricultural training[3]. Other significant barriers for smallholder farmers include lack of access to credit (78%), lack of access to markets (49%), and lack of access to finance, and insufficient institutional capacities have left the rural agrarian population vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change.

5. In Cambodia, GHG emissions are dominated by the Forest and Other Land Use (FOLU) sector (60.5%) and agriculture (16.5%), followed by energy (12.1%), Industrial Processes and Product Use (IPPU) (7.9%) and waste (3%). The FOLU sector is dominated by deforestation and forest degradation (43.5%), followed by energy (31.0%), waste (5.5%), and IPPU (3.3%). Under a business-as-usual (BAU) scenario, the country’s GHG emissions are projected to increase from 287 MtCO2e in 2019 to 460 MtCO2e by 2030. Agriculture is a significant source of methane (from rice paddies) and nitrous oxide (from fertilizer use). Limited adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices reduces emission and, in the absence of the project intervention, emissions from agriculture are projected to increase under BAU scenario compared based on regional studies[4]. Farmers also have poor fertilizer management: the amount of fertilizer applied is not adjusted according to crop needs. This results in a significant loss of nutrients and contributes to GHG emission intensity of rice—driven by the need for better growth in a shorter growing period and to ensure return on other agri-input investments. The use of organic fertilizers (livestock manure) occurs to a limited extent, but is not widespread due to lack of access to livestock and poor management practices. Soil erosion remains a challenge. Deforestation and land use change, particularly for agriculture, logging, and urban expansion, are also significant sources of GHG emissions in Cambodia. The country’s forest cover has declined from 50% in the early 1960s to less than 20%, with forests concentrated in protected areas and wetlands and reducing the country's capacity to absorb CO2. Weak enforcement of land-use regulations and illegal logging exacerbate this problem[5]. There is low participation in reforestation initiatives, such as energy conservation projects, which could help reduce deforestation.

Climate change affects women, men, and vulnerable people differently, and their anticipatory actions and responses to its impacts also differ, including in relation to safeguarding food security and livelihoods and coping with hazards and risks. Women and children are among the most vulnerable groups to climate change. In the project area, women are responsible for household decision-making on land use and agricultural production, extension information and adaptive agricultural technologies, education (low literacy levels), and other resources. They also have limited access to credit and markets, and their participation in income-generating activities is low. Women’s lack of participation in agriculture than men. Cambodia's Resilience Index found women less resilient in times of disaster than men[6]. The project area has a high prevalence of poverty (40%), inadequate education levels, inadequate climate information and early warning systems, poor housing, weak social safety nets and absence

## D

### of emergency management. Furthermore, women's responsibilities in managing household resources positions them well to contribute to livelihood strategies adapted to changing environmental realities. However, lack of information access to planning processes, and domestic and care obligations limit their economic empowerment and ability to participate.

#### The CAISAR target provinces are Kampong Speu, Kampong Chhnang, Kandal, and Pursat. These provinces[1] have the highest population density, high total percentage of poor households, and a high percentage of people living in poverty. Most of the economically active population is young, and vulnerable people in the region depend mainly on agricultural production and water availability through precipitation, river flow, and irrigation schemes.

#### Figure 1: Map of Project Area

### The project area was already under stress prior to the COVID-19 pandemic with small-scale farmers and larger food producers struggling to produce food and access markets. Food production faces additional pressure from climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of droughts, floods during the rainfall season. Rural communities highlight major water problems due to aging infrastructure and reduced water availability in rivers and ponds. The proposed underlying investment project will support the government's efforts for food security and rural livelihoods through the restoration and enhancement of climate-resilient irrigation systems.

### Since 2020, the negative impacts of climate change and the pandemic have led to a 10% loss in GDP[2] in addition to negative climate impacts, stagnating global agricultural commodity prices, rising labor costs and the limited availability of credit for small-scale farmers. The high cost of production and distribution of agricultural products is high compared to neighboring countries, largely due to high energy and transport costs. Private sector investment in waste-saving or energy-efficient technologies remains low, and their engagement in extension services remains low due to an unfavorable business environment.

# Climate Change Impacts and Vulnerability of the Agriculture Sector

## The Tonle Sap Lake Basin

### Droughts and Floods

The Tonle Sap Lake Basin is often referred to as the rice bowl of Cambodia, undergoing cycles of severe droughts and floods that have resulted in lower agricultural productivity and reduced cultivation for smallholder farmers. Similar to other monsoon-dependent countries, climate change is altering Cambodia's monsoon season. The frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall are increasing, with more frequent heavy rainfall expected; the number of days with rain is decreasing and daily rain intensity is increasing. This has led to increased flooding in the Tonle Sap Lake Basin, particularly in the upper and lower Toulle Sap watershed as extremely high water rates. Annual variability in rainfall significantly affects Cambodia's agricultural productivity. The average annual rainfall over the project areas, about 1500 mm of which approximately 25% falls in the project areas, will be submerged during the wet season, reaching critical thresholds for irrigation and ecosystem stability.

### Droughts

On average, the length of the dry season is caused by floods and droughts is approximately 120.501 hectares, of which 70 percent of the damage and loss due to floods and 30 percent is the result of droughts. The most significant impact on Cambodia's agriculture sector has been from flooding, although as high water levels in the Mekong River and Tonle Sap Lake observed. Impacts of climate change trends include increased frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall events, earlier onset of the monsoon season, increased rain intensity and frequency leading to floods and unexpected dry periods during the rainy season. Annual mean temperature has increased at a rate of 0.23°C per decade since 1965. The average annual rainfall over the project areas is approximately 1487 mm, with an earlier onset of the monsoon season.

### Economic Impact

The increase in mean annual temperature and decrease in dry season rainfall have manifested in droughts and water shortages that have affected agricultural productivity. Droughts have had a significant impact on Cambodia's agriculture sector. The estimated US$ 356.23 million loss and damage from 2013 floods, water and irrigation infrastructure accounted for approximately 48 percent of the total economic losses in the country.

### Drought Frequency

Drought has had a significant impact on Cambodia's agriculture sector. In 2009, out of 24 provinces affected by drought, 15 were severely affected while 9 were moderately affected. 364 hectares of transplants rice in 2011, drought affected 3804 hectares of rice fields while in 2012, drought affected 70% of the total area planted with rice (CEDMA, 2017). Drought events have become more frequent and are expected to be intensity in future.

12. Agriculture currently accounts for (94-98%) of the water use in Cambodia with 1-2% for industry and 4% for the domestic sectors. Water use across sections is 27% of available water that flows through the country annually, and water scarcity results primarily from limited access to irrigation and a lack of water storage capacity. In 2050, there will be an increase in water demand by 13%, 48% for agricultural uses (irrigation and domestic uses) will increase to 15% and 11% respectively by 2050 leading to increased competition for water resources. These increases are driven by population growth, urbanization, climate change (e.g., droughts, floods, deforestation, etc.) that are transboundary or national. Therefore, enhancing water use efficiency in agriculture is critical to meeting future demands and includes through tailoring advisories linked to agrometeorological information and climate forecasts.

### Key Barriers to Climate resilience

1. Decline in the rainy season days
2. Rapidly increasing temperature and evapotranspiration
3. Increased risk of heavy precipitation potentially leading to flooding, soil erosion, and landslides
4. Unpredictability of rainy season onset and the timing of rainfall within the season.

## 20. Climate analysis has highlighted four main risks for farmers: (i) a decline in the rainy season days, despite increasing precipitation; the project area is likely to experience more extreme heat; (ii) rapidly increasing temperature and evapotranspiration; (iii) increased risk of heavy precipitation potentially leading to flooding, soil erosion, and landslides; (iv) unpredictability of rainy season onset and the timing of rainfall within the season. ## Major barriers to sustainable and climate-resilient development of irrigation and water infrastructure in the project area are insufficient investment in planning and long-term maintenance of infrastructure as a systematic way. Many farmers lack access to remote sensing technologies, which would improve their efficiency. In addition, the technical capacities of relevant national institutions and Farmer Water User Associations (FWUAs) are limited, and there is no capacity building for FWUAs during the construction phase. In most irrigation schemes, there is a lack of a systematic cost-recovery system leading to its inefficiency. The project will address these challenges by strengthening institutional arrangements including a capacity needs assessment of MODWRM, PDVRMARS and relevant FWUCFs in the 3-12 months period after project completion. This will be done through workshops and training sessions (3-1) under component 3 in the project to build the skills needed to undertake O&M responsibilities and make the arrangements more sustainable and inclusive.

# Alignment with ongoing projects and programs

The project is aligned with IFAD’s Strategic Framework (2025-2030) and relevant Sustainable Goals (SDGs) 6, 12, and 13 and will support the United Nations Sustainable Development Cooperation Framework (UN SDCF) and the UN’s sustainable development objectives in FAD’s Country Strategy Paper (COSOP 2022-27) by supporting the development and adoption of climate-resilient and environmentally sustainable agriculture techniques, promote agricultural diversification and climate resilience of poor rural people to achieve food security, nutrition security, and income generation for smallholder farmers and climate resilient development as outlined in the RSC’s overarching Partnership Strategy Realizing the Cambodia Sustainable Agriculture and Climate Resilience (SACR) Project and the Land and Natural Resources Management of the Mekong Region Cooperation Programme (MRCP) 2022-2025.

## The project design utilizes lessons and experiences from IFAD's on-going projects in the country. These lessons illustrate that public extension services are generally underfunded and understaffed, and many focus on quantity rather than quality of service delivery. As a result, they have limited impact and reach out expand outreach; there is also need to provide greater focus on value addition and increased access to markets. In order to address these challenges, the project will support public extension services to build demand in terms of quality and volume of production required, access to resources that can help them add value and better link to markets through information technology. IFAD’s experience in Cambodia shows the great potential for public extension services to be more effective when they are supported by private sector partners with their multiple constraints (Agricultural Services Programme for Innovation, Resilience and Extension (ASPIRE) Project). The project will support public extension services to improve their service delivery facilities, creating markets and ensuring access to financial services. It is also possible to empower rural women through the use of mobile technology and information systems that can help them to increase a demand driven approach learned from ASPIRE on agriculture extension, the need for support to market infrastructure, digital technology, and youth programmes (Sustainable Assets for Agriculture Markets, Business and Innovation (SAMBIT) Project).

## Lessons from Accelerating Inclusive Markets for Smallholders project (AMIS) regarding the effectiveness of public extension services in Cambodia. The project will also support the development of tools for improved market access will be deployed. Projects can also enable farmers to become more resilient and recover faster after a disaster with appropriate resources that can help them in the recovery (ASPIRE).

## The design also builds on similar ongoing and planned projects and programmes in the country financed by other donors, including the following: Cambodia’s National Agricultural Policy (NAP) 2015-2030; Cambodia Agriculture Value Chain Sector Project (GCF GP 076) on application of CSA technologies and market access for smallholder farmers; the ongoing ASEAN-Cambodia Capacity Building Project (ACBP) (GCF GP 149); the ongoing ASEAN-Cambodia Capacity Building Project (ACBP) (GCF GP 158) in areas of value addition and market linkages for improved business environment for farmers. The alignment with these projects will be achieved by complementing their activities, particularly the activities in PEARL – specifically, it will leverage PEARL’s outputs on tailored agro-meteorological advisory services to support decision making by agricultural producers and value chain actors.

## Both IFAD and PEARL’s feasibility study assessed that Cambodia’s robust network of hydrological stations provides a good basis for early warning systems and can inform on multiple hazards, significant infrastructural and capacity gaps remain in the provision and dissemination of agrometeorological forecasts and climate information to farmers. The project will support the development of tools and water use efficiency. CASUR uses key lessons from the GEF project Promoting Climate Resilient Livelihoods in Rice-Based Communities in the Tonle Sap Region and will link with future projects on rice-based livelihoods in Cambodia. The project will also build on the experience gained by PEARL in a protected landscapes: bio-diversity friendly crop and livestock systems for adaptation” that aims to

### 30. International Rice Research Institute (IRRI)

The International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) has been long engaged in Cambodia in promoting locally developed climate-resilient rice technologies and practices. Since its launch under the Excellence in Agriculture (EaA) Initiative, many lessons have been implemented since its launch in 2020.

### B.2 (a). Theory of Change Narrative and Diagram

The theory of change is based on the assumption that by increasing access to climate-resilient rice technologies and practices, farmers will be able to increase their productivity and income while reducing their vulnerability to climate change impacts.

### 31. The Adaptive Climate Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CADAR) Project

The CADAR project is focusing on climate change adaptation with cross-cutting benefits through the reduction of GHG emissions linked to this use of energy-intensive irrigation systems, by using renewable energy for water pumping, and cover techniques (retained residue) to reduce evapotranspiration – underpinned by timely and relevant early warning and climate information services. The project will also focus on increasing access to climate-resilient rice technologies and practices.

### 32. Overall Goal of the Project

The overall goal of the project is to make the agriculture sector in Cambodia resilient and sustainable through the adoption of climate-resilient rice technologies and practices, timely and relevant early warning and climate information services, and improved irrigation systems that enable farmers to adapt to climate risks and mitigate crop emissions through use of energy- and water-efficient techniques and practices.

### 33. Theory of Change

The theory of change of the project has premised its investments on an understanding of the pathways that can help reduce GHG emissions and help smallholder farmers adapt to climate change by applying climate-resilient agricultural technologies.

**Page 22**

34. The Theory of Change of the project is based on the assumption: IF the irrigation system is upgraded and modernized, farmers are made aware of climate risks and extended support for mitigation and adaptation THEN GHG emissions will be reduced by 10% in the first year and 25% after five years, and CO2 eq emission will be reduced. BECAUSE, of enhanced resilience of the agricultural sector to climate change resulting from improved irrigation infrastructure, more efficient water use, resilient irrigation infrastructure, water and soil management, better market linkage, and institutional support.

35. The theory of change postulates the following with respect to each of the three key pathways that CAISAR has chosen for implementation:

Pathway 1: System Level

At the farm level, the lack of knowledge about adaptation technologies and practices and limited access to improved adaptation technologies and clean energy sources leads to low productivity, inability to cope with climate change impacts, and increased GHG emissions. This can be addressed by increasing awareness and adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies that are appropriate for each farm’s conditions, affordability or high labour requirements. The technological packages that will be provided include: (i) improved irrigation systems; (ii) water management tools; (iii) soil health management; and (iv) integrated pest management and enhancing the use of organic inputs, diversifying to other crops, promoting residue retention, and deploying solar irrigation pumps (to pump water from canals to fields) instead of the diesel pumps. CAISAR will link farmers with innovative entrepreneurs who offer technologies or engage in value chain activities that are appropriate for each farm’s conditions. The increased awareness and access to these outputs will be reduction of GHG emissions by 1,056,792tCO2eq over 20 years (detailed in Annex 23), reduction in crop losses and improved production and enhancement of delivery delay and post-harvest losses. In the first year, approximately 48,000 farmers will be reached with these outputs; this will mitigate approximately 35,062 hectares (ha) in the rainy season and about 22,051 ha in the dry season – increasing ongoing GHG emissions by 79.5% and 50%, respectively.

36. Increased adoption of new and improved climate-resilient agriculture technologies and practices can be achieved through working closely with smallholders. Often the farmers that firms face in adapting the technologies and practices to their conditions are not aware of what is available, or they do not have the resources to adopt them. CAISAR will link the farmers with innovative entrepreneurs who offer the technologies and practices that are appropriate and enhance their adoption; CAISAR will link the farmers with resource providers such as extension services, input suppliers, credit institutions, etc., to better integrate them into the value chains and increasing access to finance to enable them to continue to use these inputs and strengthening the value chain of key agriculture commodities. CAISAR will organize its investments in a way that ensures that the technologies and practices are appropriate for each farm’s process about adaptation to changing rainfall patterns and increasing drought conditions, providing them access to water and soil management tools, and other climate-resilient agricultural practices. This will improve decision-making, and provide start-up support to innovative entrepreneurs that offer technologies or engage in value chain activities that are appropriate for each farm’s conditions.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

#### Pathway 2: Irrigation Scheme Level

- **Much of the irrigation infrastructure in Cambodia is outdated and in need of rehabilitation and upgrading,** and most of it is not climate proofed and is vulnerable to damages from floods that also result in siltation of canals and poor drainage – particularly during extreme precipitation and flooding events.
- **According to a recent study by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF),** most of the irrigation schemes in Cambodia were never completed and do not always have the appropriate tertiary level infrastructure. This results in high conveyance losses which aggravates the impact of climate risk. The Theory of Change of CAIDAR builds on this understanding that improving the design, construction, operation, maintenance, and high conveyance systems will increase the efficiency and reliability of the systems, protect the natural capital stocks, reduce the need for frequent rehabilitation and reconstruction owing to frequent breakdowns, and lower the cost of operations. The Theory of Change also highlights the importance of strengthening the irrigation scheme management and governance as this will make the systems more sustainable through cost recovery mechanisms.
- **The introduction of solar pumps in place of diesel pumps is expected to reduce the amount of irrigation water used for pumping and reduce losses,** cropping system diversity, and enhance food security. The use of solar pumps will also replace diesel pumps with solar pumps that will contribute, albeit limitedly, to reduced GHG emissions and generate additional income for farmers. It should be noted however that due to any type of maladaptation as the solar pumps will not be used for pumping ground water but simply to help convey the water from the main canals to farmer’s fields as well as to drain the flood water.

#### Pathway 3: Institutional and Enabling Environment Level

- **At the national level, the institutional and policy framework in Cambodia can be strengthened through strengthening the sector institutions,** including the improved irrigation infrastructure designs and planning (river basin management), use of Information Communication Technology (ICT), deploying water accounting systems, and improving the capacity of the sector institutions.
- **The project will also support the development of a national strategy for climate change adaptation,** including the preparation of a loss and damage strategy, enhancing M&E capacity and encouraging greater collaboration between the sector institutions. The project will develop a post-project strategy for sustainability and engaging the private sector in its design and implementation can help ensure that the institutional strengthening is sustainable over time.

### B.3 Project programme description

40. The project's results will be achieved through the implementation of three components. The first component will focus on building farm-level resilience and investments at the local level in the command area of the Krong Porleng sub-districts. This includes the establishment of a flood control system, which is designed to bring resilience at the irrigation flood control/prevention system level and will involve modernizing and climate proofing existing infrastructure and introducing new technologies such as solar-powered pumps for the dry season. The first component is designed to bring about a transformation in these farming systems through awareness, capacity building and re-orientation of farmers' production practices and assist in both improved water management and increased farm-level resilience.

41. The project area is expected to comprise 32,056 Hectares. The schemes or sub-projects under the project will include the Ou Ta Paeng (OTP) and Lum Harch and 4 smaller sub-projects in the Krong Porleng valley. This number includes the total area of all the projects. In the OTP scheme, which is expected to plant wet season rice crop, the area in which a second irrigated dry season rice crop can be established will be about 23,051 hectares. The OTP scheme also includes the establishment of poultry homestead gardens and poultry production as well as aquaculture can be established. In several of the 4 Krong Porleng sub-projects commercial vegetable plans are due to the close vicinity to Phnom Penh city and will provide a market for these crops, which will help to increase the income levels and ensure that the benefits from the investments.

**Component 1: Farm-level climate adaptation and resilience (NCDD-S)**


---
**Page 26**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

42. The objective of this component is to build climate resilience (CR) of smallholder farmers and ensure sustainable production through evidence-based planning and context-relevant climate resilient practices at the farm level. This component is designed to address the lack of knowledge and skills to deploy technologies and practices that are climate-resilient, as well as the lack of institutional capacity to propagate them. It will introduce farmers with various climate resilient techniques and practices for both rice and non-rice crops. The component will also focus on improving irrigation management for value addition, facilitate market linkages, introduce agro-meteorological information and services for climate informed decision making, and promote the use of climate smart technologies and practices in sustainable production and marketing. Component 1 is expected to benefit over 110,000 farming households. In addition, some of the technologies and practices, for example the Alternate Wetting and Drying technique (AWD), are also expected to contribute towards reducing water consumption by up to 50% while also reducing methane emissions contributing to GHG reduction. The component will be implemented by NCDCS through its existing network of extension officers and other partners. The project will also seek to capitalize on the presence of existing platforms, studies and plans in order to avoid potential overlapping, duplication or gaps in implementation.

Outcome 1: Reduction in crop losses & improved production

Output 1.1: Increased farmer capacity in climate resilient agriculture (NCDC-S)

43. This output will focus on developing farmer’s capacity in deploying climate resilient technologies and practices to transform the agricultural production system to adapt to the changing climate context. Separate dialogues will be held with farmers, extension officers and other stakeholders to determine the content of the training curriculum, the schedule and delivery methodology. The main objective is to strengthen adaptive management strategies to mitigate climate variability risks. Both men and women farmers will be trained to first understand the climate change impacts on their farming systems and then develop adaptation strategies for production in a manner that is better adapted to factoring in the agro-ecological context and expected changes in rainfall patterns, temperature and other climatic variables. The focus of the training will be on climate focused rice cultivation. However, for those parts of the project areas which have insufficient irrigation water supply or no seasonal rain fall, the focus will be on non-rice crops such as maize, beans, groundnuts production and horticulture especially for farmers with small land holdings. Crops of interest to women will be included in the selection. Farmers are generally resistant to adopting new technologies due to their lack of knowledge about them or because they are not climate smart, do not fit well into their local climaticity if it is labour intensive, costly or not suitable for smaller farmers. Those designing the courses should therefore ensure that the training content and methodology are appropriate and can be refined over time based on the local context and crop choices and emerging adaptation technologies.

The project will use the Farmer’s Field and Business School approach (FFBS) and design context specific training modules to address the needs of both men and women farmers. The training will cover climate smart non-rice crops and will use a cascade approach to disseminate the training messages to a wider audience using key opinion leaders, extension officers and other stakeholders. The training content should be re-oriented so that at least 40% will be women. The improvement of the irrigation systems will be critical to the training on the transition to farming provided.

Activity 1.1: Develop climate-based action plans (AP) to transform agriculture with CR practices and technologies (NCDC-S)

44. The project will utilize a Climate Smart (CS) Mapping approach to develop awareness materials on climate change impacts, vulnerability and adaptation in RRB areas. These maps will be developed for the first year as a part of their TA contract, containing participatory data with GIS and crop modeling to create risk maps identifying areas vulnerable to climate stresses like droughts and floods. These maps will also outline


---
**Page 27**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

**D**

recommendation and adaptation options and be gender disaggregated where required to ensure that the needs of women are reflected. Additionally, the project will facilitate the creation of Value Chain Action Plans (VCAP) at the provincial level for both rice and non-rice farmers. The VCAPs will focus on improving the non-rice agricultural value chain planning at the provincial level. The VCAP will help the target provinces to conduct VC planning and the communities in augmenting their development plans with considerations on agronomic and marketing aspects, including climate change adaptation options. The VCAP will also provide training for women farmers, Producer Organizations, water users' groups, service providers, and NGOs, ensuring resilience of the agricultural value chain at provincial level.

45. The annual AP will specify preferred rice varieties, vegetable choices, and adaptation measures like soil fertility management, crop rotation, along with capacity support needed for the selected value chains. It will also outline implementation schedules, targets, and monitoring arrangements, with revisions made annually based on feedback from farmers and other stakeholders. This will be done in close collaboration with household-headed households in their development. A community-based monitoring and evaluation system will track AP outcomes and provide feedback to the project team for continuous improvement of APs targeting smallholder farmers. Furthermore, representatives from each commune involved in AP preparation will participate in discussion forums at the provincial level to share experiences and lessons learned. The project will also ensure communication between the commune and district levels. Special efforts will be made to ensure the inclusion of women farmers in these discussions. In addition, a series of training workshops and discussion forums will be held to discuss their role in agriculture development in Cambodia. CAISAR will include elements of the AP into comprehensive plans and build capacity for women farmers to make informed decisions on their own. Representatives from other provinces will also be invited to learn from those experiences for further dissemination.

46. The provincial level staff will also be trained in the development of these VCAPs to enable them to develop them at the commune level. This will ensure that the communities are well prepared and equipped to work with local farmer groups and cooperatives and develop them independently. Representatives from other provinces will also be invited to learn from those experiences for further dissemination.

47. Key training topics will include best practices for irrigated and rainfed lowland rice, vegetable production techniques (e.g., drip irrigation), fish farming, and improving household chicken production. Where required

## Activity 1.1.6 Community-based monitoring and evaluation (CBME) of the implementation (NCDG-S)

56. Local farming communities play a crucial role in implementing climate-smart measures, and their involvement is essential for ensuring the successful adoption of these practices. The Community-Based Monitoring and Evaluation (CBME) approach enhances community ownership from planning to execution by involving local farmers, extension service providers, project staff, and other stakeholders to track the effectiveness of interventions. These indicators may include metrics on training participation rates, crop yields, water use efficiency, and the introduction of new climate-smart crop varieties. The indicators will be validated for integration into the project's monitoring and evaluation framework and national Climate Action Monitoring system.

57. The CBME approach will be integrated with the commune-based monitoring system. This will facilitate information flow both ways: allowing feedback from national levels to inform commune-based systems, and vice versa. At the sub-national level, discussions will explore the potential for integrating community action planning and other processes into routine operations beyond the project's duration. The M&E system will report data on the project's impact at various levels of the monitoring hierarchy.

Community-level officers and MAFF and NCDG staff will be included in understanding the monitoring system so that they can provide feedback to the commune-based monitoring system, which may lead to its upscaling to other areas.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

### Activity 1.2.1: Value chain study and planning (ICSD-S)

The project will focus on enhancing the value chains for rice, vegetables, native chicken, and fish in selected agricultural clusters. The project will be implemented by the first six teams of the Agricultural Enterprise Development (AED) team, supported by international experts, national partners, and district partners to develop concise value chain action plans (VCAPs). These plans will involve mapping commodity communities, analyzing structural and dynamic factors, performing financial and economic analyses, and highlighting interventions that provide higher income potential, are inclusive of vulnerable groups, and are climate-smart.

### Activity 1.2.2: Establish Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (MSPs) (ICSD-S)

The project will establish Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (MSPs) to facilitate collaboration among value chain actors, focusing on enhancing governance and improving relationships within selected agricultural value chains. Two types of MSPs will be created: District Commodity-Based Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (DMSPs) and Provincial Agricultural Enterprise Development Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (PMSPs). These platforms will bring together key stakeholders such as farmers, traders, financial institutions, and Provincial Agro-Financing Workshops to connect local producers with buyers, processors, and investors. The MSPs will be used for market information sharing, to improve market linkages, enhance transparency, and foster partnerships, while the Agro-Financing Workshops will occur bi-annually at the provincial level to inform participants about financing options and opportunities.

The MSPs will provide a structured approach to identify challenges and opportunities within the value chain. The workshops will discuss potential interactions and joint actions that can be undertaken from both perspectives. The outcomes may include improved market information, stronger partnerships, coordinated actions, and conflict resolution among value chain actors. Follow-up activities will ensure continued dialogue between stakeholders through regular meetings, technical assistance facilitation and engagement. How women can best be engaged in the value chains will be an area of focus.

The Provincial Agro-Financing Workshops will provide a platform for financial institutions and relevant stakeholders to share innovative financing options, ensuring the participation of MSMEs, and can be used as a mechanism to promote the use of microfinance. The workshops will also facilitate the organization of the large number of events throughout the project lifecycle which will foster partnerships and can be used as a mechanism to promote the use of microfinance.

The bi-annual workshops at the provincial level may not continue after the end of the project due to mutual benefit. While the bi-annual workshops at the provincial level is expected that some of the introductions made during

## Activity: 1.2.3 Public Private Producer Partnership Facility (PPF) and leveraging of capital (INCCD-S)

### 63. Cambodia's agricultural sector has experienced substantial growth over the past two decades, leading to increased food security, improved livelihoods, and a significant reduction in poverty levels. However, this growth has also highlighted the need for greater focus on climate change adaptation, sustainability, and environmental impact of this growth. In response, the Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC) has adopted its Agriculture Sector Strategic Plan (2015-2030), which aims to achieve sustainable development through a "Triple Green Line" approach, focusing on economic, social, and environmental goals. The RGC aims to foster public-private partnerships to enhance investments, improve the quality of services, ensure access to finance, and increase the capacity for climate change adaptation.

### 64. The Public Private Partnership Facility (PPF) is designed to strengthen micro, small, and medium enterprises in Cambodia's agricultural sector by providing financial support to women-owned businesses and women entrepreneurs. Through grants and technical assistance, the PPF encourages investments that benefit both businesses and local men and women farmers. Eligible enterprises can receive financial support for up to 50% of their total investment costs. The PPF aims to improve project feasibility, viability, equity participation, and potential impact on target groups. The types of investments which are eligible under the PPF include: (i) capacity building; (ii) access to finance; (iii) market development; (iv) capacity for processing, access to markets; (v) improved product quality, reliability, and produce in sufficient scale to meet demand; (vi) technology transfer; and (vii) other investments. The PPF aims to increase the capacity for inclusion of youth, a target of 20% will be established for inclusion of youth. Separate windows are envisaged for women-owned enterprises and women entrepreneurs.

### 65. Building on FAO's ongoing projects such as AMS and SAMABAT, the initiative will encourage the financial sector to identify and invest in climate change adaptation and rural value chain development opportunities. While the PPF is designed to provide grants and technical assistance, it does not include any management tools to engage in climate adaptation efforts. To bridge this gap, CAISAR aims to attract financing from various financial institutions by enhancing their understanding of rural activities and investments that are climate-smart. The initiative will also support the development of a database at the farm and business level by organizing semi-annual Provincial Agro-Finance Workshops to improve information on agricultural products and services. This will help in the creation and dissemination of information. Further, commercial finance will also be integrated into the 4 inputs where possible, creating opportunities for climate-smart investments through financial intermediaries.

### Output 1.3. Increased access to climate information and advisory services for climate responsive water-use and crop planning (INCCD-S)

### 66. In Cambodia, the capacity to utilize weather and climate data for agricultural applications is limited, despite significant progress in recent years. The Climate Information for Agriculture project by the GCF identifies critical gaps in meteorological services, particularly in value-added services and their uptake by farmers. While some NGOs have provided climate-informed advisories, these efforts tend to be project-specific and lack


---
**Page 33**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 27

67. To address these challenges, Component 2.4 of the project will focus on strengthening national institutions to develop and disseminate information systems, enabling more effective forecasts and early warnings for floods and droughts. This output aims to develop and disseminate relevant agrometeorological information services at provincial level. The project will work with existing institutional structures and will draw on approaches such as PICS (Participatory Integrated Climate Services for Agriculture) that allows for the development of a demand-driven, participatory process for the production and dissemination of agricultural information. The information needs of women and the most appropriate channels of communication for them will be identified in each province. A provincial coordination platform will be established to facilitate information management. A diversity of dissemination channels and mechanisms has been proposed to take unique circumstances into account. In general, information will be accessible through mobile apps but also disseminated via radio (e.g., community radio stations), television, SMS, and other means.

68. The project will work with existing institutional structures in each province to develop a provincial coordination platform that will facilitate the production of agrometeorological information and its delivery through various channels. The focus is on building capacity at the provincial level for the development and dissemination of agrometeorological information and its use within the agricultural sector, particularly during critical periods such as the cropping season or when specific risks are triggered. A coordination platform will be established at the provincial level to facilitate the production and dissemination of agrometeorological information. The project will work with existing institutional structures in each province to develop a provincial coordination platform that will facilitate the production of agrometeorological information and its delivery through various channels.

Activity 1.3.1 Strengthening meteorological stations in the targeted area (INCDG-05)

69. A process to integrate meteorological with agricultural data and produce national level agrometeorological advisory is being developed outside this project. This component will facilitate the transition of this process into a sustainable model at provincial level.

Activity 1.3.2 Establish Provincial-Level Coordination Platform for climate-informed agricultural advisories (INCDG-05)

# Activity 1.3.2 Build the capacities to deliver services (NCDS-5)

70. Capacity development will focus on enhancing the technical skills necessary for climate data collection and management, as well as translating this data into agricultural advisories through modeling and translation tools. This will be achieved by providing training in climate data collection and analysis among various government entities and farming communities, primarily targeting staff and professionals in central offices of the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MoWRM), as well as farmers to train approximately 250 individuals through structured training courses, including short-term summer courses on GIS and data validation, as well as advanced training on topics like crop modeling, both formal training and informal learning. The training will be conducted in a way that builds the capacity of individual participants.

Inclusion of women will be an area of focus for capacity building ensuring that at least 40% of the individuals trained are female.

# Activity 1.3.4 Establish the agrometeorology information and warning systems and the mechanisms including advisories raising (NCDS-5)

72. To enhance climate advisory services will be structured as a daily, bi-weekly or weekly bulletin (interval will be confirmed during implementation), utilizing multiple channels to reach farmers and stakeholders in targeted areas. The bulletins will provide weather forecasts, crop development stages, and project irrigation requirements. Given the existing gaps in systematic communication within Cambodia, the following strategies will be employed to ensure that advisories are received by all relevant individuals.

This includes posting advisories on the websites of the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MoWRM), as well as local government platforms. Additionally, advisories will be broadcast through loudspeakers at community centers, radio stations, and mobile phones. The bulletins will also be disseminated to farmers' groups and women's farmer associations via text messages.

Special effort will be made to identify the channels of communication most accessible to women farmers in order to ensure that they receive timely information. This includes working with women farmers, commune leaders, and Farmer Water User Federations (FWUFs), who will help disseminate advisories through their networks. The bulletins will also be posted on the websites of ongoing Farmer Field Schools (FFS) to educate and inform participants directly. Furthermore, mobile applications will be developed for smartphones that will allow farmers to access the advisories.

The advisories will employ real-time access to advisory services, ensuring that farmers can easily obtain relevant information and make informed decisions about their agricultural practices.


# Output 1.4: Increased resilience of farm road infrastructure to climate change (NCDS-5)

73. Approximately 90 kilometers of rural roads in targeted project areas will be upgraded to enhance climate-resilient infrastructure, including the construction of culverts, bridges, and other drainage systems and social services. These improvements will differ from “inception roads” constructed atop embankments of primary roads. The new roads will be designed with a 10-year lifespan for maintenance and upgrade, and maintenance of upgraded irrigation systems under Component 2. When feasible, flood defense structures will also be designed to serve as reservoirs. Road construction and access improvements will benefit about 10,673 farmers across 35 villages directly and another 1.15 million people indirectly by improving connectivity.


---
**Page 35**
---

74\. Road designs will take into account current and future climate change risks, including increased flooding and drought conditions, which could affect unpaved roads, leading to higher soil levels, reduced visibility, and poorer air quality. Addressing flooding and soil moisture content will be prioritized to protect road investments. Resilience will be enhanced through climate-resilient designs, embankments, drainage systems, culverts, bridges, drainage channels, and erosion control using vegetation and bio-engineering methods.

D

75\. Improving local road networks is a key supporting factor for socio-economic development and will be integrated with Component 2, particularly along irrigation canals allowing farmers to better monitor crop progress. The design of these roads will be informed by the hydrological data collected under Component 1, managed by NCCDS. Activities for these roads will include:

Activity A.1.4 Identification, selection and initial planning (NCCDS-S)

76\. This activity involves engaging with communities and the stakeholders to gather insights on local contexts, opportunities, and challenges. This engagement will inform the design process, utilizing hydrological data collected under Component 1. The activity will be aligned with the Rural Roads Improvements Project III: Key data on traffic volumes, climate change impacts, and existing conditions will be used in conjunction with NCCDS-S.

Activity A.2.3 Technical survey and design considerations, preparation of cost estimate (NCCDS-S)

77\. In this activity, the design of rural roads will prioritize climate resilience, addressing the anticipated increases in flooding and drought due to climate change. The hydrology of the project areas indicates that these risks are more prevalent during the rainy season than other seasons; therefore, design approaches should consider measures more prevalent in dry seasons. To mitigate these impacts, design approaches will incorporate advanced techniques such as embankments, drainage systems, culverts, bridges, and vegetation-based erosion control.

The integration of climate resilience features will ensure roads remain functional year-round while also improving the road's capacity for flood protection, reducing soil erosion, and vegetation-based erosion protection.

78\. Three types of roads will be considered for improvement: primary embanked roads (road embankment made with a minimum width of 4.5 meters), secondary embanked roads (road embankment made with a minimum width of 4.5 meters with a slope of 1:2.5, while ensuring adequate side road protection using vegetation-based erosion control measures), and tertiary embanked roads (road embankment made with a minimum width of 3.0 meters). Type 3 and Type 4 roads will incorporate features such as increased embankment heights, improved drainage systems, culverts, bridges, and vegetation-based erosion control to reduce the risk of flooding. Overall, the design will be informed by hydrological data and best practices to ensure flood protection and road resilience.

Activity A.3.4 Improved kilometres of farm roads (NCCDS-S)

79\. The construction of the road works will be executed through competitively procured local contractors and supervised by both technical experts and community members for proper quality assurance. The local communities will also participate in monitoring and reporting on the construction works and will be trained for the purpose by the consultants.

Activity A.4.1 Handling over of the completed works (NCCDS-S)

80\. The completed works will be handed over to the Ministry of Rural Development (MRD). The MRD will commit to the operation and maintenance of the roads in line with the sustainable road maintenance programme, which includes a 5-year agreement between the project and MRD. The costs of the annual GSM will be estimated and included in the MOU.

## Activity 2.1.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system improvement (MOWRAM)

### 56. The technical analysis will be based on the following assumptions:
- A detailed DED for all irrigation and drainage/control systems will be prepared taking into consideration the desired social and environmental impact assessment to minimize the amount of private land to be used and plot the new water distribution network.
- The new water distribution network will be designed in such a way that it can accommodate the existing irrigation and drainage control systems if feasible. This DED will primarily be based on gravity-driven flow but in case the nature of the terrain does not allow for this, then pumping stations will be installed at the beginning of the secondary or tertiary canals to allow for sufficient head to have good distribution of the natural water.

### 57. In this final step the different layout options as identified in the FIRM phase will also be reinvestigated for technical, environmental and social/safeguard considerations. The implementation will focus on maximizing the benefits from the new system while minimizing the negative impacts such as GHG emissions. In addition, current diesel pumps lifting water to tertiary and secondary canals will be replaced where possible by collecting rainwater in systems which have pre-conditions are acceptable with low pressure water supply. This will reduce the level of pumping and hence reduce the amount of diesel fuel use efficiency. Moreover, these technologies will allow to save time and labour, especially for women who are responsible for fetching water.

### Activity 2.1.2 Implementation of infrastructure upgrading (MOWRAM)

### 88. The implementation will follow a participatory approach involving farmers at all stages from the survey design, construction, implementation and operations. It will also cover all cross-cutting issues such as gender equality, social inclusion and environmental sustainability. The land acquisition for non-irrigated activities related to this project will be guided by the Resettlement Planning Framework (RPF). The project will minimize land acquisition by following existing rights of way where land acquisition is required; it will implement fair and transparent procedures in accordance with national laws and regulations, including land tenure, land acquisition, including socio-economic surveys, eligibility and entitlement criteria, consultation, grievance redressal mechanisms and access to resources for affected persons. This subcomponent will be financed by AIB and Government.

### Activity 2.1.3 Preparation of canal O&M plans including application of ICT and SCADA for operation (MOWRAM)

### 89 Upon completion of construction works, schemes will be handed over to the Integrated Agriculture Department (IAD) of MOWRAM, and O&M responsibilities will be shared between the concerned Provincial Department of Water Resources and Meteorology (PDWRM) and FWUC. MOWRAM's integrated Agricultural Development Project (IADP) will provide technical support for the operation and maintenance of a system on-going M&E and reporting of scheme performance. FWUCs will receive financial support until they are able to manage their schemes independently. The IADP will be responsible for training staff at MO- WRAM will arrange government budget for funding routine and periodic maintenance of the infrastructure, including water quality monitoring and management. This subcomponent will be handed over after FWUC formation. # Output 2.2: Flood-proofing and drainage improvement (MOVIAM)

90. It focuses on improving disaster prevention and protection of farmlands and assets by establishment of early warning systems and improvement of the existing drainage and flood protection systems. ## Activity 2.2.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading (MOVIAM)

91. This output focuses on equipping the 6 schemes with facilities to withstand the currently annually recurring floods. The scheme will be improved by establishing flood protection embankments for disaster prevention and protection of farmlands and assets by the establishment of flood protection embankments and other measures such as river training, construction of solar-powered water pumps at the existing drainage networks and flood embankments. This flooding is partly due to the water inflow from the downstream end of the scheme from the Torre La Sapa Lake and partly from local runoff of the higher areas upstream of the subprojects. The upgrading of the existing river training network (especially in Ou Ta Paing River and Lum Harch schemes) will be improved on the results of the hydrological modelling and consultation with stakeholders.

92. The applicability of nature-based solutions in flood management, e.g., revival of old river channels like Ou Ta Paing River, embankment improvement with vegetation strip, etc.) will be reviewed and subsequent measures will be taken to improve the existing river training network. These works will be supported to restore, and construct farm level damages as required while project will install sub surface drainage in needy areas. In addition, it will also support strengthening of river training and their embankments to withstand the currently annually recurring floods and other extreme events.

## Output 2.4: Water information system established and operational in three sub-projects35 (MoWRM)

97. The SCIDAM model is crucial to tackle the significant challenges in the agricultural sector on the inefficiency of irrigation systems, characterized by both indiscriminate and irregular water distribution, leading to substantial water wastage. This approach offers real-time information on water levels and flow rates, enabling more accurate decision-making for farmers and other stakeholders. The SCIDAM model also provides a framework for the modernization of existing irrigation systems, preventing flooding and it does so with a fundamental, cost-effective approach. The SCIDAM system allows for command-and-control functionalities while collecting valuable data on irrigation. In this way, it rationalizes and streamlines the irrigation process, significantly mitigating the wastage of water.

98. The water and weather information further helps in planning the crop production and also in the management of the irrigation systems. However, the furthering NRMW CWISP programs in the MoWRM will be developing area specific flood and drought warning systems for the project areas. This will help farmers to plan their agricultural activities based on water available systems to help farmers in the project areas better plan their agriculture and protect their livelihoods from climate change impacts. Furthermore, a network of weather stations and mobile phones will be established so that farmers are timely informed on evolving threats and challenges.

### B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

#### Accredited Entity (AE)
109. The International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) has a long history of implementing rural infrastructure projects in Cambodia, which became a member in 1992. IFAD has supported ten investment projects for a total value of USD 940 million in collaboration with a host of co-financing partners. IFAD will continue to be the lead executing agency (EA) and will also serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project and will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (ii) ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with the terms of reference as outlined in the Project Document; (iii) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timelines and quality of reporting to GCF; and (iv) undertaking any other tasks required by the GCF. The IFAD Country Director will be the point of responsibility are undertaken in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Manual. IFAD will also have a Project Officer stationed at the IFAD country office, as well as an implementation office in Bangkok, and its country office in Cambodia. AIFD is co-financing the project and will provide funds for the establishment of the Implementation Unit (IU) to be responsible for the overall supervision of the project with IFAD and also participate in and contribute to the evaluation of the project.

#### Executing Entity
110. The AE will enter into a financing agreement (i.e., a subsidiary agreement) with the Royal Government of Cambodia, acting through (i) MEF; (ii) MOARM; and (iii) NCODS. (EES). The MEF is the ministry authorized to sign on behalf of the Royal Government of Cambodia for all agreements. The MEF will set out the roles and responsibilities of MEF, MOARM and NCODS. MOARM will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU) in Phnom Penh to manage the project implementation, including planning and reporting. In addition, the National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development Secretariat (NCDDSS) will be established as an implementing body of the project. The NCDDSS is designed to improve farm-level climate adaptation resilience, water use efficiency and institutional empowerment in rural communities through the establishment of a Project Management Unit (PMU) for implementing the component 1 and output 3.0 of Component 2. The budget allocation for the implementation of component 1 and output 3.0 will be committed based on prepared cost tables. Designated accounts will be established for both MOARM and NCODS, and funds will be transferred directly by IFAD to MOARM and NCODS.

#### Project Oversight and Steering Arrangements
111. A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established for inter-ministerial coordination. The role of the PSC is to provide strategic oversight over the project’s overall progress toward its intended goals and outcomes, to make decisions regarding the project's overall direction and priorities, and to ensure that all parties are aligned with the vision and objectives of the project. The PSC makes decisions based on consensus. It will be chaired by the Minister of MOARM, with representation from the NCDDSS, MEF, MOARM, NCODS, MOI, MAFF, MOE, and the Provincial Governor’s Office of Pursat.
