**Management Arrangements**

123. The Government will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU) for the implementation of the CAISAR project. The PMU will be led by the Secretary of State (MOWRAM) as the National Project Director (NPD), and managed by an appointed Project Manager. The PMU will consist of a team of personnel from various central project management units, with coordination within the PMU. Within the PMU, project-recruited staff and staff seconded by the government will collectively compose a project delivery team that will ensure sound and effective project implementation. The PMU will be supported by the following key focal points: Director General, Human Resources and Administration Officer, Procurement Officer and Monitoring and Evaluation Officer. The PMU will also engage with other relevant stakeholders such as the Department of Water Resources and Meteorology (DWRM), Provincial Departments of Water Resources and Meteorology (PDWRMs) of the four provinces in which the project will be implemented. The PDWRMs will lead implementation at provincial levels, while the DWRM will provide technical support to the PMU. The Department of Farmer Water User Committees (DFWUC) has personnel at the central level but no office or permanent staff at the provincial and district levels. To ensure effective coordination with these committees, the Department of FWUC will appoint additional personnel at the provincial and district levels.

123. The Project Management Unit will consist of personnel from MOWRAM, the Department of Hydrology and River Works (DHRW) of MOWRAM, and Provincial Departments of Water Resources and Meteorology (PDWRMs) of the four provinces in which the project will be implemented. The PDWRMs will lead implementation at provincial levels, while the DHRW will provide technical support to the PMU. The Department of Farmer Water User Committees (DFWUC) has personnel at the central level but no office or permanent staff at the provincial and district levels. ## The Flow of Funds

117. The Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank (AIIB) and IFAD/GCF are the two main co-financiers of the project, while the RCG will provide contributions to the Project. AIIB and IFAD have provided grant funds and technical assistance for project preparation activities. An agreement will also be signed between the GCF and the RCG to allow the RCG to contribute to the project through the GCF, represented by the MEF, for IAFD and GCF proceeds and RCG’s financial support to the project.

The IFAD will provide grant funds directly to the Project. The AIIB will provide grant funds directly to the Project. The GCF funds will be provided to IFAD and then transmitted directly to the Ministry of Water Resources and Irrigation (MOWRAM) for investment in climate resilience and modernization of irrigation infrastructure. The Royal Cambodian Government will provide cash support directly during the implementation phase, which will be deposited into a separate financing account with the GCF. Also, cash support will also be provided directly to MOWRAM for investment in the climate resilience and modernization of irrigation infrastructure.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 40

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

119. The current project requires GCF funding to address both mitigation and adaptation measures which are beyond the ability of the Government to finance. There is need for significant financial resources for investment in climate change action, including adaptation and mitigation actions. The total cost of all mitigation actions is estimated to be above US$ 5.8 billion and US $2 billion for all adaptation actions. The high costs associated with these measures are a major challenge for the Government of Cambodia (GoC) with many development priorities and investment needs. One of these priorities is accelerating the current effort in reducing poverty, which has been significantly impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic. The GoC is one of the most vulnerable countries to the impacts of climate change which is already impacting its GDP growth and agricultural productivity, with a projected decline in GDP growth rates from 7% to 2025 and up to 8.9% in 2030. This could delay the country's aspirations to reach upper middle-income status.

120. Cambodia’s economy was badly affected by the COVID-19 pandemic, with GDP contracting by 3.1 percent in 2020 compared to a projected growth of 7%. The pandemic also led to an increase in poverty, estimated to be 8.5 percentage points higher than pre-pandemic levels and pushed about 460,000 people into poverty. The government responded quickly and effectively to the pandemic, assisting business and vulnerable families, but this associated costs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Introduction

The current state of development of the regulatory and fiscal incentives that inhibit private sector investments in irrigation infrastructure is a major challenge for the future sustainability of the project. The project will use USD 40 million from concessional loans and USD 50 million from grants, with an additional amount of loans from AIBG, IFAD, ADB, and the Royal Government of Cambodia. The project has proposed only 17% of the total estimated cost as a grant from the GCF, which is insufficient to cover the costs for the concessionary funding. The primary beneficiaries of the concessional funding will be smaller farmers, particularly rice farmers.

## Challenges

The current state of payment for water services is not sufficiently developed at this stage to ensure the provision of adequate and reliable water supply in a sustainable manner. This lack of transparency can increase risks and raise doubts about the ability to protect investments and ensure that they are used effectively. The private sector does not always have the necessary knowledge or experience in the design, construction, operation, maintenance, and management of irrigation systems, which may result in displacing local communities or harming ecosystems. These concerns can act as a deterrent for investors who might be reluctant to invest in the medium to long term.

## Synergy with GCF

The project will also benefit from the concessional funding provided by the GCF Public-Private-Social Partnership for Ecologically Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihoods (PPS) program, which aims to support smallholder farmers through a combination of agricultural suppliers, traders/exporters, retailers, and local financial institutions. The project is designed to complement existing public sector programs in Cambodia.

## Conclusion

The GCF funding will address the paucity of public sector funding and the lack of private sector financing due to the current stage of development of the regulatory and fiscal incentives that inhibit private sector investments in irrigation infrastructure.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 43

The document outlines several initiatives related to water management and irrigation schemes in Cambodia:

127. The project will capitalize on the existing Cambodian water law under which the farmers have to contribute a certain amount of money for maintenance, and this requirement is well accepted although at present O&M contributions are far below the required levels (usually 8–10 USD/year). Experience from some pilot schemes in Cambodia has shown that when farmers pay up to US$2.5 per ha annually, they will be able to maintain their irrigation systems effectively.

130. Water user associations enhance ownership and participation of local beneficiary communities which play their role in scheme cleaning and maintenance. To ensure the long-term institutional sustainability of such schemes, the project will invest in the development and training of Farmer Water Use Committees (FWUCs) for each water scheme. The project will also set up a team to train FWUCs on how to conduct a thorough survey of FWUCs in the CAISAR command areas, which were included during project design and are now being implemented.

129. During its institutional strengthening, MOWRAM will be assisted in developing a post-project support fund for irrigation schemes in the private sector so that they can continue to operate and maintain their systems by developing innovative and sustainable models for management and operation of maintenance of the irrigation system. The project will also assist MOWRAM in establishing clear financial incentives with transparent arrangements in place.

The project will also provide training on water management to enable them to improve the governance of the irrigation system and collection of water user charges. The project will also support the development of a national strategy for adaptation to climate change in collaboration with MOWRAM and NCCDS for better formulation of the policy and regulatory framework for encouraging private sector investment in renewable energy technologies. The project will invest in the introduction of new technologies such as solar pumps and the investment in the dissemination of information on Early Warning Systems (EWS) for flood, droughts and for operation and monitoring the irrigation system. The total GCF financing which is expected to be used for capacity building and institutional strengthening is around USD 16.3 million.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

## The CASAAR Project

### Project Description and Objectives

The project aims to promote significant yield and crop production improvements in all targeted irrigation sub-project areas. It will be novel in Cambodia, with its focus on reducing the vulnerability of rural communities to climate change by integrating a centralized Irrigation Water Management based project design that incorporates flood protection, sustainable water resource development, improved agricultural practices, solutions, environmentally aware design and gender equality.

The CASAAR project will promote economic growth through increased crop yields and income for smallholder farmers in Cambodia, reducing farmers' exposure to variability in crop yields, and increasing monthly income for families. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases from existing and proposed agricultural practices with the introduction of solar pumps and improved water management and agrometeorological services. It will support the implementation of the Climate Smart Agriculture and Dryland Farming techniques together with improved agronomic practices like IPM.

The project will support the USAID-funded Water for Cambodia (W4C) project in its efforts to improve irrigation infrastructure in Cambodia through improved water information system and agriculture advisory services. Through combined investments, the project aims to increase agricultural productivity by 20% while adapting to climate-resilient future, enabling rural populations to adequately adapt to current and future climate impacts and increase their food security and livelihoods whilst also enabling the RGG to meet its climate targets.

### Key Indicators

CASAAR is expected to impact 562,757 people directly and 1,151,614 people indirectly (Annex 23). The project will contribute to increased agricultural productivity by promoting improved irrigation cropping and diversification patterns, improved access to water, enhanced water governance, climate-resilient agriculture practices, and the use of solar pumps. The combined impact of these investments will be to enhance water and food security. Improved irrigation infrastructure will allow for more efficient crop cultivation in the dry season, while the project that changing from rain-fed irrigation or using diaphragm irrigation infrastructure to proper systems will increase yields by 20%. This will lead to increased agricultural productivity and income increases. Water security will be enhanced as a result of modernizing the irrigation systems, making them more resilient to climate risks and reducing conveyance losses. The project will enhance water use efficiency through improved irrigation management practices in the dry season for the rice crop.

From this area, a second irrigated dry season rice crop can be established by using diaphragm irrigation infrastructure in the wet season for the rice crop. In the dry season, mainly homestead gardening including vegetables, chicken production and aquaculture will be promoted to increase food security and income generation opportunities for additional income in the dry season. The project will develop the required irrigation infrastructure to allow for this second dry season rice crop. The project will also promote the use of solar pumps that will be used to provide investments for flood control/irrigation for the entire gross project area of 40,000 hectares.

### Reduction in GHG Emissions

Reduction of GHG emissions from rice production will be achieved by shifting away from traditional flooded rice methods to improved water management with required drainage facilities diversifying the use of solar pumps and diaphragm irrigation infrastructure. This will reduce the need for fossil energy sources for pumping water from canals to fields instead of the diesel pumps currently used.

The project will also contribute to reducing GHG emissions by promoting improved land cultivation, improved soil management and replacement of diesel pumps with solar pumps for fertilizer application in rice production. The baseline data on the reduction of GHG emissions undertaken to estimate the reduction in GHG emissions and baseline data sources are described in Table 1A and additionally assumptions are outlined in each sub-section (Annex 22). Briefly, based on the project design, a total of 300 farmers will be selected from the target communities (representatives) and field surveys, including agronomy survey conducted during feasibility and engineering studies. The baseline data were collected through interviews with farmers with support from IFAD and AID. The key factors contributing to the decrease is the reduction in the area under traditional flooded rice production practices and a switch to solar and diaphragm irrigation.

The use of solar pumps as an alternative to diesel-powered pumps, which are financially out of reach for many vulnerable smallholder farmers. The method is fueled by fear for traditional pumping methods and lack of knowledge about how to operate solar pumps. Smallholder farmers see an opportunity to enhance their agricultural output and minimize losses through the use of solar pumps. Solar pumps can be used during both the dry season and wet cropping seasons, thereby increasing agricultural resilience. They also contribute to reducing environmental degradation by eliminating the need for diesel fuel and associated carbon dioxide emissions. This not only lowers energy costs for irrigation but also enhances crop resilience against climate change impacts such as droughts and floods. The use of solar pumps also creates an environment suitable for fish and water birds thereby generating global public goods. # D. Needs of recipient

## 146. Country Vulnerability
Due to a combination of political, geographic, and social factors, Cambodia is considered as vulnerable to climate change impacts, ranked 140th out of 181 countries in the 2020 Climate Risk Index (CRI). The country has a high vulnerability score and low readiness which accounts for the high-risk ranking compared to other countries. This is due to its small size, limited natural resources, heavy reliance on agriculture sector and low adaptive capacity (including limited financial, institutional and technological resources) as well as poor infrastructure development in many areas of the country. Cambodia has a high risk of natural disasters. Projected climate change trends indicate that Cambodia will experience an increase in temperature by 2050, with average temperatures increasing by nearly 10% by 2050. As a result, The Royal Government of Cambodia is committed to addressing the vulnerabilities of its population so that they are adapted and resilient to current and future climate shocks.

## 147. Water Resources
Water resources in Cambodia are in a state of flux as a result of major human development projects such as dams and irrigation infrastructure. Recent studies have shown that lack of modern irrigation infrastructure is holding back agricultural production in Cambodia, with the country’s water sector being ranked last out of 20 countries. The country is simultaneously highly dependent on the resources provided by the natural river flow regime and the flood regime. As a consequence, the country has been experiencing every extreme weather change. In a context where climate change is expected to exacerbate these changes, the area discussed in Cambodia’s National Conservation Zone (NCZ) is on maintaining the flows level necessary to sustain ecosystem services. It is hoped that this will be achieved through the development of an integrated water resources management plan which includes the necessary productivity of the ecosystem supporting Cambodia's inland fisheries would represent a major contribution towards food security and poverty reduction, as well as ensuring that the Cambodian population still dependent on natural water sources for domestic consumption, drought and flood mitigation, and other livelihoods. The Tonle Sap Lake is a unique and vital natural resource in Cambodia. The lake’s complex hydrological interactions with the Mekong River make it vulnerable to changes in the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 52

## Agriculture: Climate change will influence food production via direct and indirect effects on crop yields, water availability, soil quality, and pests and diseases. Direct effects include changes in precipitation patterns and temperatures. Indirect effects include impacts on water resource availability and seasonality, soil organic matter content, and the spread of invasive species. These impacts are expected to damage key staple crops such as rice, even at lower emissions levels (2°C above pre-industrial), with yields falling by 10–35% for wheat and maize yields respectively even if the Paris Climate Agreement is met and warming is limited to 1.5°C. Shifting rainfall patterns will also increase the risk of droughts in some regions, while increased precipitation may lead to flooding in others. The impacts on rice are expected to be particularly severe as it is a crop in Cambodia that is highly vulnerable to climate change.

### Poverty and Inequality: Poverty reduction in Cambodia has been rapid, yet climate change could reverse this progress by increasing poverty levels and potentially pushing people back into poverty. Climate change will affect the poorest groups in society. Poorer farmers and communities are less able to afford local water storage, irrigation infrastructure, and technologies for adaptation. The rate of poverty decline in Cambodia is expected to be lower than that of other countries with similar levels of development and inequality may grow. Though data is scarce; second, poverty declines are strongly correlated with increases in agricultural productivity.

### Financial Constraints: The updated NDC has estimated that the total funding required for all mitigation actions in the country is USD 8.5 billion. The FOLU, LULUCF, and energy sector actors will be key to ensuring that this funding is effectively mobilized.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

The project sites were selected by the RGSC given their resource endowments and the high poverty rates in the context of their SDG targets, especially given the moderate-to-high poverty incidence rates that prevail across the region. The project will focus on addressing the irrigation infrastructure, poverty status, food insecurity, vulnerability to climate change especially threat of high flood risks, and lack of access to finance for smallholder farmers, enterprises, farmer groups, MSMEs, SMEs, and cooperatives.

The targeting under the project will look on agricultural value chains, including smallholder farmers, enterprises, farmer groups, MSMEs, SMEs, and cooperatives. CAISAR is designed to build a cluster approach which works with these diverse target groups to build their capacity in order to improve their livelihoods and increase their income generation as outlined in the PIM (annex 21). MSMEs account for around 70% of employment in Cambodia and are an important source of income, especially among rural households. However, most MSMEs face several challenges, including concerns about financial access, technical services, market access, and lack of business skills. The project will address these specific constraints revealed that more than half of SMEs needed help with capacity development and marketing. In addition to the above, the project will also provide advice on their debt management and debt term, as well as advice on their sale and operational strategies.

The Public Private Partnership (PPP) model under CAISAR is designed to strengthen micro, small, and medium enterprises (MSMEs) in Cambodia through financing support for sustainable rural entrepreneurs.

## Gender and Women’s Empowerment

The majority of smallholder farmers in agriculture sector and 20% of the households are women-headed. Key factors that account for the differences between women's and men's access to resources include: lack of land ownership, limited access to inputs such as seeds and fertilizers, lack of access to assets and credit, treatment by formal institutions, which can constrain women’s opportunities, skewed social norms and practices, and gender-based violence.

Under various led Government Programmes such as WRM and the participatory irrigation management and development (PIMD) projects, women have gradually participated more in village and community level decision-making processes. However, despite their increasing participation in socio-cultural and physical constraints, only a few have been able to fully join farmer user committees and other similar structures that are responsible for planning and implementing agricultural activities. Compared with the past, women have become more active, committed and able to put forward their views on issues related to agriculture and rural development. However, women’s household chores still prevent them from doing so.

In addition, women in Cambodia are less likely than men to be involved in income-generating activities. In fact, only a few women have the time to join FWUCs and village and commune level committees. This is due to their heavy domestic responsibilities. Compared with men, the number of women in FWUCs (and also in commune councils) is low at around 20%. Most of the women who are involved in these structures are widows or single women who have lost their husbands and are now responsible for taking care of their children. Women are often elected as treasurers and accountants in the committees.

Women’s participation in decision-making and productive activities needs to be proactively enhanced through special quotas and more supportive measures. The majority of women-headed households in Cambodia are small-scale farmers who feed both crops and livestock. Women also bear the largest brunt of the losses that households incur due to climate change, as they are responsible for feeding their families and ensuring food security.

CAISAR is designed to target women and deal with some of these key constraints.


### Youth: Very few young people are involved in agriculture

Youth are a very important group. However, many rural youth are not engaged in agriculture because it has high risk and not very profitable. It is expected that gradual structural transformation will lead to some increase in the number of youth who engage in agricultural activities.

Project design recognizes this and places weight on creation of rural employment opportunities as an important element for increasing youth engagement in agriculture. A range of incentives and retention to youth in agricultural activities will be undertaken. CAISAR will through NCCOS, provide training and mentoring services to young people. The project will also make in CAISAR to target youth in activities which are of interest for them such as in the operation and maintenance of irrigation systems, water harvesting structures, and other related activities.

Youth will be trained as mechanics, drivers, processors and traders. Youth will be trained on post-harvest handling techniques. The project has developed a practitioner’s guide for assessing skills and training needs of youth based on a case study in Cambodia. This guide is being shared with SLO project regarding the Cambodian Strategy for Sustainable Agriculture (CSA). It will also be used to promote innovation, entrepreneurship and youth engagement in the agricultural sector as well as be used. RRII will be encouraged to use this guide in their work. The project will also provide support to the development of youth-farmed agricultural trainings which require improvement of training quality and availability of appropriate training content.

#### 15.4. Relevance to and Alignment with Existing Policies such as Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), National Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMAs), and National


---
**Page 61**

Adaptation Plans (NAPs): The Government also updated its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) in 2020: Cambodia's NDCs update process utilizes a bottom-up approach initiated by 16-line ministries. The effort was supported by the NDC Partnership and its Climate Action Environment, Agriculture, Water, Energy, and Health sectors. The implementation of these priority actions is being coordinated by the Department of Climate Change and Meteorology (DCCM). The NDC update process has improved in several important ways: First, coverage has increased to include climate change mitigation measures as well as adaptation measures. Second, it includes a wider range of key sub-sectors, such as energy efficiency. It includes a stronger set of adaptation actions. It pays particular attention to gender and vulnerable groups, in order to ensure that the adaptation and mitigation efforts are inclusive and equitable. The NDC update process is also designed to develop a solid framework for monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV), so that the country is able to track progress towards its climate change commitments. The Climate Change Fund (CCF) will directly support several NDC adaptation actions identified (e.g., Establish a national climate and flood warning system; Establish nationally standardized best-practice systems for irrigation; Establish a national climate information service; Support private sector investments in irrigation infrastructure and CIVS; Improve institutional capacity for river basin management, including the development of water resources plans; Support smallholder farmers and so on). It will indirectly support MRV efforts through building PMU capacity on the use of ex-ACT tool in project MICE (which was already used in the NDC development for EUJCI). GHG mitigation actions are also included under the following priority sectors: Agriculture, LULUCF; Energy; Transport; and Industry, while adaptation to climate change is addressed through the following priority sectors: Water Resources, Forests, Coastal zone. Cambodia initiated the NAP process in 2014 to identify and address key vulnerabilities in key sectors. The NAP was developed with technical assistance from the CCF and UNDP through GCF support. In a Second National Communication (SNC) to UNFCCC in 2015, Cambodia identified agriculture as the most vulnerable, followed by water resources, forests, coastal zones and health. The SNC also highlighted that climate change is a cross-cutting issue and used it to illustrate the geographic variation through layering variables such as socio-economic status, population density, land use/land cover, elevation, rainfall, temperature, etc. A national plan on irrigation management, climate information and early warning services, design of resilient infrastructure, and institutional support will enable Cambodia to develop its mitigation and adaptation strategies.

Existing National Climate Change Strategy: The country has in place a strong overarching policy structure for implementing its plans and strategies which include Cambodia’s Sustainable Development Strategic Plan (SDSP) 2014-2035, the Climate Change Action Plan (CCCAP), 2014-2023. Each relevant ministry has developed associated action plans (Climate Change Action Plans - CCAPs), such as Climate Change Action Plan for Rural Development Sector (2012-2020) and Climate Change Action Plan for Agriculture Sector (2012-2020). Work: CCP 2016-2035 was the first ever comprehensive national policy document responding to climate change. It is a strategic plan that sets out Cambodia’s priorities, strategies, and readiness for reducing the negative impacts of climate change. CAISAS is in keeping with the emphasis of the CCSP on building the climate change response of the country in the agriculture sector by 2035. The Climate Change Action Strategy for Agriculture Sector (CAISAS) 2012-2035, CAISAS will help to build mechanisms at the local and institutional level for green growth. ### Cost-Benefit Analysis of the Mitigation and Adaptation Outcomes: GCF is being requested to provide total financing of US$ 80 million for CASAIR. The programme is expected to benefit around 562,714 people in the project area over a period of 30 years. This represents an average cost per capita of GCF projects reported at USD 17 per capita. The per capita cost within GCF and other programmes generally vary considerably depending upon the level of support provided to the beneficiaries. CASAIR is expected to provide a net benefit of USD 254 million over its lifetime, which is much lower in cost while those which provide concrete support in terms of infrastructure investments are likely to be more expensive than CASAIR. The GCF projects that have received funding for infrastructure investments and thus its cost will be higher than the average GCF project. Based on the above analysis, it can be concluded that CASAIR would achieve a net present value balance of -2.3 USD per hectare per year. This would be achieved a unit cost of between USD 1.22 and USD 3 per metric tonnes. This is well below the benchmarks for many other GCF projects and the average cost per capita of USD 40.

### Economic and Financial Rate of Return: The incremental economic and financial analysis of the project, considering all costs, indicates promising economic returns over a 30-year evaluation period and social discount rate of at 8.7 percent and cost of capital of 11.36 percent. The estimated Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) is projected to be 25.49 percent while the Net Present Value (NPV) is projected at USD 143.6 million, with a benefit-cost ratio of 1.28 and a payback period of nearly 12.0 years. The sensitivity analysis shows that the project remains economically unprofitable if project benefits decrease by more than 20.7 percent or if costs increase beyond 59.1 percent in relation to the base case scenario, which is unlikely given the local economic context and macroeconomic trends. ### Potential target scenario (Description)

- Enhanced awareness and access to technologies that are adopted by neighbouring districts and at every level.
- Increased access to early warning systems which are used not just in the project area but also nationally through social media platforms.
- Increased presence of the private sector in the project area as a result of increased demand for adaptive practices and technologies.

### How the project/programme will contribute (Description)

The project will assist with training of 40,000 farmers on climate change adaptation risks and assist people with improved market access to sell their produce. The project will also provide extension services to over 170,000 people. The project will target 60% of the beneficiaries as women.

The project will enhance water use efficiency for an area of 32,056 ha out of a gross area of 48,900 ha in the dry season and wet season for the rice crop. The project will also provide training to over 170,000 farmers to allow for a second irrigated rice crop per year on an additional 32,056 ha (50% using AII-based resources). GCF resources will be used to purchase and install solar-powered irrigation systems and flood control structures for the entire project zone.

E.E. Projecting programme activities and deliverables

Activities Description Sub-activities Deliverables

Output 1.1 Increased farmer capacity in climate resilient agriculture.

Activity 1.1.1
Prepare a community-based action plan (AP) to transform agriculture with CR practices and technologies.
The AP will be prepared blending the knowledge of farmers, extension workers, and other stakeholders with the knowledge and skills of farmers. It will be developed through an interactive consultation facilitated by the project team. The AP will assess climate change and agriculture and establish a baseline for potential options for adaptation.

Community-based action plans (CBAPs) will be developed at the community level to implement them. The plan will set out crop choices and other adaptations based on the information and knowledge available from implementation of Output 1.3 as well as the improved climatic information made available from implementation of Output 1.3

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 78

## Activity 1.1.2: Preparing training materials to support implementation of the AP

This activity will develop training curricula and accompanying materials to be used to support farmers in adopting climate-resilient crop varieties, technologies, and practices as outlined in the action plans. These materials will include a combination of printed materials combined with charts, leaflets and other communication tools. The training material are available to farmers who usually have limited access to information on climate change adaptation. The training materials will be modified and updated based on field experiences and lessons learned. The revised curricula will be mainstreamed in the MAFF extension services. The revised curricula will also include key learning and training materials for the adoption of climate-resilient crop varieties, technologies and practices in the country.

Training materials will depend on rice (wet and dry season rice), vegetables, groundnuts, maize, sorghum, millet, wheat, and other crops as identified in the AP. The revised curricula will be prepared for the whole agricultural calendar covering from early planting to first harvest.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.1: Compile and review of existing training materials (Cereals and Best practices)

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.2: Prepare training materials for Rice, non-rice crops, poultry and livestock

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.3: Organize the revised curricula to run in the FFS (Activity 1.2.3)

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.4: Mainstream the revised curricula to be used at national level

## Activity 1.1.3: Conduct training of trainers to create a pool of experts to propagate the CR technologies and practices.

This activity will train a pool of expertise to propagate the CR technologies and practices in the country. The training will use early warning and climate-informed crop management, Extension department of MAFF, interested NGOs, Extension department of MAFF, extension services of other institutions and private entities. Altogether 200 Master Trainers (MTs) will be trained. The training will be organized by the MTs who will act as trainers in their respective provinces. ## Activity 1.1.6: Community-based monitoring and evaluation (CBME) of the implementation

The gross root level CBME has particular importance in the context of climate change adaptation, as it provides a means to monitor agricultural producers and supporting interventions on an ongoing basis but with consistency. Weather patterns and their effects are highly variable across space and considerable uncertainty regarding what the actual impact will be from climate change in a given agricultural system.

A set of community-based performance indicators has been developed together with the farming communities and other relevant stakeholders, which will monitor and evaluate the outcomes of the interventions based on the adoption of the AP. These include: number of farmers adopting new technologies, rate of female farmers engaging in income generating activities introduced etc. These indicators will be monitored at the commune level and information can be used in the project's MRV framework as well as in the Action Monitoring systems at national level.

## Output 1.4 Increased resilience of farm road infrastructure to climate change

### Activity 1.4.1 Identification, selection and initial planning

The initial planning shall be carried out in accordance with the principles established by the communities and other relevant stakeholders. The consultant firm (or experts engaged) will help the communities identify the most critical and vulnerable roads that need improvement for future road design. Lessons and experiences from the Adaptation and Resilience Framework (ABRF 2018) prepared under the Road through Climate Change project shall be used in the community engagement process to ensure that the communities receive relevant information on traffic numbers and types, expected increase in rainfall or drought resulting from climate change, existing road conditions, and future needs for maintenance. The estimated costs and resources requirements will also be taken into account.

#### 14.1.1 Organize consultative workshop with local government and stakeholders

Locally developed plan for farm road improvement

* **14.1.2 Conduct Walk through along roads**
* **14.1.3 Assess current and future traffic volumes**

#### 14.1.4 Finalize priorities areas of improvements based on above

### Activity 1.4.2 Technical survey, design considerations preparation and cost estimation

Survey and designs shall be carried out in accordance with the design principles established by the communities and other relevant stakeholders available in the country and available internationally. The designs will integrate climate resilience features to ensure that the roads are safe for future use under expected conditions. The following design approaches including for climate proofing:

* **Embankments safe from expected increased rainfall intensity**
* **Drainage provisions capable enough to handle the increase of the expected intense flooding**
* **Pavement materials able to adjust both increased heat in line with the increase in temperature**

#### 14.2.1 Conduct technical survey of road alignment and other structures

Design, cost estimations and bidding documents

#### Activity 1.4.2
Improving 90 kilometers of farm roads

The farm roads will be improved based on the results of the road condition assessment earlier. In addition, bridges and culverts will be constructed to mitigate adverse hydrological results from current road construction. The GCF and MRCOS will be responsible for producing the technical design documents and tendering process for the required bidding documents. The roads will be designed in a way that is sustainable, respecting the current land use pattern with sufficient consideration of historical and cultural sites. While the construction of the road will be carried out by suitable local contractors, it will be subject to supervision and quality assurance oversight of the road after completion.

#### Activity 1.4.4
Handling over of the completed roads

The completed roads will be handed over to MRCOS. The MRCOS will commit to the operation and maintenance of these roads through a sustainable road maintenance program. This program will include regular inspections, repairs, and maintenance activities. The costs of the annual O&M will be estimated at 10% of the total project cost. Local communities will be engaged in monitoring and reporting on the condition of the roads and will be trained for the purpose by the contractors.

### Output 2.4 Water information system (CWEWS and SCADA) established and operational in all three sub-projects

#### Activity 2.4.1 Preparing all processes for establishment of the SCADA and Water information system
One of the most important problems facing the agricultural sector: irrigation is inefficient, causing wastage of large quantities of water. This problem can be effectively addressed by taking advantage of real-time information on actual water use and providing a possibility to respond to fluctuations more accurately.

This is an emerging approach in the management of irrigation systems with emphasis on the development of basic low-cost solutions using the CWEWS and SCADA system, which will allow for better control over irrigation to rationalize and streamline the process. This technology has been community adapted by the irrigation schemes and can be used for modernization of old manually gated systems.

The development of irrigation systems strongly contributes towards socio-economic development, but there are still many general problems associated with these systems. For example, although irrigation systems play an important role, there is a lack of awareness among farmers about training and higher education for human

### Activity 3.2.1: Strengthening of NCODS at the national level
The National Committee for Sub-National Development Strategies (NCODS) in Cambodia plays a crucial role in promoting decentralization and deconcentration of services, enabling local governments to effectively carry out their functions. The strengthening of NCODS is seen as an important element aimed at enhancing the skills and knowledge of provincial national government officials.

### Activity 3.2.2: Strengthening of the provincial teams
NCODS and provincial staff have the responsibility for implementing policies and activities aimed at promoting sustainable development, gender equality, and poverty reduction. However, their activities and capacity are often hampered by weak links and ineffective due to a lack of coordination mechanisms between different levels of government.

E.F. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

163 Reporting from MOWRAM to GCF: Programme-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken to meet the information needs of the key financing and implementing agencies and will be in compliance with IFAD, GCF, AIB and RGG policies. As the main implementing agency, MOWRAM is responsible for developing a Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) plan and reporting system to analyze and quantify the causal and attributable change of the project’s outputs and outcomes. The monitoring and evaluation framework will be developed by MOWRAM will be responsible for coordinating and preparing a consolidated report for submission to all key stakeholders through the Project Steering Committee.

170 Monitoring and Evaluation Framework/Plan: The Logical Framework matrix, as elaborated in Part Four, will serve as the basis of the M&E plan and monitoring framework of the project. The M&E plan also include a set of indicators that can be measured and monitored over time to track progress towards achieving the project’s outputs and outcomes, including activities, outputs and outcomes. The monitoring of CASAR will involve (i) process monitoring; (ii) monitoring of the implementation of Resettlement Plan; (iii) monitoring progress in construction of infrastructure; (iv) monitoring of the implementation of the Project Implementation Plan; (v) gender and social dimension monitoring; (vi) monitoring of the implementation of capacity building activities; and (vii) monitoring of compliance with loan covenants. Legal forms/matrices shall be developed for each of these aspects.

171 Key Indicators: Project performance monitoring and evaluation will aim to ascertain the success of the project in achieving the intended outputs and outcomes through their respective performance indicators. The indicators will also help determine the extent to which the project is meeting its expectations and decide what actions ought to be taken to redress any shortfalls or deviations from expected results, considering additional digital tools to better monitor specific issues arising under the Project and ensure such digital tools will be subject to Cambodia’s data protection and privacy laws regulations.

172 Some of the key indicators for CASAR will include training and adoptances rates in crop diversification; number of farmers trained in water management practices; performance of the agricultural extension forums and multi-stakeholder forums and the volume of purchase contracts negotiated; households reached through early warning systems and its reported benefits. Under component 2 the project will monitor the performance of the irrigation system, including canal and drainage systems, solar pump performance, durability and effectiveness of concrete irrigation structures, water quality, water use efficiency, water availability, improvement of transport links alongside canals, improvement of drought and extreme flood resilience, and impact on gender equality, etc. The performance of FWUCs will also be monitored through the number of farmers trained in water management practices; recovery of fees for irrigation system maintenance. The project monitoring system will track the growth in area under improved water use efficiency (AWE) and area under improved water use diversity (AWD) in rice production. The monitoring system will use data-driven technologies such as Smart SCADA technology to monitor the system for leaks, food damages, and other issues. Regular surveys and assessments will also be conducted to gather feedback from farmer water users.

#### Types of Reports:
The M&E Specialist in the Programme Management Unit will coordinate and consolidate all reports from different implementing partners including a M&E Specialist in the NCDSS, GCF/IFAD teams, and other relevant stakeholders. The consolidated report will be provided to the GCF Secretariat. GCF will be provided the following reports: (i) an inception report; (ii) Annual Performance Report (APR); (iii) Mid-term review report; (iv) Final evaluation report.

With the support of the PMIC, will provide six-monthly updates/reports on the project's implementation and performance which will be consolidated to provide APRs. The status of achievements against the project outputs will be provided in the six-monthly updates. The project outputs will be provided in the six-monthly updates. AII/BG/GCF/IFAD teams will coordinate with the PMIC to ensure that the GCF Secretariat receives these reports on time.

The APRs will be reviewed and approved by the GCF Board at its bi-annual meetings or at six-monthly or annual review missions as required. The corrective actions will be agreed with the executing entity and recorded and subsequently implemented by the EE and monitored by AII/BG/GCF/IFAD teams.

#### CAISAR Safeguards and Gender Action Plan reporting:
The project places particular importance on ensuring the monitoring of differential impacts by gender especially monitoring components relevant to women and more vulnerable populations. Furthermore, the monitoring system will be designed in a way that it is consistent with the GCF’s safeguarding framework to safeguard their interests. This will ensure a comprehensive and holistic monitoring system.

#### Interim and final evaluations:
FAD has a well-structured system for undertaking annual supervision missions, a mid-term review and a project completion report. The project will undertake surveys at mid-term and at completion to assess the performance of the project, draw important lessons from these reviews and use them in future projects. The GCF Secretariat will review these survey reports. The interim or mid-term survey will incorporate key aspects of project performance and outputs. At project completion, a final evaluation will be undertaken to assess overall performance and achievement of the outputs and outcomes.

At project completion, a final evaluation will be undertaken to assess the overall performance and achievement of the outputs and outcomes. The mid-term and final evaluation will compare project performance against the project’s objectives and expected results. The evaluation will also assess the overall performance and the paradigm shifts outlined in the project log-frame and the indicators used for monitoring these shifts. The evaluation will also assess how well the project has contributed to the GCF's higher-level goal of achieving a paradigm shift in adaptation to climate change in the project districts.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Factor 1: Delay of Approval by GCF Board

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

176. There is a risk related to the timing for approval of CAISAR by the GCF Board which is expected to be undertaken in 2025. Delay in this approval could impact the plans of MOWRAM, the financing schedule of IFAD and AIBB and impose additional costs and impact the financial returns from the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

177. The NOA, IFAD and AIBB will try to expedite the process of design and approval to ensure that the FFP is submitted according to the required modules and will endeavour to address all comments received from GCF as efficiently as possible.


## Selected Risk Factor 2: Technical & Operational Capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

178. The institutional assessment report prepared by PPIC and WAPCOS, December 2022 noted that the weak and insufficient capacity of human resource at all levels of the irrigation management system in Cambodia is a major challenge to the effective implementation of CAISAR. The lack of trained professionals in water resources and irrigation management in Cambodia is a key issue. The capacity building for MOWRAM and PDWRAM staff will be critical to ensure that they will need enhanced capacity for improved water management and governance to develop a comprehensive water management plan that includes equitable water distribution and allocation and promote the efficient use of water resources, as well as to improve their ability to optimize water use.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

179. Based on the institutional assessments report of the Water Resources Management Sector Development Programme (WRMSDP), the training for MOWRAM and PDWRAM will include all personnel in the organization. The training will be conducted by PPIC and WAPCOS. CAISAR will also include an institutional strengthening component for MOWRAM. For improved implementation of CAISAR, the following components are proposed: i) Capacity Building for Project Implementation Plans; ii) FWUC Formation and Strengthening; iii) Gender Awareness and Gender Mainstreaming; iv) Water Resources Management Plan Development; v) Climate Change and Meteorological Equipment; vi) Environmental Awareness; vii) Construction Management and Supervision and viii) On-Farm Water Management and Operation and Maintenance.

## Category: Technical and operational | Probability: Medium | Impact: Medium

### Description:
180. Inadequate policies and strategies to mainstream climate change agendas. While Cambodia has made impressive progress in formulation of climate change strategies and policies, mainstreaming is lagging behind.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):
181. The policy efforts indicate that the public sector is aware of climate risks and the importance of mainstreaming climate agenda and is motivated to address those issues. The project aims to assist the government in strengthening its capacity for mainstreaming climate change considerations into national development planning processes, including through the implementation of the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and NDCs. To support this objective, several measures have been proposed—through activities under Component 3—to improve NCDD’s capacity to mainstream climate change considerations into sectoral strategies and plans (e.g., agriculture projects) and implement effective policies and programs at sub-national level; improve technical capacities of provincial teams on sustainable agriculture and climate change adaptation, etc.

## Selected Risk Area: 4. Weak coordination among institutions

### Category: Technical and operational | Probability: High | Impact: High

#### Description:
182. Lack of coordination at the ministerial level is a common issue in the country and also impacts the working between the institutions of the environment and agriculture sector, and between national and sub-national levels. This could slow down the project process with implications for cost and efficiency.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):
183. A robust coordination mechanism has been proposed establishing a Project Steering Committee. The PSC will have members from all key ministries involved in project implementation. The PSC will meet at least once every six months to discuss progress, challenges, and solutions. In addition, there is a clear division of responsibilities between the NCDD and the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF) and NCCD. Initial orientation sessions, clear terms of reference and regular meetings between implementing partners at the provincial level will further help to reduce these risks.

### Category: Technical and operational | Probability: Medium | Impact: Medium

#### Description:
184. A principal reason for delays of projects during implementation is the delay in establishing procurement arrangements, prolonged procurement procedures or failure to sign contracts for delivery of the goods and services.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):
185. CAISSAR will establish a dedicated team of national and international procurement experts to ensure procurement is timely and is approved as efficiently as possible (IFAD, ADB and

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Social and Environmental

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description
186. Several social and environmental risks have been identified in the ESMP (Annex 6). These risks can negatively impact the project environment and the livelihoods of local population in various ways. There is a risk of insufficient consultation / engagement with stakeholders on key project environmental and social issues, which could result in negative impacts on the project's ability to engage with key stakeholders, which may eventually undermine project delivery and sustainability of project activities. The risks are likely to be most significant for the next 5 years and will continue over the long term. The target populations and ecosystems are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
187. A SECAP mission was deployed to identify the potential social and environmental risks prior to project design which identified the frameworks and principles that would be adopted in case of any major adverse impact on the environment or local communities. The project's ESMP (Annex 6) lays out the overall strategies to mitigate or minimize any harmful social and environmental impacts, including a detailed risk management plan (Annex 7). The plan (Annex 7) specifies specific measures to ensure that project activities reflect their interests and needs in an equitable manner. The SECAP mission also identified the key stakeholders for the project and provided recommendations on how to engage with them. To avoid the risk that effective grievance channels are not established, a multi-level grievance mechanism (GRM) plan has been prepared. It includes plans for a multi-level GRM to be established and information made available to project stakeholders and affected persons through multiple channels. The project will not involve any significant land acquisition or resettlement activities. However, the project may have economic assets or infrastructure due to climate variability / hazards or result in ecosystem degradation. To avoid this risk, the project will focus on investments that are resilient to climate change by mitigating the negative impact of extreme climate events (reduced losses) and through improved livelihoods and resilience. The project will also ensure that any potential adverse exposure will continue to inform planning processes during implementation, ensuring reduced vulnerability of local communities. The project will also ensure that all activities are fully contextualized into ESMP at project inception to implement targeted mitigation actions (e.g., occupational health and safety risks associated with land acquisition, integrated livelihoods and resilience).

192\. Under IFAD's Anti-Money Laundering and Countering the Financing of Terrorism (AML/CFT) Policy, IFAD has developed a software called Open Compliance (https://ifad.fco.org). The software conducts screening of IFAD's counterparties against sanctions lists maintained by the UN Security Council. The software also contains certain enforcement results and adverse media, to assist with Third Party Diligence (TDD) checks. The software is designed for use in identifying potential money laundering or terrorist financing risks associated with counterparties IFAD is seeking to engage with such as individual consultants, vendors, suppliers private sector and other parties. The software has been integrated into IFAD's Online Procurement System (OPS) and will be used to screen all financial transactions related to procurement activities entered in OPEN by the borrower and covering any applicable exclusion and restrictive measures lists, adverse enforcements and assessment and recommendation based

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 193 Risk Category Assignment and Rationale
The proposed environmental and social risk assessment is based on a comprehensive screening and assessment of E&S risks and impacts that may arise from proposed project activities. It notes that risk assessment takes into account the following: (i) nature, scope and scale of proposed project activities; (ii) potential direct, indirect, and cumulative impacts; (iii) pathways through direct, indirect, and potential cumulative impacts; (iv) vulnerability of potential project affected people (both intended beneficiaries and adversely affected groups) as to project impacts; (v) sensitivity of the environment and social systems in the project sites; and (vi) socioeconomic and environmental settings in the project sites that bear inherent E&S risks. The risk assessment is based on the information available at this stage, including the baseline data on the achievement of the intended project results. It notes that the risk assessment considers the effects of the proposed project activities as well as any future additional mitigation measures and does not assume any future additional mitigation measures that are outside of the measures proposed in the project.

### 194 Environmental and Social Assessment Instruments
To manage the identified E&S risks and impacts effectively, a number of E&S instruments have been prepared in line with the requirements of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) policies. These include: (i) an Environmental, Social, Climate Management Framework (ESCMF), Land Acquisition and Resettlement Action Plan (LARAP); (ii) detailed assessment tools were also conducted at project level, including such as an Environmental and Social Risk Assessment Tool (ESRAT); (iii) at the sub-scheme level, three Environmental, Social, Climate Management Plans have been prepared; (iv) a series of environmental and social risk screening instruments have been developed based on a series of activities: (i) risk screening based on the proposed project activities against the GCF’s Environmental, and Climate Assessment Protocol (ECP); (ii) risk assessment under the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF’s) Environmental & Social Safeguard Policy; (iii) risk assessment through extensive consultations with relevant stakeholders at provincial, district, commune and village levels; (iv) development of mitigation measures for identified E&S risks and impacts in the project area based on the field survey and stakeholder consultation for project purposes); and secondary data that were obtained from the identification of E&S risks and impacts in similar projects in the region and other parts of the country.

### 195 Main Outcomes ESCMF and Mitigation Measures
The E&S instruments highlight environmental and social risks and impacts under project stages: (i) before construction; (ii) during construction, including physical and non-physical impacts from physical activities such as blasting, earthmoving, and other construction activities that are as manageable as such; (iii) during construction process, potential impacts on biodiversity is envisaged, particularly in the form of direct and indirect impacts (e.g. habitat destruction, loss of species exposed to impacts caused during construction, including physical and non-physical impacts from physical activities such as blasting, earthmoving, and other construction activities that are as manageable as such); (iv) during project completion, potential impacts on biodiversity is envisaged in the form of direct and indirect impacts (e.g. hitting, hunting for trading and/or consumption), and in the form of cumulative impacts (over years

# Social risks and impacts were also identified by project stage and are mostly associated with activities under component 2.

## Before construction, permanent and temporary land acquisition is anticipated to cause social risks such as displacement of local communities for improved water intake (for feeder canals), and for improved water distribution (for secondary and tertiary canals). The risk of loss of livelihoods due to land acquisition is also anticipated. Permanent land acquisition - through exploring engineering design options. So, minor permanent land acquisition is anticipated to cause temporary economic displacement for households in the project area. Some permanent land (which is small scale) is anticipated to cause temporary economic displacement for households in the project area. The risk of loss of livelihoods due to land acquisition is also feasible). During construction, some risks are anticipated, including risks of disease transmission during construction activities and risks associated with the use of chemicals.

### Exploitation and Abuse: Sexual Harassment is identified - due to presence of contractors' workers in the locality. The contractor's workers are also exposed to the risk related to occupational health and safety (OHS) issues such as exposure to pesticides, lack of adequate treatment on the part of workers hired locally is envisaged.

Under component 1, project activities that could lead to social risks include: a) temporary land acquisition for feeder canals; b) construction of feeder canals; c) construction of secondary and tertiary canals. These activities may give rise to risk of elite capture and unequal water distribution during operation project stage since the canal will be constructed in close proximity to existing canals, which are owned by those who expose themselves during pesticide spraying.

### Mitigation measures for social risks include: a) workers who foresee impacts are isolated through technical engineering design options. When avoidance is not possible, risk and impacts are managed through appropriate mitigation measures such as compensation, resettlement, etc., if residual impacts remain (See ESCA for details). Mitigation measures are proposed in ESCIA and ESCAMPA for the three sub-basins of Ou Ta Pong, Keproa, Timpem & Yutabae. The SEP is subject to review by the project team and stakeholders during the environmental impact consultation process during project implementation to receive timely feedback from those who have been consulted.

Redress Mechanism (GRM) in place to enable affected people to raise their concerns and complaints directly with project owner - through project's grievance procedures that are easily accessible and used.

## Due Diligence for Financial Intermediation - The ESCMP will be implemented with sub-contractors appointed by the Provincial Project Implementation Units (PUIs). Contractors will adhere to relevant financial management rules and regulations. PUIs will ensure that contractors receive support will be provided to MOIVRAM PMJ and PDWRAM to strengthen their capacity to explore, design, implement and monitor projects. The ESCMP will also cover (i) overall Project Management; (ii) establishment of FWUCs and (iii) capacity building for project implementation. A team of technical experts and financial management national consultants will be engaged to fit and build capacity of MOIVRAM PMJ and PDWRAM.

### Capacity and Compliance Monitoring - Capacity building is prioritized, and training is planned for the following topics: i) Project Orientation and Presentation of Project Plans; ii) FWUC Formation and Strengthening; iii) Establishment of Water Users' Associations (WUA); iv) Training on Hydro and Meteorological Data and O&M for Meteorological Equipment; v) Environmental Awareness; vi) Construction Management and supervision and (vi) On-Farm Water Management and Operation and Maintenance.

G.2\. Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

203\. Gender assessment - A gender assessment has been conducted in accordance with the procedures outlined in the GEF Gender Policy. The assessment examines the status quo of gender dimensions in agriculture and livelihood activities in the project area. It aims to: (i) identify existing constraints and opportunities for women and men in relation to their livelihoods through project investment; (ii) assess vulnerability of project population, with a focus on vulnerabilities and capacities of women and men groups; (iii) propose measures to engage them more effectively in project planning and decision-making processes; (iv) propose a gender action plan that ensures transparency, non-discriminatory access to resources, and equal participation for women and men stakeholders during project implementation. The gender assessment was carried out based on the results of focus group discussions with 100 households, key informant interviews with 25 stakeholders during project preparation.

The process also made use of a household survey which included 630 households for quantitative analysis. The findings were validated through a validation workshop held in December 2022 and reviewed in the second half of 2023. Feedback was obtained on constraints and opportunities for promoting gender equality, as well as on measures to ensure that women and men have equal access to the resilience of target group in addressing the climate change impact and propose priority interventions. The findings were validated through a validation workshop and stakeholder consultations were conducted during the preparation of the funding proposal.

204\. Key findings of the Gender Assessment - The findings confirmed that in rice production, which is the main livelihood activity for most households (95%), women are involved in all stages except in production stages where labor intensity is high (e.g. land preparation and harvesting). The more ambitious farmers are involved in post-harvest activities such as processing and marketing of produce. Farming households use machinery for land preparation (60%) and harvesting (70%). Women are involved mostly in unique household chores and small-scale animal husbandry. They also play a key role in decision-making by providing inputs on the type of seeds to be planted, crop trading. It is estimated that male workers spend on average, 59.4% of their time on farming while female workers spend only 30%. Women are involved mostly in unique household chores and small-scale animal husbandry. They also play a key role in decision-making by providing inputs on the type of seeds to be planted, crop trading.

205\. Addressing gender issues is essential in promoting and advancing the role and economic, social, political, legal and cultural status of women. The need to address this concern has been


---
**Page 107**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 101

increasingly acknowledged by the government of Cambodia since it would help to improve and sustain not only farm economy but also national economic development and growth. Under IWRM and Participatory Irrigation Management and Development (PIMD) policies, women are being encouraged to gradually participate more in village irrigation management and development issues. However, due to limited capacity and socio-cultural and physical constraints, only a few have been able to participate effectively in the planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of reduction activities. The study found that people prefer to have more women in management and development roles (e.g., as members of village irrigation committees or water users' councils) because women understand their issues and can solve some critical issues (water use conflicts), which are often difficult for men to resolve. Women also tend to be more cooperative than men. Rural women have notably participated in meetings or training on women's rights, saving groups and agriculture. They are actively involved in their communities. Furthermore, they are more likely to participate in community activities because of the support of their families or communities. Compared with the past, women have become more active, committed, and assertive in their roles as water users and managers. This is especially true since women have many household chores to perform. Because of this, only a few women have the time to participate fully in irrigation management activities such as planning, monitoring, and irrigation management or in disaster risk reduction. At the same time, even though they are committed, women's participation has been limited because of their socio-cultural roles which affected the ability of women to join in public affairs. CAIAR will deal with some of these issues and set up a FIVUC (Feminist Irrigation Users' Council) as part of the gender strategy.

## Proposed Project Intervention: A gender assessment and a Gender Action Plan (Annex B) has been developed for CASAIR. Key activities to support the implementation of the project's GAP include training and capacity building, developing diagnostic tools and approaches, promoting the use of tools and approaches to promote normative and behavioral changes in gender dynamics, establishing a network of women water users' groups, establishing Water Users Centers for delivering training to women; Developing gender-sensitive diagnostic tools and manuals, conducting analysis and other diagnostic tools. Since the role of women is not produced is limited therefore projects should be encouraged to diversify their income sources and increase their household income by growing vegetables, raising poultry and aquaculture. A gender strategy will be developed as part of the project's GAP. The strategy will address the value chains of interests they are interested beneficiaries will be trained and facilitated to enter into a business plan that will help them to generate more income and contribute to the FIVUC.

### Encouraging Women’s Role in Irrigation Management and Development (in the Context of Climate Change)

Women's participation is critical for successful irrigation management. Under PIMD policies, women will be encouraged to gradually participate more in village irrigation management and development issues. CAIAR has established a quota for women's participation in the FIVUC.

### Strengthening Women’s Capacity in Adapting to Climate Change

In addition to the above mentioned activities, women will also be helped to address the potential impact of climate change which could include: a) adoption of water-saving alternatives (e.g., drip irrigation); b) adoption of drought-resistant and drought-tolerant; c) adopt sustainable intensification packages that reduce seed rate, fertilizer and pesticide use to save agricultural input costs and increase crop yield. Women will be encouraged to participate in the development of these technologies as well as support adoption of climate-resilient technologies that also respond to their needs and preferences.

### G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 207. Procurement Arrangements:
The procurement procedures to be followed by the CASAM project are guided by the IFAD Procurement Manual. This manual is based on the results of meetings during the preparation of the project concept and the funding proposal.198 Public procurement in Cambodia is governed by the Law on Public Procurement, 2007, as amended, and the Government Regulations Governing Public Procurement. For donor-funded projects, the Standard Procedures for Procurement of All Externally Financed Projects and Programs (SOP) was produced in June 2007. The SOP document is based on the Law on Public Procurement, 2007, as amended, and the Government Regulations Governing Public Procurement. The Standard Procedure for Procurement of Donor-Funded Projects (SPPDP) took into consideration the revised procurement policies of donors such as ADB and IFAD. The General Directorate of Public Procurement (GDPP) is responsible for administration of the Law on Public Procurement. The GDPP oversees the implementation of public procurement in Cambodia, while the General Department of International Cooperation and Debt Management (GIDICM) oversees the externally financed projects.

#### 208. A procurement risk assessment for CASAM was conducted according to the IFAD Procurement Manual. This analysis shows that the overall inherent procurement risk is moderate. The main project procurement risks include the following: (i) The absence of national and international standards, guidelines, and best practices in terms of values and rules as the General Department of Public Procurement; (ii) Governance risks including lack of transparency and accountability in public procurement processes due to limited knowledge of IAFD and AIIB procurement and safeguard policies and requirements.

#### 209. Mitigation measures for project procurement risks include the following: (i) Upload information on procurement activities, including contracts and agreements, on the IFAD website or on IAkiproject websites; (ii) Using IFAD's Online Procurement End-to-End system (OPEN) and IAFD Contract Monitoring Tool for monitoring project procurement outcomes; (iii) Request

## Procurement Using AIIIB/GCF/IFAF funds will follow the Standard Operating Procedures on Procurement (SOP) for IFAD, GCF and AIIIB as well as other relevant policies and guidelines such are consistent with the IFAD Project Procurement Guidelines, and AIIIB's Procurement Policy and AIIIB’s Interim Operational Directive on Procurement Instructions for Recipients: Procurement of Goods and Services.

### NCDCDS PIU will be directly responsible for project procurement activities of Component 1 and will have a procurement officer. The NCDCDS PIU will also oversee the procurement activities of Component 2 and Output 3.1 under component 3. The NCDCDS PIU will have a procurement officer, a procurement assistant and a procurement specialist. MOIRWAM will be responsible for project procurement activities in component 4.

#### A Contract Project Procurement Arrangement Letter (PALL) will be issued to establish the detailed procurement requirements and thresholds for procurement methods and IFAD prior review of all contracts. The NCDCDS PIU will prepare an overall project implementation plan at the project design stage, and subsequently the annual procurement plans in consistency with the AWPBS shall be prepared and a PID no objection obtained before proceeding with any procurement activity. The Project Implementation Plan (PIP) and Open Procurement Evaluation System (OPEN) will be used for preparation and monitoring implementation progress of project procurement activities. All contracts will be registered on the IFAD Contract Management Tool (CMT). Data and information of all contracts and related payments must be listed and updated in the CMT and the IFAD Contract Management Tool (CMT). The register of contracts must be kept up to date on a quarterly basis.

### Financial Management: MOIRWAM and NCDCDS PIU shall follow the Royal Decree of Cambodian Government No. 12/PM dated 05 June 2013, as well as other guidance on financial management; each unit shall also develop a Financial Management Manual for the specific project. The Financial Management Manual will be prepared in accordance with computerized accounting software to be used as the main tool for bookkeeping, budget monitoring and reporting. The NCDCDS PIU will submit the AWPBS quarterly financial reports for consolidation and submission to IFAD and AIIIB.

#### The key financial management risks are (i) Potential low capacity of financial management staff at project level; (ii) Lack of adequate training in financial management; (iii) Previous external audit reports did not meet IFAD compliance standards, and (iv) Complicated distribution modalities. To mitigate these risks, the NCDCDS PIU will include specific measures to deal with these risks including the inclusion in the design the following measures to mitigate the risk of low capacity: training for financial management staff on relevant educational qualifications, development of a Project Implementation Manual (PIM) and a Financial Management Manual (FMM), as well as guidelines for filing supporting documents and maintaining accounting records for all implementing partners, customization of accounting software to facilitate accurate record keeping and submission of quarterly financial reports to IFAD and AIIIB. The NCDCDS PIU will also ensure that the project team is adequately qualified and staff seconded to work with the government with adequate qualifications and experience for the FM team; (ii) AWPBS following GCF budget format, (iii) report contract disbursement modality to request funds from GCF and IFAD sources, (iv) MOIRWAM SOP FMM for financial accounting and reporting, financial reporting and monitoring accounting software for financial accounting and reporting.
