## Staffing: MOWRAM is the Lead Project Agency and NCCD is an implementing partner; however, both agencies already have a finance unit with experience in international donor funded projects that can support CAISAR. PMU and PPU will be set up for MOWRAM and NCCD with a combined team of 10 staff members to ensure the effective implementation of the project. The project's financial management to ensure the effective FM function and internal control are maintained by the Finance Unit of MOWRAM and NCCD. The Finance Unit of MOWRAM and NCCD will have relevant experience working on other internationally funded projects. The contract Finance staff will be hired to support project FM functions daily including: a) Ensuring sufficient supporting resources for the project, b) Maintaining the financial records in accordance with software; c) Providing periodical and financial reports to the management; d) Submitting of IFR and VAT returns on time; e) Preparing the budget and submitting it to IFAD. The contract Finance staff will be recruited with relevant experience and qualifications. FM functions will have clear roles and responsibilities to ensure the segregation of duty and payment authorization in accordance with SOP and PIM.

## Planning and budgeting: The project budget is separate from the government budget; however, it will be submitted as part of the national budget for approval by the National Assembly as described in the SOP. And the AWPP will also include the projection of counterpart contributions. AWPP is subject to approval by the IFAD Country Director. The AWPP will be prepared by PPU and submitted to MOWRAM PMU. PMU will consult the AWPP and submit it to IFAD for NOL. The AWPP and procurement plan will be prepared in IPAD format with details and approved by the National Assembly. The project budget will be maintained on the software. PM/PU/PPU will ensure to keep track of the progress reports against the budget and provide regular updates to the management.

## Internal control: According to the PIFEA for Cambodia, the effectiveness of internal audits was rated C. PMU and PPU will set up or set-up from the government system and will follow the internal control procedures as described in the SOP. The implementation of internal controls is deemed to be effective for internationally funded projects. The SOP articulates the responsibilities and essential elements of internal control including transaction processing, accounting, reconciling accounts, access controls, PIM, and Financial Management Manual will outline key FM arrangements and controls. The Internal Audit Department of MOWRAM and NCCD will perform an annual internal audit to ensure compliance with the internal control procedures; the outcome of the internal audit will be shared with IFAD.

## Accounting systems, policies, procedures, and financial reporting: PMU and PPU accounting procedures will follow Standard Operating Procedures (SOP) for internationally funded projects. Accounts are required to be maintained in electronic format as per the SOP (on a daily basis), which is based on the International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS). Standard accounting software shall be used by PM/PU/PPU and will be compatible with IFAD and other donor agencies. The accounting software will be set up to capture all payment transactions, in addition, project components will be set up in the accounting software to ensure that all financial transactions are captured in the software to ensure financial reports and budget reports produced in accordance with the SOP. PMU/PPU will ensure that all financial records are maintained as per the records are outlined in the SOP. All accounting records are required to be maintained in electronic format for at least 10 years from the date of completion of the project. The quarterly consolidation of financial reports from NCCD-PMU for each quarter, within 30 days; NCCD PMU will send to IFAD for approval and submission of the agreed forms that can be consolidated and submitted to IFAD for justification of advance or requesting new advances.

The CGI will maintain a copy of all documents containing sensitive or confidential information and will provide a redacted copy for disclosure on the CGI website.

Proposal name: Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CAISAR) in Cambodia

Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development

Project title: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project targets vulnerable provinces like Kampot, Kampong Speu, Kampong Chhnang, Kandal and Pursat – where agricultural livelihoods are most dependent on rainfed agriculture but face water scarcity. The selection of these areas reflects robust climate change vulnerability assessments and socio-economic targeting, including a focus on women-headed households and areas with high poverty levels. | The project’s success heavily depends on the capacity and coordination of multiple domestic agencies such as Ministry of Water Resources and Irrigation, National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development secretariat. Weak institutional arrangements could create risks. |
| The proposal effectively combines adaptation and mitigation goals. This is complemented by strong financing structure with a USD 240 million loan from the Asian Development Bank (ADB) and contributions from multiple sources including GCF International Fund for Agricultural Development, ADB Cambodia Investment Trust Fund (AITF), and the Royal Cambodian Government. Together, these contribute to the project’s sustainability through long-term support and strong multilateral and national support. | The viability of irrigation modernization depends heavily on cost recovery through Farmer Water User Associations (FWUAs) with limited capacity and lack of a systematic fee collection mechanism could create risk. Without clearer arrangements for infrastructure design and maintenance, financing and capacity-building, there could be risks of infrastructure degradation and underutilization post-project. |
| With a clear focus on operations and maintenance (O&M), the project anticipates a return on investment within 10 years which highlights its focus on sustainability and long-term success. |  |

## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

The objective of the Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CASIAR) project is to strengthen the resilience of Cambodia’s rural communities and agricultural systems to climate change. Key components of the project include modernizing the irrigation infrastructure, promoting climate-resilient agriculture practices, and strengthening institutional capacities.

Targeting four highly vulnerable provinces - Kampuchea Sen, Kampong Chhnang, Oddar Meanchey, and Takeo - the project aims to address the increasing vulnerability of poor and women-headed households and exposure to worsening climate risks such as severe water limitations, reduced agricultural output, and food insecurity. The project adopts a gender-responsive and socially inclusive approach, ensuring that women’s voices are heard and their needs are integrated into resilience-building interventions.

The project is structured around three interconnected components:

1. The first component focuses on enhancing farm-level climate resilience by scaling up climate-smart agriculture practices, improving crop water management, strengthening value chains for rice, vegetables, and other crops, and promoting the use of solar-powered irrigation systems in remote rural roads.
2. The second component supports the climate-proofing and modernization of irrigation infrastructure through the construction of new irrigation canals, rehabilitation of existing irrigation pumps and training of Farmer Water User Committees (FWUCs) to promote equitable and sustainable water management practices.
3. The third component aims at creating a conducive policy environment through capacity-building of key government agencies (i.e., the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries, and the National Democratic Development [NCD] secretariat), policy development, digitization of climate tools and support for river basin management and planning systems.

CASIAR is designed to adopt a pathway towards climate-resilient, low-emission agriculture in Cambodia by promoting the adoption of proven technologies such as alternate wetting and drying (AWD) and water-saving irrigation techniques. The project will be implemented through local implementation units (LIUs) under technical assistance provided by the International Rice Research Institute. The project also integrates market-based approaches, private sector engagement, such as public-private-productive partnerships, and other innovative financing mechanisms to ensure long-term sustainability.

CASIAR is expected to directly benefit 54,277 rural people and reach an additional 1,153,618 people indirectly through system-wide improvements. The project also anticipates a mitigation impact through a reduction in greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions of 1,000,547 tons of CO₂ equivalent per year. This is expected to contribute significantly and proportionally to Cambodia’s climate targets, with 7 years of implementation and a 30-year lifespan.

The proposal outlines a total budget of USD 240 million. This budget leverages contributions from GCF totaling USD 80 million (USD 40 million grant and USD 40 million loan).


---
**Page 122**
---

### Component-by-component analysis

**Component 1: Farm-level climate adaptation and resilience (Total cost: USD 49.4 million; GCF cost: USD 26.3 million)**

- **Component 1 Focus**: Strengthening farm-level climate adaptation and resilience by equipping smallholder farmers with the knowledge, tools, and support necessary to adapt to climate change impacts. The project promotes climate-smart agricultural practices, such as improved soil management, water conservation techniques, enhanced crop variety selection, better pest and disease management, to enhance productivity and reduce vulnerability to droughts, floods, and shifting weather patterns. # Demonstration plots

## Project Overview

The project operates in a highly climate-sensitive environment where extreme weather events and prolonged droughts are common, leading to reduced crop yields and poor outcomes. These climate risks could undermine irrigation infrastructure and agricultural productivity gains. However, residual climate risks remain, necessitating continuous monitoring and adaptive management strategies.

### Component 2: Upgrading Climate-Proofing Water Infrastructure

#### Objectives
- Enhance the resilience of Cambodia's irrigation systems and agricultural productivity in the face of climate change.
- Improve water availability for irrigation through upgrading existing infrastructure such as canals, water storage facilities, and control structures with a strong emphasis on climate-proofing.

## Project Overview

The project supports a range of innovative and sustainable activities designed to strengthen institutional capacity and climate governance for resilient water and landscape management. Emphasizing the integration of climate-resilient irrigation and ecosystem-based adaptation approaches into national and subnational policies, the project enhances the sustainability of agricultural livelihoods in Cambodia's rural provinces - Kampong Srei, Kampong Cham, Kandal and Pursat - which face increasing climate risks, including rising temperatures, erratic rainfall, floods and droughts.

The project is considered to have significant impact potential, aiming to transform the vulnerability landscape of Cambodia’s rural agricultural communities through integrated climate-resilient irrigation and ecosystem-based adaptation approaches. The project will directly reach an additional 1,151,618 people (575,809 female). This represents approximately 9.4 per cent of

### Gender, environmental and social management framework, monitoring and evaluation components

The project, classified as Category A, anticipates significant positive environmental and social impacts. The gender component is a key element of the project design recognizing the need for careful management of any potential adverse impacts. Institutional arrangements include a Project Management Unit (PMU) with close coordination with MIF and the NCID secretariat, and supported by international technical assistance to ensure consistency in implementation across all components.

The environmental and social management framework includes a zero-tolerance policy for sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH). The project will also implement a gender mainstreaming strategy (GMS) and a grievance redress mechanism (GRM) to address concerns during implementation. Project risks and further information are noted in the relevant sections below.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The project is considered to have significant impact potential, aiming to transform the vulnerability landscape of Cambodia’s rural agricultural communities through integrated climate-resilient irrigation and ecosystem-based adaptation approaches.

# Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project is strategically poised to contribute significantly to sustainable development in Cambodia, which aligns with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) (No poverty), (2) (Zero hunger), and (6) (Clean water and sanitation), (12) (Responsible consumption and production) and 13 (Climate action).

By modernizing irrigation infrastructure, scaling climate-smart agriculture and strengthening institutional systems, C4S aims to enhance agricultural resilience, improve water resource management, and build the capacity of local communities among vulnerable rural communities. This integrated approach supports the development of resilient livelihoods in climate-affected provinces through improved food security, increased economic opportunities in climate-affected provinces. Through its focus on gender inclusion, institutional capacity-building and the introduction of financially sustainable mechanisms for


---
**Page 126**
---

service delivery and infrastructure maintenance, the project is expected to lead to improved food security, income stability and long-term resilience to climate shocks - contributing meaningfully to Cambodia's achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals.

### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

1. The project aims to meet the needs of Cambodia’s rural population and agriculture-dependent economy, which are increasingly threatened by climate change, poverty and food insecurity.
2. Focusing on climate-vulnerable provinces and communities, particularly smallholder farmers in these areas, the project seeks to build long-term resilience through sustainable irrigation development, climate-smart agriculture and institutional reform.

#### 3.4 CASAM directly addresses challenges such as prolonged droughts, erratic rainfall, declining soil fertility, and increased pest pressure by promoting climate-resilient practices with adaptive farming techniques, improving water management systems and enhancing institutional capacity for financial sustainability; the project seeks to build long-term resilience to climate shocks.

### Country ownership Scale: High

32. The project exemplifies strong country ownership, aligning closely with Cambodia’s national strategies on climate resilience, sustainable agriculture and water resource management. It is fully aligned with the government of Cambodia’s priorities, including its nationally determined contributions, the Rectangular Strategy, and the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) for 2018-2035. By mapping out collaboratively with key ministries such as MOWRAM, the Ministry of Environment and the NCSD secretariat, the project ensures alignment with national plans and builds on local institutional capacity.

#### 3.5 Country ownership is further reinforced through active engagement with provincial departments and districts in planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and learning and implementation. This participatory approach ensures that interventions are context-specific and community-driven, enhancing both the effectiveness and the long-term sustainability of the project while contributing to national priorities for climate resilience, sustainable agriculture and agricultural resilience.

### Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

38. The project is expected to deliver significant and quantifiable climate benefits. It is expected to reach mostly smallholder farmers, who are expected to benefit from improved water resource management systems, increased access to irrigation services, and strengthened early warning systems; institutional capacity and the adoption of climate-resilient practices will also be enhanced.

#### 3.6 The project is expected to deliver significant and quantifiable climate benefits. It is expected to reach mostly smallholder farmers, who are expected to benefit from improved water resource management systems, increased access to irrigation services, and strengthened early warning systems; institutional capacity and the adoption of climate-resilient practices will also be enhanced.

39. The project is estimated to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 1 million t CO2 eq., primarily through reduced methane emissions from improved rice cultivation rates and the replacement of diesel-powered pumps with solar photovoltaic systems. Climate co-benefits include increased water availability (5 per cent), increased crop yields (6 per cent) and GHG emission reduction (5.5 per cent) quantified.

#### 3.7 The CASAM project is expected to generate a net present value of $102 million, an internal rate of return of 16.34 per cent, exceeding the applied social discount rate of 9.47 per cent and the benefit-cost ratio is 1.26. Component-level analysis confirms that both

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Description and Financial Viability

The project demonstrates resilience to shocks, remaining economically viable under scenarios involving cost increases or benefit reductions of up to 20 per cent.

### Financial Analysis

From a government perspective, the project is financially viable even under conservative conditions: financial internal rates of return from both the private (13.8 per cent) and the government (15.4 per cent) sectors are positive at all times. From the government perspective, incorporated loan repayment obligations and cost recovery via irrigation tariffs, the project breaks even; from the government perspective at year 20 it turns into a net revenue generator.

### Sensitivity Analysis

Sensitivity-tested financial returns show limited headroom. There is also clear differentiation between the private sector and the government in terms of risk exposure. The project has significant impact components (e.g., flood protection, GHG emission reduction, institutional capacity) and the cost of these components is not covered by the project itself. This implies that the project relies on irrigation investments. GCF funding-risks for the projects, catalysts co-financing from AfDB and IDA, and other financial risks are discussed in detail.

### Project Affordability

The project has a high level of affordability and efficiency, with targeted and efficient grants. Grants are directed to non-revenue-generating, public good components, while concessional loans support the development of productive infrastructure. The overall financing structure achieves a grant element of 13 per cent, aligning within minimum concessionality principles while enabling co-financing from AfDB and IDA.

## Assessment of Consistency with GCF Safeguards and Policies

### Environmental and Social Safeguards

#### Project Background
The project aims to increase climate adaptation, mitigate the negative impacts of extreme weather events, improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers and vulnerable rural communities in four provinces of Cambodia (Pursat, Kampong Chhnang, Ratanakiri, and Stung Treng). The project is designed to address these challenges through a) possible changes to, and more effective implementation of, policies and regulatory frameworks for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction, b) the promotion of development, specifically the modernization of irrigation schemes and ponds, flood protection, and poverty reduction in rural communities, and c) the creation of an enabling environment for project success.

The E&S co-benefits include improved harvests, reduction in pesticide use and increased agricultural production and food diversity through increased yields in vegetables, fish and poultry products. The project also contributes to improving smallholder incomes, leading to better diets and nutrition and access to better healthcare services, which could result in a healthier population.

#### Environmental and Social Risk Category and Safeguards Instruments
The project has been assigned an E&S risk category of A (high). This was determined on the basis of a comprehensive assessment of the potential environmental risks and impacts from the proposed activities under the project. The assessment considered the nature, scope and scale of the project's activities; the extent to which these activities could have adverse effects on biodiversity in the project’s area of influence, and the broader socioeconomic and environmental settings in the project sites. The Secretariat concurs with the assigned risk


---
**Page 128**
---

category and notes that it is within the AB accreditation level. Since the specific designs and locations of the various interventions (subprojects) will still be developed or finalized during implementation, the framework approach has been adopted for this project. The project has an environmental, social and climate management framework (ESCMF), a land acquisition and resettlement plan, and a stakeholder engagement plan.

### 41. Compliance with GCF's environmental and social safeguards standards

The paragraphs below describe the project’s compliance with GCF’s ESS standards requirements.
#### 42. The project is expected to have positive environmental impacts, which include improved ecosystem services, soil fertility/self health, forest health and natural resources management.

Key potential negative environmental impacts are related to construction of rural infrastructure (e.g., construction/removal of small-scale rural roads, irrigation channels and small-scale reservoirs), land use changes, dust generation from earthworks, noise and vibration during excavation and earthwork activities; traffic safety hazards during construction and operation; construction waste generation; and potential for increased soil erosion due to deforestation, dust/mud nuisance, noise, vibration, and solid waste and wastewater generation from workers’ campsites. However, the project will implement environmental and social management plans prepared for the three selected subprojects.

### 43. The project is expected to have positive social impacts, which include improved access to water resources, improved sanitation facilities, and increased access to education and health services.

Key potential negative social impacts are related to displacement of local communities; conflicts with other communicable diseases; conflicts arising with local communities and gender-based violence (GBV) risks; risks associated with the implementation of environmental and social management plans during project construction; risks related to forced labour and/or child labour or age-inappropriate work; and risks for workers’ safety, health and well-being due to lack of inclusion due to vulnerability, land tenure and disability issues.

### 42. Labour and working conditions

The labour and working conditions standards apply to all workers employed directly by the project and contracted workers in subprojects (employees of contractors). The ESCMF highlights key labour issues in the country, including child labour, forced labour, gender-based violence, and risks related to the implementation of international standards. Other issues include inadequate provisions for occupational health and safety requirements; lack of training and capacity building for workers; non-compliance with contract for seasonal ethnic minority workers; SEAH in the workplace and exposure to OXO hazard. Management measures include adoption of a worker’s code of conduct and camp guidelines, establishment of a grievance mechanism, provision of information on labour and working conditions on primary suppliers and sources of aggregate materials, establishment of health and safety committees, and implementation of a range of site-specific environmental, social and climate impact assessments, compliance with national regulations and international standards for occupational health and safety procedures and inclusion of labour-related measures in agreements with workers and contractors/subcontractors, as well as conduct of training and capacity-building.


---
**Page 129**
---

### ES3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The project contributes to resource use efficiency by targeting the optimization of water and energy resources. The project aims to improve water use efficiency at the farm level by implementing climate-resilient water management practices; rehabilitation and modernization of irrigation systems to ensure efficient water application, and the adoption of water-saving technologies such as drip irrigation and AWD methods in rice cultivation. The project will also incorporate renewable energy sources for irrigation pumping and other farm activities, including the use of concentrated solar power (CSP) and photovoltaic (PV) systems. This will reduce greenhouse gas emissions from fossil fuel combustion while generating clean electricity to meet farm needs. However, it identifies potential environmental pollution that may arise during project implementation. These include soil erosion and sedimentation, water quality degradation, noise and air emissions, and the contamination of the environment from pesticides and agrochemicals during project operations.

Integrating measures for the prevention and mitigation of these risks is essential to minimize sedimentation, noise and air emissions, and waste generation. Other approaches to mitigate pollution issues include the adoption of an EAS code of practice for construction and the adoption of a zero-tolerance policy on the use of pesticides in rice production. The project will also adopt a landscape management approach to minimize the use of pesticides in crop production.

#### ES4: Construction, safety and security

Project activities on the ground may affect community health and safety due to the associated hazards that increase the likelihood of traffic accidents, the spread of communicable diseases such as human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) and malaria, and other environmental risks. These include dust, a heightened risk of GRI/SEAR and potential exposure to UXO hazards from land clearing and earthmoving activities. To mitigate these risks, the project will adopt appropriate health and safety regulations, implementation of traffic and waste management plans, employment of disease prevention measures such as mosquito nets and insect repellents, and UXO prevention through the adoption of worker codes of conduct and the adoption of a standard UXO policy in accordance with relevant regulations.

#### ES5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project seeks to minimize land acquisition and involuntary resettlement by exploring alternative designs and sites. However, some projects may still require land acquisition and/or necessitate the physical relocation of people. Additionally, some projects may require road rehabilitation that may restrict people's access to or land in restricted use zones, affecting individuals and communities that depend on agriculture and natural resources. A land acquisition and resettlement plan will be developed for each project with a detailed assessment of risks. This acquisition and resettlement planning establishes principles, procedures and measures to ensure fair compensation and the protection of human rights.

#### ES6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project may negatively impact biodiversity and forest sustainability, particularly through land use changes that could lead to deforestation or habitat destruction. For example, irrigation and flood protection could lead to the loss of wetlands, forests and aquatic ecosystems. The construction of dams for water storage and supply purposes can also threaten ecosystem functions. Poor water management could also facilitate the spread of invasive species into new areas. To mitigate these risks, the project will conduct a detailed habitat assessment and implement measures to preserve critical habitats such as wetlands and forests by avoiding or minimizing construction within these areas. The project will also adopt an EAS code of practice for land use in areas where land clearance is necessary and only use native species to restore these habitats.


---
**Page 130**
---

manage soil erosion, control measures such as silt fences, erosion control baskets and sediment basins will be established to prevent and reduce these. This will complement the soft measures such as working with local authorities and communities to develop land-use plans that minimize negative impacts on agriculture and forestry. Water and waste management plans to minimize water pollution and soil contamination will also be developed in consultation with affected communities following the mitigation hierarchy approach (i.e., avoidance, minimization, restoration or rehabilitation). The project will establish a Community Climate Change Committee (CCCC) at the sub-district level to provide technical assistance and guidance on climate change adaptation measures. The CCCC will be responsible for coordinating the implementation of these measures.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESSS (Indigenous Peoples)

The AE has confirmed that Indigenous Peoples or communities will be affected by the project. A detailed assessment of the potential impacts on Indigenous Peoples is being conducted as part of the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which contains mitigation measures for risks related to Indigenous Peoples. Initial screening conducted during project preparation identified six sub-components with a significant degree of influence (for six subthemes). During project implementation, additional consultation will be carried out in order to ensure that the needs and interests of Indigenous Peoples are fully taken into account.

### ESSS: Cultural heritage

The project could potentially have an impact on cultural heritage. The AE has confirmed that the project will conduct archaeological surveys before construction in culturally sensitive areas; the results of these surveys will inform the design of mitigation measures to protect local communities in preservation efforts, and the adoption and implementation of a change funds procedure for the protection of cultural heritage.

### Implementation arrangements

The implementation of the CAISSAR project will be coordinated across multiple agencies. A PMU, composed of personnel from MOWRAM, the Department of Water Resources, and the Meteorological Service, will handle daily project execution. Oversight will be provided by the Steering Committee, which is chaired by the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry. The Inter-Ministerial Resettlement Committee, led by MEF, which will manage land acquisition and resettlement activities for affected communities, will also provide oversight. The Department of Farmer Water User Communities will coordinate agricultural support services, ESSS and stakeholder engagement. Provincial departments of water resources and meteorology will assist with the implementation of climate change adaptation measures in their respective provinces. The project will also establish FWUCs to enhance community involvement in irrigation scheme


---
**Page 131**
---

management. These local groups will help to monitor infrastructure conditions, resolve water disputes and ensure sustained operation and maintenance. Regular assessments will evaluate material quality, infrastructure resilience and the social impact of project activities. The project will also deploy key personnel, including an Environmental and Climate Safeguards Specialist, who will be responsible for implementing the ESFMC and ensuring compliance with the management plan. A Gender and Social Safeguards Specialist will ensure implementation of social mainstreaming measures in all aspects of the project. An Indigenous Peoples’ Rights Advisor and a Resettlement Plan Specialist will ensure fair compensation and relocation for affected households. The project will also deploy an Environmental Monitoring Officer, one Social Specialist and one GEF Focal Point for full-time project support. These specialists will play a critical role in ensuring compliance, conducting training and maintaining ESFMC standards throughout implementation.

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure
The project undertakes extensive stakeholder engagement to ensure that the project is transparent and accountable. The ESFMC outlines a comprehensive process of stakeholder engagement and consultation, which included multiple meetings with key stakeholders at various stages of the project development. Ongoing engagement will be undertaken throughout the project implementation to receive timely feedback from those who may be affected by certain project activities. A Stakeholder Engagement Management Plan (SEMP) has been developed to ensure ongoing engagement with stakeholders throughout the life of the project. The ESFMC indicates that all safeguards-related documents and information will be made available in a public place, such as a community center or school, in a form and language understandable to key stakeholders (Kmer and English).

### Grievance redress mechanism
The project has established a grievance mechanism (GGM) that is accessible, transparent and user-friendly. The ESFMC outlines the procedures for lodging grievances and the resolution processes for general complaints, as well those related to specific project activities. The GGM will be implemented in accordance with the implementation of the subproject; the GGM will be established by the implementing agencies, which will ensure that all complaints are recorded and resolved within a reasonable time frame. The GGM is easily accessible to the public and able to resolve issues at the lowest level as quickly as possible. The GGM will also provide for escalation procedures if necessary.

### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) safeguarding
The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision BIMR 2018/1 requires safeguarding from SEAH in all projects funded under the GCF. This includes the current project as part of the implementation of the funding proposal. The project includes activities that involve engaging with community members, including women and youth, who may be vulnerable to sexual exploitation and abuse at provincial level. Risks factors encompass gender inequalities that tend to be greater in rural areas of Cambodia where the farming communities are located; construction of rural irrigation infrastructure will also increase access to water resources for these communities, likely to be mostly male, from outside the farming communities or project locations who will need to be provided with training and capacity building. The project will implement a range of mitigation which may result in physical and/or economic displacement. SEAH risks related to interventions under this subproject are considered high and have been included in the ESFMC. Further assessment of SEAH risks will be undertaken at the subproject level. PMU members will be trained on the identification, reporting and response mechanisms for SEAH risks. A workers’ code of conduct is annexed to the ESFMC, which all construction workers will be required to sign. The project-level GRM will receive and record complaints related to SEAH in the case of any incidents or allegations of sexual exploitation and abuse. The PMU coordinator for immediate support. In addition, confidential reporting will be ensured. Procedures for SEAH

# Gender policy

## 4.2 Gender policy

54. The AIF has provided a gender assessment and action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The legal and institutional framework that demonstrates the commitment to gender equality, including women's rights, women's empowerment is outlined in the assessment. Identified issues in the national framework are addressed through the action plan. The AIF has provided a strong equal value scope to make the training provided by the Ministry of Women’s Affairs at its development centre accessible for all participants and will ensure that it complements and supports existing climate change strategies and policies. Women are identified as vulnerable to climate change. These characteristics are also reflected in the action plan, which addresses cultural norms that perpetuate and reinforce the traditional division of labour between women and men, particularly the burden of household duties, time poverty, limited access to resources, and lower decision-making power.

56. The gender action plan covers activities, performance indicators, timelines, and responsible persons. It also includes a list of specific actions for each activity that will be performed as form or female-headed households have been included in the gender action plan. It includes activities such as training on climate change adaptation practices and technologies for small enterprises and to improve access to agrometeorological advisories. Training will be offered to communities on climate-resilient practices and G4M of irrigation canals as a way of empowering women and men, particularly female-headed households. The action plan also includes community-based action plans for the adoption of climate-resilient crop water management practices. These actions are expected to contribute towards the achievement of the SDGs in influencing decision-making. A gender consultant and officer will be recruited during the project’s implementation phase to ensure that the gender action plan is implemented, to undertake further gender analysis at the subproject level and identify any additional actions that can be implemented to address the needs of women and men.

# Risks

## 4.3 Risks

### Overall project assessment (medium risk)

56. The Secretariat considers the overall project as medium risk.

### Accredited entity/executing entity capacity to execute the current project (medium risk)


---

## Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Credit Risk: The borrower of the GCF loan will be the Government of Cambodia (rated B by Moody’s Investors Service). This is a credit risk to the country. The country is currently facing several challenges given the uncertainties around trade policies. However, the concessionality of the loan and the fact that it is a GCF loan should mitigate this vulnerability.

The GCF loan will be ranked part pass with other co-financers such as AIFD and AIIB.

### Co-financing risk: AIIB provides a stable funding to the programme; however, the agreement between the two parties does not specify how the co-financing amount or reduction of the co-financing, the impact might not be materialized. To partially mitigate the risk, the GCF will ensure that the co-financing is disbursed in accordance with the budget and the AE/EEs have to inform GCF as soon as possible in case of cancellation or delay in the co-financing.

## Compliance Risk (medium risk)

### The project is being implemented in regions with developing regulatory frameworks, which introduces a number of risks of financial oversight. Additionally, the focus on small-scale agricultural finance introduces risks related to fund allocation and potential misuse due to weaker governance structures. Furthermore, given the reliance on local government authorities and financial intermediaries, there is also the possibility of encountering risks related to sanctions compliance.

### IFAD has established control measures to mitigate these risks, including a thorough due diligence process for all project partners. This includes KYC checks and regular sanctions screening processes. The use of financial intermediaries also helps to reduce exposure from ongoing financial monitoring and auditing, which helps detect any irregularities early on. Furthermore, the project will benefit from technical assistance provided by IFAD that supports oversight, including the provision of technical assistance to enhance local government and financial capacity. These measures help maintain transparency and reduce the likelihood of non-compliance.

### While controls such as due diligence, sanctions screening, and continuous monitoring manage many risks associated with compliance, there are still inherent challenges in implementing these measures effectively. Considering these factors, the residual compliance risk rating for the project is medium. However, given the project’s intended safeguards and ongoing external vulnerabilities inherent in the operational environment, this rating may be revised.

## GCF portfolio concentration risk

### In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration levels; results area, single proposal area of concentration.

## Recommendations

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

### a) MOWRAM will establish a PMU for the implementation of the CAISAR project and will be responsible for the overall management of the project.

The PMU will be led by the Secretary of State, and will consist of the following members: Project Manager, Human Resources and Administration Officer, Procurement Officer and Monitoring and Evaluation Officer. The PMU will be responsible for overseeing the implementation of the project and undertaking detailed design services and construction supervision processes.

The mitigation measures to address these risks include recruitment of qualified consultants for project implementation and management, development of a Project Implementation Manual and a Financial Management Manual that outline clear roles for funds flow and disbursement arrangements, as well as guidance for supporting documentation and material preparation; receipt of independent verification/implementation of accounting software to facilitate accurate record keeping and financial reporting and close coordination with the IFAD team in terms of financial management.

### c) CAISAR project financial statements will be audited annually by a private audit firm.

### 4.6 Legal assessment

75. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 24 September 2018 (the “AMA”) and it became effective on 9 November 2018. The Accredited Entity’s five-year accreditation term lapsed on 9 November 2023 but was extended by Decision B:37/18 until 9 November 2028, subject to the terms of the AMA. The extension of the accreditation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board, TCF and/or GCF will be determined in accordance with Article 5(4) of Decision B:37/18, and negotiation of the Amended and Restated Accreditation Master Agreement is ongoing.

76. The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

77. The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Cambodia, which in itself may present some challenges for GCF. For example, there is no law against other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which rights need to be further clarified and defined by the Cambodian authorities. Furthermore, access for investigations in such a country may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for GCF.

78. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation or whether this should be provided by appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

79. Since the Accredited Entity’s accreditation is a signatory to a contract with the executing entity, it is not feasible for the AE to disengage from the provisions of the AMA, allowing the step in to the Accredited Entity’s contractual arrangements with the Executing Entity, i.e., Royal Government of Cambodia (the “RGC”). In light of the nature and scope of these contracts, it is not feasible for the Accredited Entity to allow GCF or any other party to take over its contracted relationship with the Executing Entity under the subsidiary agreement. # Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP270

## Proposal name: Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CASAR) in Cambodia

### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development

### Country: Cambodia

### Project size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview
- **Persistent vulnerability of Cambodia's agriculture sector:** As a cornerstone of rural livelihoods and national food security, Cambodia's agriculture sector is constrained by systemic development challenges.
- **Climate change as a threat multiplier:** The impacts of climate change are exacerbating Cambodia's existing agricultural challenges, acting as a threat multiplier across ecological, economic, social, and political dimensions. Increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, including storms and rising temperatures, are disrupting traditional cropping calendars, lowering yields and accelerating soil degradation.
- **Increased vulnerability to shocks:** The country is particularly susceptible to shocks and reduces its ability to transition towards a more sustainable and inclusive economy.

#### 1.2 Project description
The Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CASAR) project is designed to address Cambodia's critical water scarcity, water security, and institutional capacity in response to increasing climate risks.


---
**Page 138**
---

### Scale: Low to medium

#### 1.2 Impact potential

**Adaptation impact:** The CASAR project is aimed at increasing the climate resilience of Gambian agriculture sector with wider-reaching benefits for communities, ecosystems, livelihoods and infrastructure. By rehabilitating and modernizing irrigation systems and canal networks, the project will reduce water stress in agricultural areas, increase yields and droughts, and ensure more stable agricultural production in the face of erratic weather patterns. The project will also improve access to clean drinking water by constructing new boreholes and dams to protect farms and enhance the reliability of water supply throughout the year. The integration of climate-smart agricultural practices (e.g. efficient irrigation methods), sustainable land management techniques, and improved crop varieties will increase yields and farm incomes. These improvements aim to support diversified cropping and multiple harvests per year helping farmers to diversify their income sources.

**Mitigation impact:** CASAR is designed to strengthen institutional and community capacity through training for project staff and beneficiaries on climate-smart agricultural practices and tools to manage irrigation systems and climate services effectively. The project seeks to promote social inclusion by providing targeted livelihood support, including access to credit and decision-making and providing targeted livelihood support to the most vulnerable households. It is also geared to enhance financial inclusion by expanding access to rural credit, supporting financial literacy training for farmers, and improving access to insurance products and other financial products. Through these combined efforts, CASAR is expected to build stronger, more adaptive and resilient communities that are better equipped to cope with climate change and a climate-resilient rural economy. The CASAR project is expected to benefit a total of 17,143,375 hectares of agricultural land in the Gambia, which represents approximately 20% of the country's cultivated area. This will be achieved through the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices such as alternate wetting and drying for rice cultivation, crop diversification, improved soil and fertilizer management (including organic fertilizers), and integrated pest management.

**Note:** The project is expected to reduce and/or sequester approximately 1,006,507 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2-eq) over a 20-year period. This mitigation impact will be achieved through the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices such as alternate wetting and drying for rice cultivation, crop diversification, improved soil and fertilizer management.


---
**Page 139**
---

### Risks to impact

#### 1. The rehabilitation of a 100 km road may lead to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.

The AE indicates that all-weather road under the command area is expected to have several key impacts on GHG emissions, including:

- An influx of up to 735 skilled and unskilled workers is anticipated, primarily sourced from local communities during construction; the ESCA states that this is a "short-term" influx. However, in the long term, an increase in urban population scope, on existing and planned roads, a population increase is expected.

#### (d) Deforestation is not considered a risk as the project focuses on knowledge improvement and infrastructure development. The AE does not identify any direct or indirect deforestation impacts identified.

#### (e) On biodiversity, areas may be temporarily affected during construction but mitigation measures are in place, as detailed in the biodiversity assessment.

In response, the view of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) is that the rehabilitation of a 100 km road under the command area is expected to have several impacts and the following key concerns remain unanswered:

- **Influx of people:** The anticipation of a short-term influx of 735 workers during construction, which may lead to increased demand for housing and other services. These pressures resulting from improved road access; improved connectivity could attract new settlers or investors, leading to land use changes in the vicinity of the command area. This could also lead to land-use changes (e.g., conversion of land use from agriculture to residential and/or commercial uses).

#### 10. Deforestation mapping.

The ESCA currently refers to short road sections; yet the total project involves 80–100 km of roads, a significant length that should not be dismissed as negligible. The impact on rehabilitated segments would improve transparency and help to assess cumulative impacts.

#### 11. Deforestation exclusion.

The ESCA terms of reference explicitly exclude deforestation risks originating from road rehabilitation in the project area. This is because the project does not include rural roads, which are a significant determinant of access. However, if rural road financing is included, this omission becomes a critical gap. Road rehabilitation, especially over 80–100 km, can indirectly drive deforestation by facilitating land encroachment or illegal logging; either unintended consequence of deforestation risks.

and/or maladaptation as well as malintegration impacts) should be properly assessed, or the road rehabilitation component should be reconsidered.

12. Impact on GHG emissions. The road rehabilitation may or may not have a potentially significant impact on climate change mitigation. This is not clearly addressed in the funding proposal. It is recommended to provide more credible evidence regarding whether or not the road rehabilitation component is immaterial.*

13. Land use and land cover changes. The project proposes several infrastructure construction activities (such as the rehabilitation of canals and farm roads along with changes in land use and water management practices) that may have significant impacts on biodiversity, including species survival, disturbance to nesting and foraging areas, along with disruption to natural water flow and sediment transport, which could negatively impact aquatic and terrestrial ecosystems. The report, however, does not assess the potential impacts (e.g., increased traffic, urbanization, GHG emissions) of a rehabilitated all-weather road in these areas – areas that still form part of the proposed project area. These areas include parts of the districts in the upper portions of Purat, Kamping Chhimo, Kandal and Kamping Speu provinces.

14. The following are some of the potential impacts on biodiversity and associated risks: (i) provide detailed road mapping; and (ii) either provide an analysis on unintended consequences (e.g., deforestation analysis, land-use change) or simply remove sub-activity 1.4.3 from the project.

15. Monitoring and evaluation. The monitoring and evaluation framework should be used to reassess the potential impacts of the road rehabilitation component in light of the introduction of practices or technologies vary from those used in the original (ex ante) baseline projections. This ensures that the reported emission reductions reflect real-world implementation, rather than assumptions about future scenarios.

16. Subject to the resolution of this issue, the funding proposal's impact potential is assessed as low to medium.


### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium to high

Advancing a paradigm shift in climate-resilient agriculture and water management, the CASAAR project seeks to make a significant departure from conventional, flood-prone irrigation systems by integrating improved agricultural practices with modernized irrigation infrastructure. By modernizing irrigation schemes and canals, the project is expected to improve year-round water resource availability for farmers while reducing their vulnerability to floods and droughts. This integration is designed to ensure that water infrastructure directly supports climate-resilient agriculture.

CASAAR is designed to contribute to low-emission development by replacing diesel pumps with solar-powered irrigation systems, reducing GHG emissions and enhancing energy security. The project's focus on climate resilience reflects a forward-looking commitment to low-carbon, climate-resilient agriculture.


*The IFAD has already identified the same issue in P1273 “Towards Resilience to Climate Change of Smallholders Receiving the Services of the Inclusive Agricultural Value Chain Programme”, where IFAD was the AE. Lessons learned from this project can be applied to CASAAR.
*See funding proposal biodiversity assessment report for CASAAR.


---
**Page 141**
---

19. The institutional capacity and mandate of the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM) is envisioned to shift from infrastructure delivery to strategic water governance and integrated planning (see para. 37 below). The capacity-building at both the national and provincial levels, as well as the development of a new set of four pillars (4Ps), are planned to improve their ability to manage water equitably and adaptively over the long term.
20. CAIASS plans to promote inclusive access to rural finance via Village Savings and Loan Associations and PFVICS, linking smallholders to credit, insurance and input markets. The public-private partnership model is designed to leverage private sector financial investment and crowd in private investment, thus reducing dependency on subsidies and enabling the scaling up of water management services.
21. Through support for policy reform, the project is geared to help to embed climate resilience into national irrigation planning and governance. MOWRAM, in its planned enhanced role, is expected to play a key role in supporting the transition towards more resilient agriculture systems across the country.
22. CAIASS is designed to prioritize knowledge exchange and farmer training, facilitating the uptake of new technologies, improved agricultural techniques and positive behavioral change. This is intended to enhance adaptive capacity not only during project implementation but also into the future.
23. Overall, CAIASS represents a strong paradigm shift by combining infrastructure modernization, low-carbon innovation, institutional transformation and financial inclusion. It strengthens rural livelihoods in Cambodia’s agriculture sector while laying the foundation for sustainable, scalable impact across Cambodia's agriculture and water sectors.
24. The paradigm shift potential is assessed as medium to high.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential Scale: Medium to high

25. **Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):** The proposal aims to contribute to the achievements of the following SDG 1 (No poverty) by helping farmers to earn more and become more resilient to climate impacts; SDG 2 (Zero hunger) through better farming methods and improved access to markets; SDG 3 (Good health and well-being) by improving access to finance, training and livelihood opportunities; SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation) by protecting water resources from pollution and ensuring sustainable water use for irrigation and livestock growth) by supporting small businesses and rural jobs in farming and beyond; SDG 9 (Industry, innovation and infrastructure) through the development of new technologies and innovations; SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities) by focusing on poor, remote and vulnerable communities; SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production) by promoting sustainable practices that protect the land; SDG 13 (Climate action) by helping to be able to adapt to climate change and reduce emissions; SDG 15 (Life on land) by restoring forests, protecting soil and improving biodiversity; SDG 17 (Partnerships for the goals) through collaboration with local communities and international partners.

26. **Advancing rural socioeconomic development:** The CAIASS project is designed to enhance livelihoods and income security for smallholder farmers, thus playing a vital role in strengthening rural livelihoods in Cambodia. The planned improved irrigation infrastructure will provide water resources that are more reliable and accessible to farmers, empowering farmers to sustainably manage water resources and increase yields. Capacity-building, the 4Ps


---
**Page 142**
---

initiative and improved access to finance and markets aim to generate inclusive economic opportunities, particularly for vulnerable communities.

### Promoting environmental sustainability

Environmental preservation is central to the CASAIR design gives that the project prioritizes nature-based solutions such as agroforestry, soil conservation, and water harvesting. These interventions are aligned with the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 13 and 15. Nature-based solutions help to restore degraded ecosystems and reduce GHG emissions. These interventions are aligned with the UN SDGs 2, 6, and 17. The project also aims to improve biodiversity, enhance soil health and mitigate flood and drought risks while ensuring climate-resilient livelihoods.

### Empowering women and promoting gender equity

CASAIR is designed to incorporate a robust gender action plan to advance women's empowerment in agriculture and water governance. The project will work with the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) and the Women’s International Network for Information (WINI). CASAIR also works with the FAO and WINI to provide training in climate-smart agriculture and financial literacy, and supports women's access to land and resources. The project aims to empower women by increasing their decision-making roles and to ensure equal access to project benefits, thus strengthen women’s agency and contribute towards achieving SDG 5.

### Risk to sustainable development potential

The issue on rehabilitation of a 100 km road which may lead to GHG emissions and could adversely impact other SDGs such as SDG 13 and 15. The sustainable development potential is assessed as medium to high.


## Needs of the recipient

Scale: Medium to high

### Need to reduce vulnerability of target beneficiaries

Over 75 per cent of Cambodia's rural population depends on rain for agriculture, making them more exposed to droughts, changing precipitation patterns and water shortages. The project addresses this by establishing community-based early warning systems for flooding, flood and extreme weather advisories to safeguard vulnerable groups.

### Need for equitable access to climate-smart irrigation

Smallholder farmers (especially women) in flood-prone regions are particularly vulnerable as these communities often lack access to irrigation infrastructure. These groups remain more exposed to droughts and water shortages due to their limited access to irrigation systems, which in turn helps to reduce rural poverty and food insecurity by enabling all farmers to benefit from productive land use. The project aims to increase the area under climate-smart irrigation is expanded, ensuring that low-income and marginalized farmers have access to efficient irrigation systems. Indigenous farming communities in flood-prone regions will gain resilient water management skills through training.

### Need for access to finance

Many farmers lack access to concessional financing or formal financial mechanisms. Limiting their access to loans in climate-adaptive technologies. Financially constrained small farmers often struggle through the rainy season, unlocking long-term adaptation investments. The financial barriers faced by vulnerable farmers will be


---
**Page 143**
---

addressed by the 4Ps initiative,10 which promotes microfinance, concessional loans and climate-smart credit lines.

36. **Need to reduce gender disparities**: Women farmers represent nearly 50 per cent of the agricultural workforce, yet face structural barriers in land ownership, credit access and participation in decision-making. CASAIR aims to increase women’s access to finance and enable smallholder farmers actively participate in agricultural decision-making, promoting gender-equitable climate change adaptation strategies that are inclusive of women’s rights and financial inclusion.

35. Removing barriers to climate adaptation, strengthening financial accessibility and embedding climate resilience into agricultural resilience strategies ensures that the needs of Cambodia's most vulnerable populations are prioritized in national climate adaptation efforts.

36. The needs of the recipient are assessed as medium to high.


### 1.6 Country ownership

**Scale: Medium to high**

No-objection letter secured. The Ministry of Environment issued a no-objection letter on 24 February 2024.

Aligning with national climate and development priorities, CASAIR is strategically aligned with Cambodia's National Adaptation Plan (NAP), Climate Change Strategic Plan and institutional capacity building efforts. The project contributes to the NAP’s five key areas in supporting key national adaptation targets, including the development of climate and flood warning systems, standardized best practices for irrigation and strengthened institutional capacity building.

The project is consistent with the priorities outlined in the 2019-2023 Implementation Plan of the Climate Change Strategic Plan by enhancing food, water and energy security; safeguarding agricultural productivity through improved access to climate information services; and promoting resilience through inclusive, coordinated approaches. Leveraging climate finance, concessional funding and private sector investment will support the project in achieving tenure sustainability through policy alignment and blended financing mechanisms.

39 Strategic partnership with MOIWARM: MOIWARM is the designated national authority responsible for managing Cambodia's water resources. Its extensive mandate includes river basin planning, irrigation development and management, flood and drought monitoring, and coordination of climate change adaptation efforts at the national level. The CASAIR project is institutional and strategic given its mandate, operational scope and capacity to implement cross-sectoral interventions in water resource management.

MOIWARM will serve as an executing entity and host the Project Management Unit (PMU), which is tasked with the coordination of all components and consolidation of planning and reporting. The PMU, staffed by experienced personnel from MOIWARM, will be responsible for project implementation, financial management, procurement, and monitoring and evaluation. The PMU will also manage cross-sectoral interventions in water resource management (e.g., irrigation development, building), which are central to the project's transformational ambition. MOIWARM has a strong track record of implementing large-scale projects with international partners.

The project is supported by a high-level Project Steering Committee, which includes high-level representation from the Ministries of Economy and Finance, Agriculture, Environment, Water Resources, and Planning and Investment. The PMU will be responsible for coordinating all components and consolidating planning and reporting. MOIWARM is positioned to effectively manage cross-sectoral integration and risk mitigation.

The ministry is actively strengthening its institutional capacity through targeted training in climate-resilient water resource management, flood and drought monitoring, irrigation development, river basin management and gender and social inclusion. These capacity-building efforts are embedded within the project’s implementation strategy and will be supported by MOIWARM.

### Access to local implementation partners supporting MOWRAM

To ensure effective, inclusive and sustainable implementation of the CASAR project, MOWRAM collaborates with several key national institutions that have a strong track record in water resources management and mandates.

#### The National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development (NCSSDD)

This plays a pivotal role in implementing component 1 and output 3.2. The committee has extensive experience in facilitating decentralized service delivery and works through provincial, district and commune agencies to implement national rural development and climate adaptation programmes such as Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF), Ministry of Rural Development (MRD), Ministry of Industry and Commerce/Accelerating Inclusive Markets for Smallholders, Ministry of Rural Development (MRD) and the Department of Water Resources Management (DWRM).

#### The Provincial Water Resources Committees

These oversee the establishment, training and operational guidance of FWICs, ensuring that these community-level institutions can manage irrigation water resources sustainably.

#### FWICs

While not directly involved with farming communities responsible for managing water distribution, maintaining irrigation systems and making local decisions that promote climate-resilient agriculture at the grassroots level. These institutions complement MOWRAM's leadership and technical expertise, ensuring a coordinated, decentralized approach to climate-resilient irrigation development across Cambodia.

### Risk to county ownership

#### Limited technical capacities of local partners

Given the central role played by MOWRAM in its relevance in Cambodia’s water resources sector, and additional limitations on the capacity of local institutions, particularly in the areas of modern technology and governance systems which remain critical bottlenecks for rural development outcomes.


---
**Page 145**
---

### Sustainability of training

The current training approach is limited to the project timeline with no mechanism to sustain capacity development. The AII should work with centres of excellence like the Institute of Technology of Cambodia to establish a permanent training unit or certification framework for digital technologies.

#### Lack of certification and assessment

There are no defined competency benchmarks or systems to assess training effectiveness. The AII should introduce a certification framework linked to the training modules, including practical exercises, assessments, and application.

#### Limited focus on digital tools

Digital technologies such as SCADA, geographic information systems (GIS), mobile planning and mobile data systems are not clearly defined and fully integrated into the training. The AII should expand training modules to include these tools and support their integration through capacity building for trainers and geographic information system platforms for real-time water management.

#### Weak stakeholder linkages

The project plan does not extend to PWG beyond their formation as a technical committee. The AII should design joint training for leaders from MOIWARM, the provincial departments of water resources and meteorology and PWG and engage private sector stakeholders in capacity building on digital tools and systems.

#### Gender inclusion

While gender awareness is noted, there is no clear strategy to involve women in technical training or irrigation leadership. A gender-responsive training plan should be developed for women leaders in irrigation management.

#### Country ownership

Country ownership is assessed as medium to high.


### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

The GCF commitment of USD 80 million (USD 40 million grants, USD 40 million concessional loan) is set to play a pivotal role in mobilizing an additional USD 160.1 million in co-financing, resulting in total project financing of USD 248.1 million. This reflects a leverage ratio of 3:1 and represents a significant increase from the initial GCF commitment of USD 50 million dollars from other financiers. AII is providing USD 100 million in concessional loans, representing 76% of the total project financing. The remaining 24% will be co-financed by other donors, which are likely to be approved within the second half of 2023.

The concessional nature of GCF funding allows for a more flexible use of funds and enables the AII to finance the CASAIR program. GCF funding is strategically targeted towards climate adaptation measures; the investment in water resources management and irrigation infrastructure will unlock large-scale capital for climate-resilient development, demonstrating its critical role in unlocking transformational finance and fostering long-term partnerships in climate-vulnerable contexts.

### Co-financing

Co-financing with other donors is highly complementary, both supporting climate-resilient market-driven agriculture in Cambodia's northern provinces and providing a platform to engage agricultural suppliers, traders, retailers and financial institutions with communities. Key areas of synergy include the following: (i) CASAIR will build on PEARL agreements with private sector actors such as agri-suppliers, farmers' unions, smallholder farmers and value chain actors; (ii) joint efforts will strengthen market access, enhance value chains and improve livelihoods for smallholder farmers in Cambodia's northern provinces through MOIWARM and the National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development, while PEARL engages with private sector actors to support climate-resilient agricultural practices. The approach: (iii) CASAIR climate-smart irrigation and farming methods complement PEARL ecologically sound practices; Advancing agricultural sustainability and resilience; and (v) both

# Robust economic and financial analysis

The CASAR project demonstrates strong economic viability with a high internal rate of return (IRR) and sustainable climate-resilient development outcomes in Cambodia. The project is expected to achieve an IRR of 14.27 per cent, which is higher than the discount rate at 9.74 per cent and exceeds the NPV by USD 68.53 million. This indicates that the project's profitability and effectiveness in resource allocation and outcome delivery **is robust**.

The detailed risk analysis of CASAR shows the analysis on how different scenarios impact the internal rate of return (IRR) and net present value (NPV). The base scenario, evaluated over 30 years at a 9.47 per cent social discount rate, yields a strong IRR of 13.4 per cent and an NPV of USD 68.53 million, which is robust against various risk assumptions.

## Project benefits reduction

A 10 per cent decline in benefits (due to lower output prices, yield or adoption rates) decreases the IRR by 13.27 per cent and the NPV by USD 74.25 million. A 20 per cent drop pushes the IRR close to the discount rate at 9.74 per cent, with the NPV dropping to USD 68.53 million.

## Project costs increase

A 10 per cent rise in costs (e.g., higher construction, farm inputs or service delivery) increases the IRR by 2.27 per cent and the NPV by USD 66.61 million. A 20 per cent increase lowers the IRR to 9.58 per cent. The project can withstand up to a 43.12 per cent cost escalation without becoming unviable.

## Shorter evaluation periods

Reducing the assessment to 20 years drops the IRR to 14.27 per cent and the NPV by USD 69.85 million. A 10-year horizon results to a near-zero IRR (1.02 per cent) but still yields an NPV of USD 68.53 million, demonstrating long-term sustainability.

## Long-term impacts

A one-year delay has limited impact, but a three-year delay sharply reduces the IRR by 15.87 per cent and the NPV to USD 137.98 million, suggesting that the project is resilient to implementation lags.

## Sensitivity analysis

A 10 per cent increase in discount rate increases the NPV to USD 133.67 million, reflecting a higher value in stable economic conditions. A higher 11 per cent rate reduces the NPV to USD 85.54 million, indicating sensitivity to macroeconomic risks or increases in the cost of financing.

## Adoption rate changes

If farmer adoption of new practices falls from 70 to 60 per cent, the IRR drops by 23.91 per cent and the NPV by USD 18.45 million. This underscores the importance of farmer commitment and constant stakeholder engagement.

## Exclusion of environmental benefits

Ignoring GHG emission reductions lowers the BESI-CA to -0.76 per cent, which is below the discount rate at 9.74 per cent. This drastically reduces the IRR by 10.42 per cent and the NPV to USD 20.44 million, emphasizing the project's value in environmental sustainability.

## Limited irrigated expansion

If new irrigated areas are added post-project, the IRR falls to 14.53 per cent and the NPV to USD 93.06 million, highlighting the need for scalability.


---
**Page 147**
---

### Risk to efficiency and effectiveness

#### 5.7.3.1 Risk of increased GHG emissions

The potential risk of increased GHG emissions due to the road rehabilitation project will also impact the economic and financial viability of the CAISAR project. The reduction in GHG emissions accounts for 54.7 per cent of benefits,20 monitored using Allen et al., carbon price of US$16 per tonne (2018) as a proxy for future GHG emissions, the economic internal rate of return (EIRR) falls to 14.51 per cent.

#### 5.7.3.2 Risk of increased costs

If the road rehabilitation project is included as one of the funded components, then the following scenarios may happen:

(a) If the effectiveness of CAISAR will be affected (i.e., post-harvest losses remain at 10–15 per cent rather than being halved), market commercialization rates drop from 90 per cent to 70 per cent, rather than being halved; market commercialization rates drop from 90 per cent (with CAISAR) to 60 per cent without it; and the selling time in markets would remain at five hours instead of reducing to two hours.21

(b) The cost-benefit assessment of component 1 will be affected (i.e., EIRR may fall below the social discount rate). This is because the benefits of component 1 are critical for economic activity.) The economic viability assessments for component 1, which includes the road rehabilitation project, would fall to 5.79 per cent; benefit-cost ratio = 1:1.12) However, this impact remains to be assessed considering that the sensitivity and risk analysis does not explicitly mention how the road rehabilitation project will affect the cost-benefit assessment.

#### Limited assessment of climate extreme events

The risks of variable climate (e.g., erratic rainfall) are not captured out a season of crops produced, irrigated or road rehabilitated. It may, however, be indirectly captured in the benefit reduction scenario (-10 per cent to -20 per cent). The potential missing impacts from climate change (which are not explicitly mentioned) include drought resilience (impact on water availability for irrigation), temperature variability and extreme events (e.g., heat waves, floods, etc.) and changes in rainfall patterns beyond the 30-year horizon) and climate extreme events (e.g. tropical cyclones). All should be considered as part of the risk assessment and sensitivity analysis under different scenarios.

#### Unclear alignment with other GCF funded programmes

The IFAD notes that the CAIFD is not aligned to any significant thematic or operational overlap with a Cambodia Climate Financing Facility (CCFF), an existing GCF-funded programme with the Korea Development Bank. As it is, the CCFF has been providing concessional credit for climate change projects in Cambodia through a dual-track approach: a lending facility offering concessional debt to project proponents and a risk management facility aimed at capacity building for market actors, including local financial institutions. CCFF is managed by the Agricultural and Rural Development Bank, a Cambodian State-owned bank, and is capitalized with $100 million from the KDB and $50 million from the World Bank as an operational facility. Given the shared objectives, particularly around promoting climate-resilient agriculture
