### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal. Given the key environmental and institutional risks identified in the CASAIR report, specific disbursement conditions for IFAD are recommended.

#### Preconditions to the first disbursement of the GCF Process Fund

1. Delivery of the GCF to the Accredited Entity, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretary.
2. An updated Environmental, Social, and Climate Impact Assessment (ESCAI), along with a road-specific Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) under Annex 6 [ESAS]. Documentation must be provided for all planned land use changes associated with project activities. This update assessment must address cumulative GHG emissions, potential land-use change impacts on biodiversity, water resources, deforestation, and forest mapping.
3. An updated Annex 22 [Management of GCF Process Fund] reflecting the Monitoring Report and any other funding proposal and supporting documents enclosed with Annex 22, to recapture the GHG calculations, including indirect impacts related to road rehabilitation and construction, and the use of red壤 land, potential land-use change and deforestation.
4. An updated Annex 3 [Economic and Financial Analysis] to the funding proposal to align with new data and findings and reflect the revised project costs, financial rate of return and other investment metrics.
5. An updated Annex 11 (Monitoring and Evaluation Plan) to the funding proposal, which should include a comparison between the GCF emission reduction estimates if the actual adoption rates of the introduced practices or technologies differ from those used in the original baseline scenario. The monitoring results must reflect actual implementation outcomes and are presented accurately.
6. Other relevant annexes, as needed, to reflect the changes and outcomes of the ESIA, CASAIR, and other studies.

7. To mitigate institutional risks (See paragraphs 41 and 42), IFAD should be required to provide the following covenants under the funded activity agreement:


---
**Page 149**

### Covenants to be included in the funded activity agreement

The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the Project Implementation Manual includes the time-bound institutional capacity strengthening plan, prepared by the Accredited Entity in support of MEWQAM, covering training in climate-smart irrigation and financial management, including the development of a database for water meter upgrades, and third-party technical support in relation to Output 3.1 of Component 2.

The Accredited Entity shall provide updates on progress towards the capacity strengthening plan on a quarterly basis, and inform the outcomes to the Fund in the APR.

These conditions are essential to ensure effective risk management, safeguard compliance, and alignment with GCF's environmental and fiduciary standards.

## Impact potential

IFAD takes note of the assessment and recommendations provided.

IFAD would like to reassure GCF that its feasibility analysis of the roads showed that it is unlikely there will be significant impacts on the environment as there will not be any direct clearing of forest during construction of the roads; the roads will be rehabilitated to enable market access for farmers, which will also contribute to reducing deforestation and land degradation. In the full-blown Biodiversity Impact Assessment, the area where the roads are to be constructed was assessed as having low biodiversity values and no significant negative externalities such as deforestation in the period after construction. Furthermore, many of the roads will be built on canal embankments and will enhance flood control and drainage systems. The roads were not assessed as significantly contributing to more GHGs owing to forest loss or increased emissions from road maintenance. 1. **INTRODUCTION**

### Project Background

#### Project Objective
The project objective is to increase climate adaptation, mitigate the negative impact of extreme climate events, and improve livelihoods of smallholder farmers and vulnerable rural communities in four provinces of Cambodia, including Pursat, Kampong Chhnang, Kampong Speu, and Kandal provinces (see map below).

Figure 1 - Map showing six irrigation sub-schemes located in four provinces

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **Pursat** | **Kampong Chhnang** | **Kampong Speu** |


---
**Page 158**
---

1.2 Beneficiaries

The CAISAR project aims to bring about benefits to 100,000 households (500,000 people, 3% of the population). The project is financed through the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM) and the National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development Secretariat (NCDD-S), a Direct Access Accredited Entity to the GCF.

1.3 Project activities

The Project will carry out a wide range of activities in the four project provinces. These activities are organized into three project components:

Component 1: Implementing farm-level climate adaptation, resilience, and water use efficiency

The objective of this component is to build climate resilience (CR) of smallholder farmers and enhance sustainable production through evidence-based planning and context-relevant climate resilient practices at the farm level. This component is designed to address the lack of knowledge and skills to deploy technologies and practices in CR by providing training, developing materials, and creating extension services to propagate them. It will introduce farmers with various climate resilient technologies and practices for both rice and non-rice activities such as vegetable production, poultry and aquaculture.

Output 1.1: Increased farmer capacity in climate resilient agriculture

This output will focus on developing farmer's capacity in deploying CR technologies and practices to transform their agricultural systems into more climate-resilient ones. Smallholder farmers will be trained to first develop Action Plans (AP) to reorient farmer behaviour and assist them in transforming the agriculture production system in a manner that is better adapted to factoring in the agro-ecological context and expected climate change impacts.

• Activity 1.1.1 Develop community-based action plans (APs) to transform agriculture with CR practices.
• Activity 1.1.2 Preparation of training materials to support implementation of the AP.
• Activity 1.1.3 Conduct training to create a pool of experts to demonstrate and propagate the CR technologies and practices.
• Activity 1.1.4 Provide advisory on CR technologies and practices.
• Activity 1.1.5 Strengthening and fostering tailored mechanization service providers for improved mechanization service delivery.
• Activity 1.1.6 Community-based monitoring and evaluation (CBME) of implementation

Output 1.2 Climate adaptive value chains developed by 4Ps and increased access to finance

This output involves improving and enhancing some value chains that are key for the project area and include rice, vegetable, chicken and aquaculture value chains, through the use of Public Private Producer Partnerships (PPPs) and Market Support Platforms (MSPs). The objective is to improve market access, climate adaptability, and ensure increased income for smallholders in the value chain.

• Activity 1.2.1 Value chain study and planning
• Activity 1.2.2 Public Private Producer Partnership Facility (PPP)
• Activity 1.2.3 Public Private Producer Partnership Facility (PPP)


---
**Page 159**
---

Output 1.3. Increased access to and use of climate information and advisory services for climate responsive agriculture planning

* Activity 1.3.1 Strengthening of agrometeorological stations in the project area
* Activity 1.3.2 Establishing a Level Coordination Platform for climate-informed agricultural advisories
* Activity 1.3.3 Expanding the capacity to deliver services
* Activity 1.3.4 Establishing the awareness raising systems and the awareness raising and capacity building of farmers and stakeholders in applying the services

Output 1.4. Increased resilience of farm road infrastructure to climate change

* Activity 1.4.1 Identification, selection and initial planning
* Activity 1.4.2 Technical survey and design considerations, preparation of cost estimation
* Activity 1.4.3 Improvement: 90 Kilometers of farm roads
* Activity 1.4.4 Construction of farm roads

Component 2: Irrigation Infrastructure for Increased Resilience

Component 2 is linked with Component 1 such that it facilitates the implementation of CR on farm crop and water management practices through improved field-level water supply delivery and drainage. It will focus on rehabilitating and modernizing irrigation and flood protection/drainage infrastructure in the six sub-projects, including irrigation and drainage canals, flood control embankments, and ponds, to provide high-efficiency climate-resilient irrigation systems that are better suited to both increasing flood and drought conditions.

Output 2.1 Modernized Irrigation schemes and ponds

* Activity 2.1.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading
* Activity 2.1.2 Implementation of infrastructure upgrading
* Activity 2.1.3 Preparation of canal O&M plans including application of ICT and SCADA for operation

Output 2.2 Flood-proofed and Improved Drainage

* Activity 2.2.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading
* Activity 2.2.2 Strengthening and construction of flood control and drainage infrastructures

Output 2.3 Farmers Water User Committees (FWUC) established and trained

* Activity 2.3.1 Formation of institutional strengthening of the FWUC
* Activity 2.3.2 Build technical capacities of FWUC for canal structure O&M

Output 2.4 Water information system (CIWS and SCADA) established and operational in sub-projects

* Activity 2.4.1 Preparing all processes for establishment of the SCADA and Water information system

Component 3. Institutional Strengthening

Output 3.1 Strengthened MOWRAM Capacity
- Activity 3.1.1 Strengthening River basin management and Water Accounting Systems
- Activity 3.1.2 Communicating with stakeholders
- Activity 3.1.3 Enhancing the Governance of Leaders of Commune, Village and WUGs and Gender Awareness

Output 3.2 Strengthened NCCDS at national and provincial level
- Activity 3.2.1 Strengthening of NCCDS at the national level
- Activity 3.2.2 Strengthening of the provincial teams

### 1.1.4 Project's Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

While the project will bring about an overall positive, long-term social and environmental impacts, some project activities (e.g., construction of water infrastructure for increased resilience) are anticipated to generate the following environmental and social risks and impacts:

#### Environmental Risks and Impacts
- Generation of noise and vibration due to construction operation
- Pollution of surface water (disturbance), soil (leakage of oil, chemicals…)
- Generation of solid, hazardous, domestic waste (temporary during construction stage)
- Potential impact on biodiversity at farming ecosystem and health of farmers due to crop intensification in the project area
- Occupational health and safety (for project workers, particularly contractors' workforce)
- Disruption of local livelihoods
- Road and traffic safety (for project workers, particularly contractors' workforce)
- Unplanned ordnance (associated with physical construction activities that involve ground-breaking, excavation)

Most of these risks and impacts are temporary, localized and small-scaled. Mitigation measures for these environmental risks and impacts are being proposed to avoid these impacts, including measures through construction measures. The project team is committed to minimize these environmental risks and impacts will be minimized during the construction phase – based on the following criteria:
- Technical standards and regulations must be abided by or followed.
- Disturbance to the livelihoods of the local people must be minimized.
- The project activities must be implemented in a socio-economically feasible manner.
- Construction equipment and methods must be environmentally friendly.
- Monitoring activities must be conducted on a regular basis.


---
**Page 161**

# Social Risks and Impacts

During project implementation, the following social risks and impacts are anticipated—at specific construction sites.

- Potential land expropriation (mostly linear impact for rehabilitating/construction of irrigation canals)
- Temporary restricted water access for farmers in target command area during construction phase
- Gender inequality (e.g., during the process of crop intensification)
- Sexual Exploitation and Abuse (SEA), Sexual Harassment (SH), Gender-Based Violence (GBV) due to labor influx
- Exclusion of vulnerable groups (e.g. ethnic minorities, poor people with disabilities, female-headed households e.g. widows, orphans, single mothers), etc.
- Disease transmission (due to labor and animal influxes; specific diseases are not specified for reasons)
- Road and traffic safety (during increased transportation activities during construction)
- Hunting, trading, and consumption of animals from the wild (due to labor influx)

## 1.1 Social Impact Measures

To ensure project-related social impacts are avoided/minimized/mitigated, the project has prepared the following documents:

- Resettlement Policy Framework (RPF)
- Indigenous People Planning Framework (IPPF)
- Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP)
- Gender Mainstreaming Action Plan
- Environmental, Social Climate Management Framework (ESCFM)
- Environmental, Social and Climate Impact Assessment (ESCA)

These documents have been developed in accordance with the relevant laws and regulations, the safeguards policy of the World Bank Group, the International Finance Corporation’s (IFC) Social (Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures), and relevant policies of GEF (Policy on Environmental and Social Safeguards, Policy on Gender Equality Principles and Guidelines for Engaging Indigenous Peoples).

The above documents will ensure anticipate, monitor, manage, and mitigate social risks and impacts as identified as proportional to the significance of the project’s environmental and social risks and impacts that are present during project design, pre-construction, during construction, and operational stages.

## 1.2 Rationale for Gender Assessment

The purpose of this gender assessment (GA) is to understand the status quo of key gender-related issues in the context of agricultural production/irrigation management in Cambodia, particularly at project level. Based on these understandings, actions for gender mainstreaming will be proposed, and monitoring and evaluation plans will be developed to ensure that gender equality is achieved. The GA will also identify entry points for the participation of beneficiaries (women and men) in project planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation.

The GA will identify gender gaps—through examining the situation of women and examining gender stereotypes, gender roles and responsibilities in farming practices, constraints to and opportunities for gender mainstreaming at different levels. The GA will also examine the impact of national laws, policies, national policies, and gender policies of the AIB, IAFD and GCF. Specific actions are being proposed to ensure gender mainstreaming actions will be implemented as an integral manner with appropriate project investments activities.

The gender action plan aims to:

1. Support climate change interventions and innovations through a holistic gender mainstreaming approach which means that more than 50% of the total budgetary will be women-driven activities.
2. Promote resilience through climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions, whereas minimizing social, gender-related and climate-related risks in all project activities; and
3. Contribute to reducing the gender gap caused by inherent social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities and limitations as well as potential impacts induced by the climate change in four project provinces.

# Methods

## Research Design

### Data Collection

Most quantitative data collection described below were conducted in 2022 by the Livelihoods and Vulnerability Survey Team. Information and charts that are used in this report are acknowledged.

#### Focus Group Discussions (FGD)

FGDs were conducted in small groups (7-8 persons each). FGD gathered people from similar backgrounds (e.g., ethnic groups, natural resource dependent) and experiences to discuss pre-defined topics related to their livelihoods, the impacts of climate change on these livelihoods, and the preparation of the required social and environmental safeguards documents. All FGDs were guided by moderators who are trained local government staff. The group facilitators introduced topics for discussion and helped guide participants in discussing them. The discussions were recorded verbatim and transcribed verbatim of local people on specific issues, particularly the range of their opinion and ideas, and any inconsistencies and variation that exists in their community with regards to their beliefs, knowledge, attitude and practices with regards to their livelihoods projections and their access to land and water resources to support their subsistence and long-term livelihoods.

| No. | Time | Number of Community meeting | Male | Female | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 26-30 June 2023 | 7 | 270 (82%) | 58 (18%) | 328 (100%) |

#### Key Informant Interview (KII)

KII aims to collect information about select pressing issues and problem in the target community – through individuals who are locally known as experts in the topics under question, and who have a broad understanding of the local context. These experts may play certain roles (e.g., production, consumption...) in a value chain that are likely to be supported by the project. One-hundred key informant interviews have been conducted by the Livelihoods and Vulnerability Survey Team.

#### Household Survey

Household surveys were administered to invited representatives of local households using questionnaires uploaded through personal mobile devices of local government staff who conducted the survey.

## 2.3 Data analysis

### 2.3.1 Qualitative analysis
Qualitative data obtained from focus group discussion and key informant interviews were analyzed using content analysis.

### 2.3.2 Quantitative analysis
Household survey data was collected through questionnaires prepared by the Livelihood and Vulnerability Survey (LVSS) module of KoboToolbox was selected because it supports offline data collection, and it offers functionality appropriate to the needs of this survey. Data collected on the field were submitted to KoboToolbox platform after each day's household interview. Data were cleaned using Microsoft Excel and were analyzed by the author using the statistical package IBM SPSS Statistics for Windows (version 28.0; Armonk, NY, USA). Descriptive statistics were collected for select questions in the questionnaire to describe the sample distribution. Various other statistical methods were used as appropriate for additional analysis. For this study, all significance levels were set to p < 0.05.

# 3. Results of Gender Assessment

## 3.1 Overview at Country Level

### 3.1.1 Background on gender issues

**Social norms for gender:**

Cambodia is ranked 92th on the The Global Gender Gap Index 2021 rankings. This is an improvement compared to The Global Gender Gap Report of 2012 where it was at 107th rank. Gender norms are often considered a key underlying cause of gender inequalities in Cambodia. Underlying gender inequalities are cultural and traditional norms that limit girls’ and women’s choices and options[1]. Back in history, traditional codes (Chabey Seay/ Proh) have been used to define the roles of men and women within the family. These codes include “men to be the head of household” and advice to women “to maintain peace within the home, walk talk softly, obey and respect her husband’s decisions”. The codes also emphasize that women should not make decisions on their own behalf or for protecting women and making decisions[1]. For women and men who have never heard of Chabey Seay/ Proh, they hold the views that are consistent with those codes – quoting common proverbs that reflect the same beliefs[2].

The traditional roles of women as caregivers and homemakers are still widely accepted. The expectations for a “virtuous Khmer woman” are still communicated which affect how girls perceive about themselves that shape the gender norms in the next generation. The ideals described in Chabey Seay/Proh influence the socialization process, especially among young girls[3]. These codes also emphasize women’s contributions to maintaining women in a subordinate position[4]. Although these codes have been no longer taught or practiced in schools and communities, they are still widely accepted by Cambodians. In fact, social expectations for Khmer women are reinforced and negotiated in day-to-day interactions in households, communities and workplaces. Women entrepreneurs face negative judgments by their family [54%], and community members [30%] when they start a business[5].

Women are traditionally responsible for household expenditure including expense control. When it comes to loan, they also take on the responsibility of seeking out local’s credit and assume greater responsibility for loan repayments than their husbands. However, limited credit access and high interest rate is commonly known[6].

Gender discrimination is deeply embedded in, and reinforced by, social attitudes[7]. According to the United Nation (2022), women/gender norms are related to vastly unequal distribution of unpaid domestic and care work which, in addition, undermines, on average, 1/3 of the work. During COVID-19 pandemic, gender inequalities have been exacerbated as women bear a disproportionate burden of unpaid care and domestic work[8].

proportion of men and women spent increased time on domestic work whereas almost 20% of employed women reported that their partners did not provide any additional assistance. It is noted under such circumstances, elderly women are often assigned to unpaid work in the absence of other support. The need to maintain livelihoods by carrying on more unpaid responsibilities keeps many women in vulnerable work, and it is a constant source of stress and strain for them. Women also face barriers when they attempt to assume leadership roles[12]. However, in practice, the household division of labor has been changing: women engage in a broader range of tasks, including those normally associated with men[13]. Prevailing cultural norms encourage young men to seek paid employment outside their homes and communities. Despite the fact that it is estimated there are more paid job opportunities for women than for men, such as in garment industry, people still feel that young women should remain in their villages[14].

Women assume a wider range of domestic and mendicant roles than men. They also wake up before men and go to bed after them. Women generally responsible for 50% of the household work, including care of dependents and the sick. People in households, mostly women and girls, spend from one to two hours each day collecting water for the family's needs. Women and girls work longer hours than men and boys. Despite that unpaid domestic labor is a major barrier to promoting gender equality in Cambodia, it is rarely addressed by public policies or development efforts[15].

Labor Force in Key Regions

The Cambodian labor force participation (LFP) rate for both the male and female population (aged 15 to 64) is high compared with other countries in the Lao region. Cambodian LFP rates are similar to those of Vietnam, perhaps Cambodia's closest regional peer in terms of structure of their economies[16]. Agriculture plays an important role in Cambodia's employment landscape. About 40% of all employed men and women work in the sector. The share of agricultural workers has been declining over time, as it was also observed in Thailand and, it is noted that during the period 2011-2016, there was a rapid shift out of agriculture because women moved into manufacturing while men moved into construction (at a similar rate). Men also shifted out of agriculture to other sectors albeit they moved out of agriculture as a faster rate – 17.2% compared to 11.5 percent of women – and moving into construction (from 7% to 14 percent) and services (from 20:3 to 26.9%).[17] (See also "Internal Migration" below).

# Gender-Based Violence

One in four Cambodian women have experienced physical or sexual violence from an intimate partner, and more than one in three have experienced gender-based violence at work. The fact that women are men entitled to sex regardless of consent, directly contributes to gender-based violence. These harmful gender norms are perpetuated by the media, which often portrays women as passive victims. As a result, men reducing gender-based violence was slower, or reversed. There is no national data available on this issue.

Studies indicated that sexual harassment affects even more women than domestic or partner violence. Public spaces and public transport are common sites for sexual harassment.11 Studies on sexual harassment found that 89% of all respondents (not just those who reported harassment) felt unsafe working or studying at night and 24%.

Women farmers currently have limited capacity and opportunities to diversify agricultural practices and lessen dependency on climate sensitive and stressed natural resources; limited access to knowledge regarding new agricultural production and post-production techniques and technologies; and limited mobility to avoid disasters and other shocks that may affect their livelihoods.

Vulnerability is further heightened for women in agriculture, as they are often unpaid family workers with few options for coping with disasters. **Recommendations for climate change mitigation and women’s economic empowerment**

The 4th East Asia Gender Equality Ministerial Meeting entitled “Building Resilience to Economic Crisis and Moving Forward”, held in Sem Reap in November 2017[1], made the following specific recommendations for Cambodia on a green economy:

- Women entrepreneurs: Mechanisms (such as women’s business associations) should be strengthened to promote entrepreneurship, access to business-related information, green technology and financial services.
- Organic and sustainable agriculture: The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries should develop an organic agriculture policy framework and program. Extension services should proactively target female farmers and women's groups.
- Green procurement: Public sector procurement should prioritize-wholesome, nutritious food products and services such as locally produced fruits and vegetables (free) food and other products for government offices, schools, hospitals and embassies. Priority should be given to women-owned enterprises.
- Public Private Partnership (PPP): Promotion of PPP will be a key strategy for promoting green growth. Regulatory frameworks need to be adjusted to provide appropriate incentives. Sector-based policy research is needed to identify the most important opportunities.
- Women’s entrepreneurship: The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries should facilitate the development of green products and services, including finance, skills, and especially knowledge and information on how to start, manage and grow a competitive, sustainable green business. Women should be widely encouraged to start and improve their own businesses as entrepreneurs, which urgently needed productivity increase innovation and employment could be generated by and for them.

# Challenges

## Gender mainstreaming in CC investments is new to the donor community, NGOs, civil society organizations or other sectors.

Limited technical and financial resources limit the planned initiatives.

### 3.1.3 National Policies related to Gender & Climate Change

#### National Legal Frameworks

Cambodia has adopted a legal framework that aims to promote the role of women in part of national efforts towards economic empowerment for women and gender equality at the constitutional level, anti-discrimination laws, and labor law. Article 36 of the Constitution recognizes the value of women's work in the home as equal to that performed outside of the home. The Labor Law, which was adopted in 1997, specifies provisions that protect female labor.


---
**Page 170**
---

### Ratification of international conventions

In terms of international conventions, the Royal Government of Cambodia has ratified 13 International Labor Organisations' conventions (see table below). Cambodia is also a signatory to various United Nations' human rights covenants and conventions. These include the Convention for Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, which was ratified in 1992. However, it is noted that the C06 - Night Work of Young Persons (Industry) Convention, 1919 (No. 6) has not been yet in force.

Despite the presence of anti-discrimination legislation, there is no provision for equal remuneration for work of equal value, although equal pay for the same work is included in the Constitution*. Provisions for minimum wages to be paid have been introduced by the government, targeting shoe industries, the sectors where a large number of female workers are employed. However, the adequacy of the minimum wage is still open to question. Many women working in the domestic, tourist, and entertainment sectors are not covered with regards to minimum wages.

In terms of land ownership, Land Law (2001) stipulates that women have equal rights to land ownership. Overall, 70% of all land titles are issued jointly between husband and wife. In practice, however, there are examples of gendered land ownership in which men take over the legal title to land even when women have an equal right to land ownership as per Land Law (2001). It seems that women are not aware of their rights and have little recourse to legal advice.

**Cambodia's Rectangular Strategy (Phase IV)**

The Government of Cambodia's Rectangular Strategy Phase IV (2018-2033) provides a framework for policies and strategies concerning the education and promoting the economic empowerment of women. The objectives of RS III relate to economic growth, employment particularly for youth, and strengthening institutional capacity and governance. The four strategic rectangles of the RS IV are:

* * *


---
**Page 171**
---

### Rectangle 1 - Human resource development: 1). Improving the quality of education, science and technology; 2). Vocational training; 3). Improving public healthcare and nutrition; and 4). Strengthening gender equality and social protection.

### Rectangle 2 - Economic Diversifications: 1). Improving logistics system and enhancing transport, energy and digital connectivity; 2). Developing key and new sources of economic growth; 3). Readiness for digital economy and industrial revolution 4.0; and 4). Promoting financial and banking sector development.

### Rectangle 3 - Promotion of private sector development and employment: 1). Job market development, 2). Innovation of SME and entrepreneurship; 3). Public-private partnership; and 4). Enhanced competitiveness.

### Rectangle 4 - Incorporating sustainable development: 1). Promotion of agricultural and rural development; 2). Strengthening sustainability management of natural and cultural resources; 3). Strengthening management of urbanization; and 4). Promoting environmental sustainability and readiness for climate change.

**Mainstream Gender and Climate Change (2018-2030)**

The threat of climate change, manifested by the increase of droughts, storms or floods, has been recognized as a key global development challenge. Not only does climate change have broad impacts on the natural environment but also it is a major factor in poverty reduction and economic development. The gravity of these impacts varies across regions, income groups and occupations, as well as between women and men. In Cambodia, the impacts of climate change are crucial for agriculture and the lives of mostly rural communities. As the majority of Cambodians live on subsistence farming, any changes to their livelihoods will have a direct impact on their ability to adapt to climate change. Poor management of natural resources directly threatens people's livelihoods, in both the short and long term. Poor management of natural resources has had serious impacts on the local economy, as most communities rely on natural forest cover; a majority of their products are derived from these forests.

The RGC recognizes that the rural poor of Cambodia, the majority of whom are women, are more vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their high dependence on agriculture and natural resources. This vulnerable group is susceptible to diseases due to their limited resources and capacity to adapt to climate change impacts, including lack of access to health services and information. The RGC has recognized that a cross-cutting approach is needed into climate response measures, such as into existing policies and laws and sector climate change strategy plans (SCCPs) in order for this cross-cutting issue to be supported by all government agencies, especially the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF), the National Environment Agency (NEA), research and academic institutions and the private sector. In response, the Government has put in place **Strategic Objective 2: Reduce sectoral, regional, gender vulnerability and health risks to climate change impacts**.

Generally, all policies, strategies and projects dealing with climate change are either about climate change adaptation or climate change mitigation. It is important to draw a distinction between the two. Climate change adaptation aims to reduce vulnerabilities and increase resilience to the impacts of climate change. This is done by strengthening national capacity for vulnerability assessment and risk management; and by increasing national efforts to integrate climate change adaptation measures into development planning and ecosystem management practices. The RGC has developed a climate change adaptation strategy as part of its response to the Nairobi Work Programme on Adaptation, Vulnerability and Adaptation in a program developed by the UN Environment


---
**Page 172**
---

Convention for Climate Change (UNFCCC) to help countries understand climate change impacts and adapt to climate change.

Climate change is expected to compound and amplify development challenges, stresses and problems in Cambodia, further affecting poor and marginalized people, particularly women and children. Women have disproportionate access to financial resources, land, natural resources, education, health, rights and other development services that are essential for effective adaptation to climate change. For the vast majority of women in Cambodia, building resilience through increased access to information or knowledge, recovering from the devastating effects of environmental disasters is nearly impossible[1].

### 3.1.4 Existing implementation arrangements

The institutions to support women's economic empowerment include the Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MOWA) and gender focal points within other government agencies (GFPA). In each line ministry that prepare and implement sectoral gender mainstreaming action plans (GMAPs), in 2013, MOWA launched a Millennium Development Goal (MDG) Acceleration Framework Cambodia Action Plan focused on women’s economic empowerment to contribute to the achievement of MDGs. The plan identifies three main areas of intervention: (i) providing training for jobs that are consistent with market demands; (ii) ensuring that women have the capacity to lead and grow their micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises and can move from the informal sector into the formal economy; and (iii) increasing access to credit. The plan is focused on poor women. In 2014 MOWA launched the Cambodia Gender Assessment and a 5 Year Strategic Plan for Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment (Nearly Ratnaka IV), which includes policy recommendations on Women's Economic Empowerment, including microfinance, access to land, and climate change adaptation.

MOWA also manages a network of 13 women’s development centers (WDCs) nationwide, which are vocational centers offering training programs in areas such as handicraft production, hairdressing, tailoring, and food processing. WDCs face a host of challenges and are not reaching their full potential as centers that promote women's economic empowerment. The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF) has proposed a Gender Mainstreaming Policy and Strategy in Agriculture 2016–2020 which includes objectives relating to greater participation of women in the civil service; enhanced capacity to integrate gender; increased ability of rural women to access and manage resources; and improved access for women to credit. The strategy also aims at increasing women’s income, but specific mention is given to assistance with social land concessions, participation in the private sector, participation in village and community groups, and access to credit, and extension services. The CFAVC GAP is designed to align to and support the operationalisation of the MAFF Gender Policy.

3.2.2 Education

Nearly half of respondents (46.0%) completed primary school, followed by secondary level (21.7%), high school (8.0%), university (1.7%), and vocational training (0.4%). Some respondents never attended school or never attended. According to a focus group discussion that was carried out under the Livelihood and Vulnerability survey, elderly people typically had very limited/no access to education due to the history of Cambodia. Younger people have been able to find relevant education to catch up. Younger generation is different. They took the opportunity to learn and have higher education. Some households have sent their children to Phnom Penh or abroad for education.

3.2.1 Assets
- Houses

Of the 630 Hts, 96% own a house. Only 4% said they are living in their children's/relatives' house. For those who own a house, house status is as follows:

| No | House Type | Percentage |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Wooden/Iron on walls (corrugate iron roof/flooring cement) | 29% |
| 2 | Semi-permanent houses (concrete or brick/tiles) | 28% |
| 3 | Simple huts (corrugated wall wood and roofing sheets) | 28% |
| 4 | Permanent house with one or more rooms | 15% |
| 5 | temporary houses | 0.48% |

In terms of land status for the house, 46% of respondents have land title registered. 49% are still under process of registration with local authorities but their land is recognised as legal. Some people are using land that are not under their ownership for temporary farming.

- Land access

Most survey participants (95.7%, n=603) own the land that they currently use. Only 2.4% (n=15) use lands of their relatives. The remaining (1.6%) use land from their children (n=5), parents (n=3), state (n=3), and renting (n=4). The average land area owned/used by surveyed households is 5,688 m² (SD=720 t).

# 3.2.4 Occupation Overall job pattern.

72.86% of respondents (n=459) are farmers. 99% of farmer respondents said they grow rice and no other crop. Man crop takes place during the wet season albeit slight differences between project provinces. It is noted that man-crop as well as paddy is in dry season (see graph below).

## Job of household heads

64% of households head in the survey (n=407) do farming, followed by unpaid work (10.5%, n=66), paid workers (8.1%, n=53), retail service (7.3%, n=45), government employees (5.9%, n=37), etc. For those who are involved in farming, it is noted that in addition to cultivating rice, 73% indicated they are involved in animal husbandry to earn income. The most common animals raised for this purpose were chickens (60%), pigs (28%), and other livestock. 48% of respondents do animal husbandry for income. About 23% raise livestock for home consumption.

It is noted that 31% of household heads (n=201) do two jobs compared to 68% who only do 1 job. The reason why they do the second jobs because they want to earn more income “to meet family’s expenditure”.

## Job of household members

While 64% of households do farming to earn income, and half of them (37%) do the second job, only 50% of household members are involved in farming. These members are usually children and tend to work in non-farm work such as work for the private sector (15.6%), services (4.8%), and government (2.8%). It is noted that 43.4% of household members are not income earners. They are retired, doing household chores, or jobless.

Source: Livelihood and Vulnerability Data 2022. Graph by author

3.2.6 Current practices

Mechanization due to internal migration over the past few decades for better income, the rural labor force has been reduced, leaving room for increased use of machinery to compensate for traditional human labor. While 32% think it is used frequently. Harvesting needs more machinery use (75%) compared to land preparation (65%). It is noted that 40% of surveyed households use machines for heavy tasks such as land preparation and harvesting, the remainder (40%) still use buffalo and manpower for the heavy works. Where needed, families reach out to neighbors to ask for labor help and return to them when they need [labor exchanges].

Use of new farming technologies: 65.3% of surveyed households (n=119) have been using traditional rice cultivation practices. With regards to applying new farming techniques, 21.7% (n=10) use occasionally, 11.6% (n=57) use frequently, and 1.2% used very frequently. 61.2% (n=299 think mechanization is better whereas 38.7% (n=204) think it is not. It is noted that 65.5% of surveyed households have adopted improved farming practices find it better. They think that if crop is better because of appropriate use of fertilizers (33.8%, n=40), pesticides (29.1%, n=34), use of machinery (12.2%, n=147), training (10%, n=122), and using improved seeds (6.5%, n=7). It is noted that 65% of surveyed households have adopted improved seeds growth of crop (42.8%, n=330), improve crop yield (43.6%, n=321), and improve soil fertility (14.6%, n=113).

Climate change and new technologies

Most surveyed households (50%, n=568) are not aware of the potential impact of climate change on their agricultural production. However, 40% (n=256) think they have experienced the impact of climate change and the remainder (not yet experienced it. 63% (n=397) have never heard of improved farming technology that helps increase their income.

Training Needs

When asked about the support/training that women need, half (48%) preferred training in agriculture/animal husbandry and finance (24.7). However, 37% of households don’t know what they need in terms of knowledge to support their livelihood development.

**Page 182**
---

3.2.7 Income level

In this survey, monthly income of the surveyed households was collected. Monthly household income refers to the total income that all members of the household could earn each month, on average, excluding children. It is noted that all members of a household are expected to contribute equally in order to cover common expenditure. The level of individual contribution varies depending on family. Below is the distribution of monthly household income across four project provinces. The average monthly household income is 152.8 USD (SD=126.7).

There is a fairly strong positive relationship between household size and income\footnote{r=.51, p<.001}, and a strong positive relationship between income—expenditure\footnote{r=.74, p<.001}. However, the data (graph below) suggests that surveyed households appear to confine their expenditure within the income they are capable of earning. Households that earn more tend to spend only basic goods and save money.

Figure 12: Expenditure vs income (Monthly at Household level)

**Page 185**
---

The reasons why both income and expenditure are unstable are due to the variation between wet season and dry season, according to the explanation from focus group discussion and key informant interviews conducted by the Livelihood and Vulnerability Survey Team. In particular, during wet season, key expense include income lower than expenditure (24.3%), medical costs (19.5%) family expenses (38.7%), children's education (13.4%), loan repayment (10.4%), investment in agricultural production (9.5%), flooding (2.4%), and less cultivation (1.5%).

During dry season reasons given, key reasons include income lower than expenditure (19%), children's education (14.8%), social expenses (13.6%), medical care (10.4%), rice drought damages (9.2%), debt payment (8%), lack of irrigation water (6.8%), payment for fertilizer cost (1.8%), and low product income (1.5%).

### 3.2.9 Social networking

All (n=189) surveyed households have had no experience working in a community farmer group. 68.3% of them (n=130) are not involved in any group activities at village/commune level. Most women in the country use their own resources to start their business, or rely on informal sources and savings to expand their businesses. They have no choice since women are underserved by banks.

### 3.2.10 Loans

At the county level, according to the Cambodia Microfinance Association (CMA), 80% of their clients are women. However, loan size offered by these institutions are typically small whereas interest rate is higher than commercial banks.

In this survey, 28.9% of surveyed households (n=189) got a loan over the past one year. The purpose of the loan (in descending order) is for house repair, investment in business, living expenses, purpose of consumables, and to buy land [See graph below]. Most of them will try to take a loan from a legitimate bank, and not the local lender because of the high interest.

### 3.3 Gender Analysis

#### 3.3.1 Farming Activities

For those who are involved in farming as their main livelihood activities, virtually all of them (97.6%, n=453) dedicate their full-time farming. The male workers spend, on average, 59.4% of their time farming vis-à-vis their female counterparts who spend 40.5% of their time farming.

In rice farming, traditionally, the first stages of rice cultivation are male-designated and the latter stages female-designated. Male farmers are responsible for sowing and transplanting, while seeding preparation and weeding are commonly assigned to women**. All other jobs such as transplanting, uprooting, harvesting, and marketing are

**Page 190**

generally shared task[1]. However, given the migration of men for better income, mechanization has become more popular. As a result, in households with men engaging women undertake all farming tasks: land preparation, sowing, threeding, and recruitment of labor, farm management, harvesting, and selling. Growing secondary crops such as vegetables also increases. These secondary crops are mainly grown in home gardens, increasing nutritional status for households as well as households' food security[1].

In livestock, most rural women (70%) are involved in raising small livestock such as chickens and ducks, and pigs. These animals provide a source of income and food for the household. However, for most poor women, livestock serves as a primary form of saving, as well as insurance against economic shocks due to accidents, illness, death and natural disasters. However, as livestock in Cambodia faces rapid market restructuring, poor livestock producers are increasingly unable to access markets[2]. This is because they lack the ability to meet requirements regarding to sanitary standards, animal transportation, storage standards, etc., required by modern structured markets.[3] In general, women raise poultry, mea and cattle and pigs.

For household chores, women still do 90% of the work. During COVID-19 breakout, women spent even increased time on domestic tasks[4]. Women are also responsible for raising grandchildren whose mothers have migrated[5]. In the project area, similarly, in families where female labor has to work in nearby garment factories, or migrants for paid work, domestic works are transferred to young girls and old people. According to the National Demographic and Health Survey (2020-2021), when men were asked about who makes decisions regarding how their earnings are used, 48% of married men aged [15–49] reported that they jointly make decisions with their wives[6], 46% said that their wives mainly make decisions, and 6% said that they alone make the decisions. However, when women were asked about who makes their own earnings for their employment participate in making decisions about the use of their earnings whereas 63% make these decisions alone[7].

In the opinion of the surveyed households, women should have the following education, followed by freedom of decision making, good husband, income opportunity, and own saving.

- Access to Agricultural Extension Service

It is estimated that only 10 percent of the Cambodian farmers with access to agricultural extension services are women[68]. The constraints affecting rural Cambodian women's ability to have adequate access to agricultural extension services include: lack of information on service provision; insensitivity to the level of literacy; limited childcare options; household responsibilities; mobility, and socio-cultural characteristics[69]. Men are still traditionally regarded as household head and do not automatically receive news or information[70].

- Market Access

Women face a number of disadvantages: lower mobility, lower level of literacy, less access to training, less access to market information, and less access to productive resources. Lower financial literacy of women and travel safety are identified as the main gender gaps in Cambodia to access to markets for women[61].

### 4. GENDER ACTION & SOCIAL INCLUSION PLAN

From the above analysis, key gaps with regards to participation of women in farming activities and household chores have been identified. To reduce the gender gap, it is important that barriers to reducing the gap be proposed. The following section identifies five key barriers to women's participation in project activities which form to address the barriers while maximizing the opportunities. The section below identified key barriers, opportunities, and priorities in promoting participation of women in the economic empowerment process. The following table shows the gender action plan for each sub-project. The other sub-project activities will adopt the same guidance and incorporate the women folk in the subproject activities.

#### 4.1 Key Barriers to Participation of Women

This section highlighted five key barriers that may potentially affect the participation of women in project activities. The barriers are inter-related thus need to be addressed together to reduce the constraints.

**Barrier 1 — Gender norms**

In Cambodian cultural contexts, which are based on social norms, have long shaped how rural men and women divide their responsibilities and time for household chores and income generation activities. This affects the participation of women in project activities. The gender roles are defined by traditional gender-based labor division between women and men based on their physical and reproductive health. While men are generally responsible for physically heavy works in farming, including works that are considered harmful to health (e.g., carrying heavy loads), women are responsible for household chores and income generation. Because of these functions, women are involved in farm work that is suitable physically for them at home such as home gardening, weeding, harvesting the rice crop, etc. Women have a corresponding pattern of time representation in family for tasks that happen outside the home (e.g., community works) with more social interaction compared to their female counterparts (See opportunities and priority for project to overcome this barrier in the next section).

**Barrier 2 — Household chore**

The gender norm defines that domestic works are key women's responsibility. Traditional codes (Chab Srey) Prohibit men from doing household chores, which is considered a woman's job. The Chab Srey also prohibits a woman to maintain peace at home and respect her husband’s [sic]. As such, women spend most of their time at home, taking care of domestic works that are unpaid. Chore work does not only take them most of their time off the farm but also prevent them from participating in other activities like attending community meetings, agricultural training, visiting friends or relatives, or keeping up with their peers in the neighborhood. Because of their work burden, only a few women have the time to join FVWUs and village and commune development activities, particularly in water and irrigation management or in disaster risk reduction. This can negatively affect women's adaptive capacity, jeopardizing the resilience of poor rural households.


[44]


---
**Page 196**
---

For families that are involved in home gardening and animal husbandry, household chore and home farming reinforces the labor stereotypes. This keeps many boys of rural, deprived communities opportunities to update their general knowledge and skills. It also refreshes the skills they use daily for their agricultural activities and animal husbandry. This forms a vicious cycle that maintains the existence of gender norms (See opportunities and priority for projects to overcome this barrier in the next sections).

**Barrier 3 – Human capital**

Education, adult literacy and skills are low for both men and women, and particularly lower among women. Income from agriculture is often insufficient to cover basic needs, and many families depend on remittances from a very young age. Therefore, they tend to drop out from school as they move through primary, secondary and high school. According to the 2021 national socioeconomic survey, main reasons why persons aged 47–65 years did not do well in school (38%), did not continue education after primary school (39%) or did not complete high school (33%). Most of them (81%) did not have access to education. Only 20% of women and men (18%) do not do well in school (15.6%), do not go to continue education (8.3%), and do not Help with household chores (7.9%). Surprisingly, the rates are higher among females when it comes to work to contribute to family income (20% for women compared to 14% of men) and to help in household chores (56% for women versus 48% for male, respectively). Early marriage in rural areas is still common. This reduces the opportunities of young females to complete their education and acquire skills that are needed for productive employment (See Barriers 2 and 2). Adult literacy is overall 85% but its level varies by location. However, adult literacy among women is lower than men – 80% and 90%, respectively. In rural area, adult literacy is even lower – 75.3% and 86.7% for women and men, respectively. The gender gap in education is also reflected in the level of skills. According to the survey, more than two-thirds of households in the lowest income quintile depend on agriculture. However, agriculture is mainly held back by low productivity due to lack of access to credit, poor infrastructure, and the predominance use of low value rice strains 48. Farming skills that are associated with old farming practices are also listed (See opportunities and priority for projects to overcome this barrier in the next sections).

**Barrier 3 – Human capital**

Although the gender wage gap has gone down - from 26% in 2017 to 9% in 2020, the wage gap between women and men remains a challenge, representing an enduring barrier for women jobseekers, and for both men and women who are employed. Women earn less than their male counterparts because they are more likely found to work less hours than men in wage employment. But this does not take into account the time women typically spend on their households chores 49. In the rural area, in general, and in four project provinces, in particular, women have a higher share of unpaid labor compared with men. This is because many farms are dominant. Given this, when searching for jobs, women tend to look for jobs near home while men can travel far to work as migrant workers. In the context of this project, it is likely that women, including women in urban areas who have access to land and resources, will be more interested in working on small farms (See those who have access to produce land). There is a high level out-migration of young people (60-80% in each project community) (See opportunities and priority for project intervention in the next sections).

**Barrier 3 – Human capital**

Traditional farming practices - characterized by dominant use of old varieties, low mechanization, old crop caring techniques, monoropics, and local market, are among potential constraints to projects’ effort in promoting adoption of new technologies and other modern factors of production (e.g. more crops per year, short crop duration, better crop care), environment protection, loan, and market access. Due to this, it is also important to note that since 30-50% of rice are retained for home use, farmers may encounter challenges


---
**Page 197**
---

In adoption of new varieties that they may not be interested in, caring new rice varieties. They may also be concerned about how they will pay for the strains, market, and how to use it effectively. New technology that will come along with new varieties, shorter crop duration, labor shortage, and demand and how to ensure better selling prices. Trial and adoption of new technology package may take a few years: from Knowledge, Persuasion, Decision, Implementation, and Confirmation[10]. To achieve the project goal, project activities are designed in such a way that women farmers will be involved at all levels. Since most farmers don't change the current farming practice overnight, it is important that the project walk the farmers through a well-designed learning and adoption process, which needs to be facilitated with project interventions.

### 4.1 Potential Risks to achieving gender related goals

- Gender-based violence (GBV) at the Hlilekwa development activities where women will participate
- Gender gap in decision-making processes where both men and women will be participating and where women will be participating for the first time due to the project activities
- Increase of sexual abuse for women and children living near the project site due to the influx of outside workers
- Losing of jobs for women due to increased mechanization

These potential risks will be mitigated through strong gender inclusion in all project-related activities, continuous awareness generation activities, training, and capacity development of both men and women farmers. The GBV will be addressed through special training and awareness to the women farmers along with their children. All other risks will be mitigated as per the mitigation strategies outlined in section 4.2.

### 4.2 Opportunities for Gender Mainstreaming into Project Activities

The project offers a unique opportunity where participation of women in project design and implementation is subject to monitoring and evaluation. This ensures the target on gender mainstreaming as do different project activities are set (as baseline) and monitored for changes (mid-term and end-term). Although 73% of surveyed households have shared household chores, this is not a universal situation across all households. There is a chance for developing more in animal husbandry and other farming activities such as cash crop income from post-harvest (e.g., straw, mushroom production).

**Opportunity 1 - Increasing share of household chore between men and women**

While household division of labor is a key responsibility of women at national level, there has been an increased share of household chores, particularly among households where demand for job/income necessitates adjustment to traditional intra-household labor division. This is indicated during COVID-19 pandemic when men participated in more domestic work while women were working outside the home (e.g., informal sector/food factories (including women who migrate and those who work in nearby communes)). This suggests that there has been a gentle shift in labor division in situations where adjusted workload between men and women in a family is essential to maintaining/improving family income. The willingness of men to share household chores is fundamental to reducing time and workload a woman typically takes on daily basis. This fosters women with


---
**Page 198**
---

additional time for self-care, child development, learning opportunities, and new income opportunities such as attending project training on new farming technologies, financial management, and taking up new jobs created in the value chain of agricultural products, particularly the agro-processing sector.

Opportunity 2 – Female are increasingly involved in household decision making

Though gender norms traditionally limit women's participation in decision making at household and community levels, empirical evidence suggests a new opportunity: while women's participation in public decisions (community meetings, village assemblies) has increased significantly over time, their involvement at the household level, particularly those related to income/financial management and children development. This suggests that men have increasingly recognized the role of women in their family well-being, especially in child care/development and financial management. The evidence also shows that women's participation in decision making has increased since 2013. Women who participated in deciding selling prices of their farm produce – jointly with their husbands albeit their participation their time and effort is less than men – have seen a significant increase in income from the sale of their produce (thanks to project investment) will generally double income from rice production and reinforce the existing roles of women but will require more participation of women in the decision making process, particularly for household decisions that directly affect the productivity value chain of agricultural production.

Opportunity 3 – Increase from migration: waged jobs and migration

Poverty (exacerbated by natural disasters) have left rural people with no choice but to migrate. This is especially true to migrant who don't have sufficient land for farming and monthly income cannot cover expenditures18. Data has shown that women are more likely than men to be forced into migration, particularly in the last few years remarkably over the past decade. Of 53% of rural women who moved to Phnom Penh, 32.2% work as garment workers, 23.6% as small business owners, 11.1% as domestic workers, and 10.3% as service/entertainment workers. The project area is located in the Kampong Speu province where there are many garment factories. In a project area where a high level of women participation in garment factories already exists (e.g at Krapsa Traume School of Khang Phaney Subdistrict in Oudong district, Kampong Speu), there would be a significant lack of women's participation in decision making and income generation activities. The project will provide training to increase the number of rice crops (from one to two crops a year) will increase income for all farming households, including households in which women worked full time in nearby factories. Increased mechanization also frees up labor intensity workers and reduces physical works of women in rice farming.

Opportunity 4 – Project activities that benefit young female farmers

Various studies suggested that income gaps are not common among youth households (compared to income gaps in households headed by persons more than 65 years of age). The openness of youth facilitating shifts of household responsibilities from men to women is also a positive sign. However, there remains a lot of work with regards to gender equality. This will be a great opportunity to maintain this momentum since intervention among youth to minimize gender gaps, and effects. This does not only serve as a good example at the local level but also prepares for transgenerational discussion of gender equality.

Opportunity 5 – Project activities that increase women participation in project activities

The project offers a unique opportunity where participation of women in project design and implementation is subject to monitoring and evaluation. This ensures the target on gender mainstreamings as to different project


---
**Page 199**
---

activities are set (as baseline) and monitored for changes (mid-term and endterm). Although 73% of surveyed households are on annual landholdings, these activities serve as family’s saving as income is low. There is still a chance for development in animal husbandry and other farming activities such as cash crops, income from post harvest (e.g., straw, mushroom production).

### 4.3 Priorities for Gender Mainstreaming

#### Priority 1 – Alleviating women's workload and taking into account their labour needs and constraints.

Women are traditionally responsible for most household chores. This unpaid work takes them out of or most of their time in a day but also restricts them from learning a chance to upgrade their farming skills which are essential for improved crop yield, and for being accepted into a value chain. To free up women gradually from traditional roles, it is important that they have more time to participate in training, community meetings, cooking, home gardening, and animal husbandry. When women have more time to participate in training, community meetings, and farming activities, they contribute to increasing family income – through optimized labor and resource use. It is also important for women to be involved in decision-making processes at the household level and promote mindset and behavioral changes among rural households, towards greater sharing of household chores and responsibilities is essential. Promoting women's access to water for irrigation, livelihood activities and domestic purposes will help them to participate more actively in farming activities. Access to credit also contributes to alleviate women’s workload in farming and household activities. It is important that the technologies delivered are informed by considerations for women’s labour and productive needs.

In addition, training and extension activities, particularly those targeting women, should be delivered during off farm season, or during time when work load is low. It is also important to be sensitive in arranging the timing of such trainings so as not to interfere with household chores. The timing of such trainings is critical and it is important to be sensitive to this aspect. Meetings and training should be organized at a time during the day when it does not clash with the time of the day when demands on the women for domestic chores is highest. This is to facilitate sharing of responsibilities within households, especially in rural areas where women are often responsible for their home for training and meetings. Training content and capacity building events need to be designed based on women’s needs and aspirations. As such, these should be gender sensitive, e.g., typologies of technologies, timing of training events, etc.

#### Priority 2 – Improve women's skills, and access to financial resources for Improved income and resilience

Improving women’s skills that are essential to their family’s income is important to economic empowerment that the project intended to foster. Skills be selected for training, development and usage should be based on the needs of the target households, particularly the female members of the target groups. Nonetheless, it is important to note that women have different access to resources and technologies including agricultural technology, including promoting access to irrigation and mechanization. Involvement of women in agricultural training will vary depending on the context and household livelihood characteristics. It is clear that women from farming households who work in nearby garment factories are more likely to be involved in agricultural training compared to women from farming households who work in nearby garment factories. It is also equally important to support women from farming households to develop non-farm skills to diversify their income sources, and to provide them with access to credit for the purchase of inputs or equipment other than production itself. Access to loans and grants are equally important to facilitate application of new skills for income generation and small enterprise development Local financial service provider could be


---
**Page 200**
---

supported/encouraged to ensure women participation is a sufficient and necessary condition to quality for a pro-poor loan. Grant schemes should also include special conditions and requirements to facilitate women's participation.

To increase income from rice cultivation, women and men farmers need to adopt new rice varieties that are high-yielding (See Boxer No. 5 above), and increase the number of crops from existing one crop per year at least two crops per year. Gender work is needed to ensure that both women and men are trained on new agricultural practices and technologies. This will also help in increasing the adoption rate of new varieties, since decisions on adoption of new rice varieties, crops and mechanization are made within the household. Increased use of machinery by both women and men is important. This is to increase ensured rice production at commune level due to increased number of crops per year (See Boxer No. 5 above) and better land management practices.

Women's involvement in vegetable gardening and animal husbandry (e.g., chicken, duck raising) should be promoted since these works can be done at home which is suitable for women and increase additional income and source of food for children. Women's participation in livestock rearing and poultry farming should also be promoted by supporting women's involvement in off-farm activities, such as processing and value-addition as well as investments in green products and women-led green enterprises.

### Priority 3 — Promoting women’s participation and leadership in community-based planning, decision-making and implementation

Women’s poor decision-making capacities and participation in community affairs, which is often driven by discriminatory social and cultural norms, are still challenges to be overcome. FWEUs are still heavily male dominated and women have limited access to resources for their own livelihoods within the community. Yet women's experiences in water and water governance and in community-based climate change adaptation activities remain limited compared with those of men. Therefore it is important that the project strengthen the capacity of women to participate effectively in decision-making processes, particularly towards gender equality; This will improve strengthening water governance by addressing its social and gender dimensions to ensure that water resources and irrigation systems are managed in a transparent, participatory and equitable manner.

Women’s participation should be ensured at all levels of planning, implementation and decision making in water management and development, early warning system and disaster risk reduction activities;

Overall project activities should require women participation to enable the household to benefit from project activities. This aims to ensure family and husband consistently support the women to participate in project activities. It is important that project staff work with local authorities and other stakeholders to ensure groups or larger communities should require at least 30% of women participation for the planning meeting to proceed. If this target is compromised, there is no inputs for later meetings that have a higher level of women participation.

Thus, if less than 40% women participation in all project planning and implementation will be compromised. Long-term sustainability of project participation will change in the gender norms and behaviours.


### Priority 4 — Promote Collective Farming among Smallholder Farmers

Contrasting farming is a model where farmers and private actors (e.g collector, agri-businessmen) work together in the long run - through legally bonded relationships, for mutual economic benefits. When farmers change to new farming practices, it is important that they work closely with each other to ensure the crops (despite individual ownership of land) are sold at a fair price and in a timely manner.

In contrast, farming should be facilitated through Local Farmers' Cooperatives or a local agri-business. It is important to ensure that women producers are actively involved in those institutions and agri-business initiatives.


---
**Page 201**
---

# Anticipated Trends During Project Life and Beyond

The project interventions are anticipated to give rise to the following trends. These trends would become clear when construction of irrigation sub-projects are completed and put back into operation.

* **Increased labor market:** is anticipated to meet increasing demand of intensified rice production, particularly in the dry season (e.g., increased use of herbicides and pesticide application) [more jobs] hired labor), created including jobs for women such as crop care.
* **Increased mechanization,** particularly at land preparation and harvesting stages. thanks to increased productivity and profitability of rice farming. This trend is also driven from farm workers jobs such as harvesting, weeding.
* **Opportunities in niche market segments that bring farmers premium (thanks to selection of rice variety and production technologies that target high-end markets), particularly for farmers with large farm size (e.g., more than 4ha) [increasing opportunities for women to participate in trading and value chain].
* **Increased income** (double or more) thanks to an increased number of crops per year and [increased crop yields] thanks to the adoption of improved rice varieties, integrated crop management technologies (e.g. IPM, integrated crop management, alternative wetting dry.) [decreasing decision making for women, particularly in nutrition and education for children].
* **Increased income** from selling produce at a premium price through farming and sell to better-off, particularly when water access becomes more reliable thanks to project investment.
* **Increased social networking:** (e.g., collective farming) is not promoted successfully
* **Reduced labor exchange:** because of a) more synchronized irrigation and b.) increased labor market [de] reduced social networking at farm neighborhood level.
* **Reduced selling prices** due to increased farm output for the same market.
